Brainstorm board · session 12

Realism additions to players, teams, tactics, and the match engine

Updated 2026-09-26 · 45 areas · 49 live threads · 12 pieces of work, all stale

Session 11: the realism test and its speed

The story of session 11

We set out to finish the realism test: how one change is judged, checks beyond averages, tuning without gaming the bands, and how fast the harness runs. We learned that a sound engine fails many bands by chance unless one test covers all bands together, and that an older outside report agreed with our direction and found four real defects in today's harness code. You chose a yardstick model whose team strengths change through a season, fitted on five seasons of real results with a long history for the slow parts. A band passes when its whole uncertainty range sits inside a margin worked out per band, so a sound engine passes 97 percent of change runs. A change run plays at least 12,000 matches within ten minutes and gives each band a verdict against the old engine. Every tactic setting is tested, tuning fits measured values first and searches the rest on the full engine, and result-identical speed-ups go first behind a replay check. You cut the in-match forks, so every test plays full matches. Still open: whether a release run fits in time, and the real state of the reference machine. The next sitting starts there, or wherever you choose.

What we believe now

Session 11 settled the realism test and how fast it runs. The yardstick is a model fitted on real results: each team has attack and defence numbers that change through a season, with home advantage and a small adjustment per league, a draw correction, five seasons of football-data.co.uk, and a long history for the slow parts. It judges results, the table, and each team's counts per gap in team strength. A band passes only when its whole uncertainty range, the real value's uncertainty included, sits inside a margin worked out per band; one simultaneous test over all bands keeps a sound engine passing 97 percent of change runs, and rare bands use exact bounds and a minimum exposure. A change run plays at least 12,000 matches within ten minutes against a stated intent, over many generated leagues, and gives every band a verdict against the old engine: better, worse, equivalent, or not sure. Every tactic setting is tested through covering arrays of both teams, only the array cells stop early, and an exploit search runs before release. Tuning fits each value with its own data bottom-up, then searches the rest on the full engine with a loss that is zero inside a band and hard rules as limits; the emulator only screens and triages, inside a recorded domain, and must repay its cost. For speed, result-identical code speed-ups go first behind a replay check, then a harness that keeps a compact row per match, resumes by fixture key, and checks every match with a running checker. An older outside report agreed with this direction and found four real defects in today's harness code. The coherence pass then cut the in-match forks from session 7, so every test plays full matches, and kept early stopping to the tactics-array cells.

What you decided this session

  • The fitted model gives each team an attack and a defence number plus home advantage, with the Dixon-Coles low-score correction; a full hierarchical model is not used.
  • Real and generated teams get their strength the same way: the same results model is fitted to a real season and to an engine season, and results are compared per gap in fitted strength; the engine's fitted strengths must also spread like the real ones. Accepted risk: Strength is read from the results under test, so an engine that makes results too random also shrinks its own strength gaps; only the spread check catches that.
  • The fitted model stays at whole-match level; goals by quarter of an hour, the effect of the score, and the effect of a red card are judged by measured ranges.
  • The results model is fitted on five seasons from football-data.co.uk. Accepted risk: Its commercial terms are unclear, the same risk accepted for StatsBomb and Transfermarkt; the game ships none of the data, but a complaint means refitting on another source.
  • The engine's results per strength gap must match real football, with no design target; the amplification of player quality must show in player numbers, not in how often favourites win.
  • One results model is fitted across all leagues, with a small adjustment per league for home advantage and scoring.
  • Results per strength gap stay real, but the spread of fitted team strengths may be wider than in real football, with a real target and a design target on the strength-spread check, so amplified player quality can make the best and worst teams sit further apart. Accepted risk: Leagues become slightly more predictable than real ones: the title and relegation are decided by wider gaps.
  • The yardstick model lets each team's strength change through a season, and the check tracks the engine's teams the same way.
  • The parts that change slowly, such as the draw correction and the spread of goals, are fitted on a long history weighted towards recent seasons; the five recent seasons fit the parts that moved, such as home advantage.
  • Strength comes from goals, not from shots on target, and home advantage is per league, not per club.
  • The fitted model covers results and every team count in the football-data.co.uk files: shots, shots on target, corners, fouls, and cards, each with its own team strengths and real spread, compared per strength gap. Accepted risk: Five more fitted parts to build, check, and explain, and the joint links between them still need the copula work.
  • All four gaps in what the test covers matter: team match counts, player statistics, how play unfolds, and season and career figures.
  • The first version of the test closes three coverage gaps together: team match counts, player statistics per player and position, and how play unfolds. Accepted risk: Player statistics and the flow of play rest on a few hundred free event and tracking matches, mostly from tournaments and a few clubs, so their targets are thin and may not describe an ordinary league season.
  • Season and career figures wait longest; until they arrive, people watching seasons at the milestones cover them. Accepted risk: Drift over long saves goes unmeasured until the season figures arrive.
  • A change run compares the new engine with the old engine on paired seeds, to show what the change moved even inside a band, and with the real target, which decides pass or fail.
  • Every change run plays the same fixed number of paired matches; milestone and release runs keep early stopping. Accepted risk: Clear changes spend the whole budget, and subtle changes come back not sure yet more often, so the developer decides more calls by hand.
  • The agreement between paired matches is measured now on today's engine with the flag comparison, and again after the random streams split, before the change-run count is fixed.
  • Before a change run, the developer states which bands the change should move and in which direction; the report flags every other band that moved and every expected band that did not move or moved the wrong way.
  • The sensitivity family becomes a library of stated rules checked at several levels of a setting, and each change runs the rules for the settings it touches. Accepted risk: The rules share the change budget, so fewer matches remain for the bands.
  • An unexpected move that stays inside the real margin does not block; the developer writes one line in the stored report saying whether it is a real side effect or a bug before the change goes in.
  • A surprise the developer explains as a real side effect becomes a new sensitivity rule (for example, harder pressing raises fouls), so the next change expects it.
  • When a change pushes a band out of its margin, the developer fixes or reverts the change, and does not move another setting only to push that band back, because that chases the band instead of the cause.
  • A sound engine must pass at least 97 percent of change runs, so each band's margin and match count are sized for the band to pass a sound engine about 99.99 percent of the time.
  • Rare-event bands, such as red cards, penalties, and own goals, are judged in every change run with a wider margin that catches only large errors, and judged again with the normal margin in milestone and release runs. Accepted risk: A small error in a rare band passes change runs and shows only at the next milestone.
  • Each band's margin is worked out on its own: the widest margin that still catches the smallest error a player would notice, checked against the 97 percent target, with a stated reason per band. Accepted risk: More work up front: every band needs a stated noticeable error and a reason.
  • A band with a design target passes around that target and must also stay within a stated distance of the real value.
  • Each band's noticeable error starts from how much real leagues differ on that statistic, and watched reviews tighten a band later.
  • The first version of the new harness uses the full pass rule from the start: an equivalence test, a margin per band, and the 97 percent target; fixed ranges are not kept. Accepted risk: The first version of the harness arrives later.
  • Every band lives in one registry with its target, margin, reason, and data source; the harness, the tests, and the module cards point to bands by name.
  • The slow tests keep their scenarios but take their limits from the registry and use the new pass rule, as part of the change run.
  • Every band is shown over versions, and a shift-detection method flags a drift to a person, who traces it to the changes; nothing blocks automatically.
  • A module swapped in configuration is a change like any other: a full change run on every band with a stated intent.
  • The yardstick compares results and goals for each gap in team strength, so real and generated teams both carry a strength rating; this fits the three-layer league bands, the fast-model check per pairing, and the fitted results model. Accepted risk: A strength rating is needed for every real team in the five seasons and for every generated team.
  • A band passes by an equivalence test: the whole confidence interval of the engine statistic must sit inside the band margin, so a small or noisy run cannot pass by luck and may return not sure yet.
  • Every band can block a change; there are no watch-only bands. Accepted risk: Under a strict family rule each band needs about 2.4 times the matches of a single test at 300 bands, so the five-minute budget cannot hold every band in one change run.
  • When a band comes back not sure yet, the developer sees the interval and the margin and decides whether the change goes ahead. Accepted risk: The call depends on judgement under time pressure, and a hurried yes can let a bad change sit until the overnight run.
  • A change verdict on every band arrives within ten minutes; a change run plays at least 12,000 matches in total (6,000 pairs), and more when they fit. Accepted risk: At today's speed the floor takes about 16 minutes; the harness needs about 1.6 times today's speed to meet the floor and about 12 times to fill ten minutes with about 90,000 matches.
  • Test every setting through a covering array: a set of team tactics in which every pair of setting values appears at least once (about 100 to 150 tactics today), each played against a fixed field, and grow it to three-way pairs before release.
  • Each tested setting must pass four things: the match finishes with valid events, the realism bands stay in range for a team using it, the setting moves what it says it moves (its sensitivity rule), and it wins no more than its fit and squad allow.
  • Beside the covering array, a search looks for the worst case: an optimiser that tries to find the setting with the highest points against the field, so an exploit is hunted rather than hoped absent.
  • The pairwise covering array of tactics runs at every milestone, a small slice of it runs in each change run, and the three-way array and the exploit search run before each release. Accepted risk: A fault that needs three settings together shows only before release.
  • The covering array covers both teams' settings together, so every pair of setting values across the two teams meets at least once.
  • Each tested tactic plays with a few squad types, for example fast, strong, and technical. Accepted risk: It about triples the matches.
  • Every tactic setting joins the covering array: formation, mentality, instructions, both shapes, about 40 roles with duties, and player and opposition instructions.
  • The tactics arrays run as decided, with no cut: the full pairwise array at milestones and the full three-way array before release, possibly on a small low-cost Proxmox cluster plus a VPS. Amended in session 11: the release's three-way array uses a predictor to choose which cells the engine plays, with every uncertain and near-edge cell and a random sample replayed. Accepted risk: The three-way array takes about 71 hours even at twelve times today's speed, so a release run lasts days, not one night, unless more machines are added.
  • The test compares whole distributions as shapes, not only averages: pass counts, possession chain lengths, shot distances, and similar counts.
  • The test scores movement against tracking data as the Waymo challenge does: how likely real speeds, accelerations, distances to others, and line heights are under the engine, summed into one score per version.
  • Blind clip reviews and a real-squad back-test are not part of the test; the watched reviews at milestones stay.
  • The movement score starts on the free tracking matches (Metrica, seven German league matches, twenty A-League broadcast matches), and licensed league tracking is bought before release. Accepted risk: Licensed tracking costs money, and the movement targets change when it arrives.
  • Each whole distribution is compared at a few fixed percentiles, such as the 10th, 50th, and 90th, each a band in the registry under the same pass rule. Accepted risk: A shape that is wrong between the chosen points can pass.
  • The round-robin and the tactics arrays rate each tactic against the whole field with Nash averaging, beside the clear-weakness rule, and flag a tactic whose field rating is far above its win rate.
  • Fit every value that has its own data bottom-up to that data; let the optimiser and the emulator search only the values with no data of their own, against a training set of bands, and judge every result on held-out bands that no search may read. Accepted risk: Some bands must be set aside and never read by any search.
  • Each tuning run holds out a fresh set of bands that its search may not read. Accepted risk: Over many runs the searches see every band, so the hold-out weakens as a test of whether tuning found football.
  • The optimiser searches the values with no data of their own directly on the full engine, as chess engines tune; the emulator does not propose settings. Accepted risk: Every trial costs real matches, so a search takes days and competes with the tactics arrays for the cluster.
  • The engine emulator keeps two roles, a sensitivity screen and milestone triage, and no longer proposes settings.
  • Feedback comes in four steps: seconds for smoke matches and replays, ten minutes for a change run, a milestone run, and a release run that may take days.
  • Beside the ten-minute change run, a larger realistic evaluation of the engine (for example every band plus a slice of the tactics array) finishes within about 15 minutes; how much slower realism may make a match, and how many machines are realistic, is not yet known.
  • Speed is explored in four families beside more machines: faster engine code, fewer matches for the same certainty, many matches in lockstep on the processor, and the setup.
  • No GPU: the engine and the harness stay on the processor and machines, one engine and one truth.
  • Faster engine code comes first among the speed families, and the setup is dropped first if time runs short.
  • A full-trace replay check on fixed seeds becomes the gate for every speed change, and the four result-identical distance-check speed-ups go in first, before any realism work.
  • The realism work runs on a staggered cadence schedule with random streams split per subsystem, under one total limit for the whole step of about 1.9 microseconds that features share, rather than a budget per feature. Accepted risk: One costly feature can use up the room the later features need.
  • The harness uses three noise-reduction methods: summed logged probabilities for cards, penalties, and injuries; the shot's on-target chance computed at the strike with short side-runs for its goal chance; and a correction from an in-play results model for results, scorelines, and points. Accepted risk: The engine must log every chance draw and become cloneable or forkable from snapshots.
  • The first-goal fork for the goalless share and sequential stopping are not taken for now. Amended: tactics-array cells do stop early. Amended again: the cells use the valid stopping rule, and no other run stops early.
  • The six platform maths calls move to a pure-Rust maths library, the script's 2 ms timer becomes a watchdog that marks a match invalid, and a cross-machine replay check is added now, with the speed-ups.
  • The harness runs in one process with a pull queue and no barriers, keeps running totals instead of per-match buffers and files, checks a sample of matches (about 1 in 16 plus every outlier), writes event files only for kept outliers, and caches the old engine's results by build, content, fixtures, and seed. Amended in session 11: each match also writes a compact row of measures, so runs keep per-match values for the bootstrap and for judging again. Amended again in session 11: a running rule checker sees every match, and the sampled 1 in 16 gets full recording with events. Accepted risk: A rare invalid state can pass in a match that is not checked.
  • The test plays full matches only: no short segments branched from saved states and no scenario bank. Accepted risk: Per-event bands cost more matches than segments would.
  • The tactics arrays stay in full, with no predictive model choosing which cells to play. Accepted risk: About 960,000 matches per milestone and a release run of days.
  • How closely the fast result model tracks the engine per match is measured, and the fast model is used to cut full matches only where the tracking is strong.
  • The speed-ups and the harness work run first on the owned 9800X3D, and hardware is decided later from measured speed.
  • Use the predictive model only for the release's three-way tactics array, keep the milestone pairwise array in full, and let the engine replay every uncertain cell, every cell near a band edge, and a random sample. Accepted risk: A broken three-way combination in a cell the predictor rates as safe and the random sample misses can reach a release.
  • Stop each tactics-array cell as soon as its bands are clearly inside or clearly outside, since cells are independent: a sound cell stops at about a third of its matches.
  • Shrink the tactics array by pairing roles only where they interact (within a unit and with neighbouring units) and by mirroring left and right slots, instead of pairing every role with every other.
  • Give each tactics cell its own check depth: one match for validity, a few for the sensitivity rules, and the noise methods for its bands, instead of about 200 full matches each.
  • Test related bands together, such as the percentiles of one distribution or a player statistic by position, as one joint equivalence test, so fewer independent bands share the 97 percent target.
  • Compare the curve of results against strength gap by its few fitted parameters instead of a separate band per gap, so the per-gap comparison costs about the same as one band.
  • Let the optimiser use the same noise methods and shared seeds across candidates, and a two-evaluation step method such as SPSA, so each full-engine search step costs far fewer matches. Accepted risk: Accepted only if accuracy holds: the noise methods keep the same expected value, and every tuned result is still confirmed by a full evaluation.
  • Speed and accuracy both matter: a speed method is taken only when it keeps the answer unbiased.
  • Test all bands together with one simultaneous test (a max-t bootstrap over paired matches) that sets one threshold from how the bands actually move together, so correlated bands need less certainty each while every band is still reported on its own.
  • There is no scenario bank for rare events; corners, free kicks, and penalties are measured from full matches. Accepted risk: Rare per-event bands may need more full matches than the result bands.
  • Each change run checks every touched sensitivity rule at every level of its setting. Accepted risk: More of the ten-minute budget goes to rules and less to the bands.
  • Each fixture's key is a hash of what the fixture is: scenario version, world seed, the two clubs, home and away, both formations, and a repeat number; the engine seed is stored beside it, a run grows by adding repeats, and the old-engine cache joins on the key.
  • Each match writes one compact row of unrounded measures (counts, numerators, and denominators), keyed by fixture, into a column file per worker, with no events and no commentary. Accepted risk: A 12,000-match run keeps a few megabytes of rows beside the running totals.
  • Each run records its full identity (program build with a hash of uncommitted changes, content, flags, random-number scheme, measure definitions, and registry version); runs are small work units with a ledger that resumes a stopped run without doubled matches; a changed band or margin is judged again from stored rows, and a changed program always plays new matches.
  • Each harness step proves itself with tests before the next step starts: a grown run keeps its earlier matches, a resumed run equals an uninterrupted run, the estimators pass hand-checked cases (zero events, a missing denominator), and every observation mode gives the same result digest; each run reports time and memory per stage (play, checks, commentary, writing, disk, totals).
  • The equivalence test adds the real value's own uncertainty to the engine's; each registry entry records the band's definition version, population, estimator, resampling unit, and minimum exposure, and marks its margin as a design choice.
  • Rare bands use exact count bounds, so zero events give an upper bound of about three divided by the match count; a rare band cannot pass before its minimum exposure; the error of a mean uses the sample spread and is never zero; the summed-probability method still applies where it exists.
  • Early stopping, used only in tactics-array cells, uses error spending over a fixed number of looks; development, promotion, and release each have their own seed set, the release set is never seen by tuning, and a tuning winner is confirmed on fresh seeds with a fixed match count.
  • Each run spreads its matches over many independently generated leagues, the bootstrap resamples whole leagues as the outer unit, and season claims use independent saves.
  • Against the old engine, each band gets one of four verdicts: better, worse, equivalent, or not sure, judged against the band's noticeable error; for a band with a range, better means closer to the real value; the old flag verdict is replaced, and a result that is not significant is never reported as equivalent.
  • Each emulator carries a record of its engine version, random-number scheme, training runs, settings and ranges, supported contexts, and validation results; it gives no answer outside those bounds, and a new mechanism or random-number scheme makes it advisory until bridge runs check it.
  • The emulator is used only when it beats a simple regression and a random search on held-out groups (whole settings, generated leagues, and seed groups), and its training matches are tracked against the matches it saves; when it stops paying back, it is dropped.
  • About 15 percent of each milestone run goes to cases the emulator did not flag, plus a random check of settings it ranked as unimportant; triage adds evidence and never removes a correctness check. Accepted risk: The sentinels take about 15 percent of the milestone budget.
  • Tuning loss is zero inside each band's margin and grows with the distance outside it, summed per family with declared family weights; hard rules and must-not-regress bands are limits, so a candidate that breaks one is rejected; a band with a real preferred centre, such as a design target, keeps a small pull towards it; when no candidate wins, several trade-off candidates are kept.
  • A running rule checker sees every tick of every match and keeps only small state, with no tick list; it must report exactly the same violations as today's checker on a fixed test set before it replaces that checker; full recording with events runs only for the sampled 1 in 16 and every outlier. Accepted risk: The running checker adds work to every tick; if its cost is too high, it is measured and decided again.
  • In-match tools and match preparation are judged from full matches only: game-state bands over natural matches with the tool or the preparation on and off, on paired seeds; the forks at a set state are cut. Accepted risk: The effect of one change at one moment is much noisier than in a fork, so these checks need more matches or return not sure more often.
  • Only tactics-array cells stop early, under the valid stopping rule; change runs, milestone runs, and release runs play their full count. Accepted risk: Milestone and release runs spend more machine time on results that were already clear.

Old documents now out of date

The engine realism document judges bands by fixed ranges and sizes the budget for 1,000 matches; the season layer document uses fixed ranges, one Wyscout season, and the real strength-spread share. The old harness document is replaced by a new piece of work at done.

Session 10: the realism test, the statistical model, and fast iteration

You asked to talk through the test harness, the bands, and the tests across all of realism, the statistical model behind them, and how the team iterates fast, with the new engine architecture in view. Six researchers read the code, the three design documents, and the published work on football statistics, simulator testing, fast tuning, and how other sports games test realism. This section is the briefing. The questions in the conversation start from it.

What you decided this session

  • The realism test must answer all four failures: bands that fight each other under hand tuning, a slow verdict, noise that the pass rule cannot see, and play that looks wrong while the averages pass.
  • A developer learns within about five minutes whether a change made realism better or worse on the bands it touches. Accepted risk: The budget holds about 3,800 matches at today speed; if the realism work halves the engine speed, it holds about 1,900, and the per-change check must shrink or stop earlier.
  • The talk goes into the statistical model and the pass rule first, as the foundation for the change check, tuning, and the checks beyond averages.
  • Of the four failures, invisible noise can wait longest: the first version of the new harness may keep today fixed ranges while the other failures are answered. Accepted risk: Every other tool reads the pass rule, so until the noise fix lands the change check and the tuning can chase noise, and a band can drift inside its fixed range unseen.
  • The yardstick is split: a statistical model fitted on the five real seasons judges goals, scorelines, results, and the table, and measured ranges judge passes, fouls, cards, running, and the other statistics. Accepted risk: Two kinds of band to build and explain, and an error in the fitted model becomes an error in the test.
  • The yardstick compares results and goals for each gap in team strength, so real and generated teams both carry a strength rating; this fits the three-layer league bands, the fast-model check per pairing, and the fitted results model. Accepted risk: A strength rating is needed for every real team in the five seasons and for every generated team.
  • A band passes by an equivalence test: the whole confidence interval of the engine statistic must sit inside the band margin, so a small or noisy run cannot pass by luck and may return not sure yet.
  • Every band can block a change; there are no watch-only bands. Accepted risk: Under a strict family rule each band needs about 2.4 times the matches of a single test at 300 bands, so the five-minute budget cannot hold every band in one change run.
  • Every change run judges every band, and the wait for a verdict grows to about an hour. Accepted risk: Nothing slips past a change, but the loop is slow, and an hour holds about 45,000 matches at today speed.
  • Each band margin is a fixed share of how much real leagues and seasons differ on that statistic, for example half, set in advance and never after a run.
  • When a band comes back not sure yet, the developer sees the interval and the margin and decides whether the change goes ahead. Accepted risk: The call depends on judgement under time pressure, and a hurried yes can let a bad change sit until the overnight run.
  • Both budgets hold: a verdict on every band within about five minutes, which makes the harness fast enough through more cores, a lighter measuring build, and cached baselines, a performance project of its own. Accepted risk: This needs about 12 times today throughput before the realism work slows each match, and until that project lands neither budget is met.

Next time

The check-in on the model and the pass rule is waiting for your answer. Still to open: judging one change, realism beyond averages, and fast tuning.

Where the test stands today

  • The harness is real and reproducible. It plays generated leagues on all cores, and each match seed is a fixed function of the run seed, so a run repeats exactly.
  • Sixteen bands pass when a point estimate falls inside a fixed range, for example goals per match from 2.4 to 3.2. The harness computes the sampling error of every band but uses it only to label a change as noise. The error decides no pass.
  • A match costs about 448 ms on one core. On 8 cores the harness plays about 12.6 matches a second: a full 1,000-match run of every suite takes about 81 minutes, and one targeted pairing takes about 87 seconds.
  • Tuning is by hand. The last sweep tried 181 settings at 200 matches each, and no setting passed every band. A tempo setting that fixed passes and ball-in-play time broke the discipline bands and the stronger-team check, so it was held back.
  • Thirteen slow tests hold their own limits on 200 fixed seeds, apart from the bands file. There are two sources of truth.
  • The flag comparison already plays both arms on the same seeds, but it compares the arms' totals separately, so it loses the benefit of the pairing. Each match uses one random stream, so one added draw changes every draw after it.
  • No optimiser, emulator, or sensitivity tool exists in the code yet.

What goes wrong, in four sentences

  • Bands fight each other. Fixing one band by hand breaks another, because settings act together and a person moves one at a time.
  • A verdict is slow. A full answer takes over an hour, and a release run at today's speed takes about 33 hours, not one night.
  • Noise is invisible. At 1,000 matches the error on mean goals is about 0.053, so a quick run cannot see a change below about 0.1 goals, and a fixed range of 2.4 to 3.2 hides any drift inside it.
  • Averages can be right while play is wrong. Football Manager shows this in public: corner and narrow-formation exploits and unreal crossing, with season figures that look fine.

The statistical model of real football

The board has measured targets and one rule: goals are Poisson once the scoring rate is known. It has no single model of real football that gives every statistic its expected spread. The literature offers a stack, from simple to rich:

  1. Poisson goals per team from attack, defence, and home advantage. This is the base, and your data already fit it closely.
  2. A low-score correction. Independent Poisson predicts about 10 percent too few draws; the Dixon-Coles correction (rho about -0.13) or a shared term moves weight to 0-0 and 1-1.
  3. A hierarchical team model fitted on the five real seasons. It gives a predicted spread for any statistic, so the engine is compared with that spread, not a range set by hand. It also splits a table's spread into skill and luck: a 2025 study puts luck at about 60 percent of a season's over- or under-performance.
  4. Over-dispersed counts for shots, crosses, and fouls, which vary more than Poisson (your shots at 1.64 times the mean).
  5. A scoring rate that moves in the match. The rate rises through the match and follows the score and red cards (an away red card lifts home scoring odds by about 69 percent).
  6. Joint links between statistics (copulas), which are the published form of a joint band.

The engine does not use this model to play. The model is the yardstick: it says what spread real football would show for a league like ours, so a band has a principled width.

How one band should pass, and how hundreds pass together

  • Test for sameness, not for difference. Failing to find a difference proves nothing when the sample is small. An equivalence test passes only when the whole confidence interval sits inside a margin set in advance, for example a fraction of how much real leagues differ from each other.
  • Hundreds of bands raise false alarms on every run. The standard answer is a few dozen gate bands under a strict family rule, which can block a change, and the rest as watch bands under a false-discovery rule, shown to a person.
  • One score per band. History matching, used for galaxy, climate, and epidemic models, divides the distance from the real value by the combined uncertainty: sampling noise, data error, the emulator's error, and an allowance for where the game departs on purpose. A score above about 3 fails.

How one change should be judged

  • The chess model. The Stockfish test farm judges every engine change with paired games, a sequential test with fixed bounds, and a stop as soon as the answer is clear. Here that means paired seeds per match and a sequential test on each gate band.
  • What pairing buys. Catching a 5 percent change in goals needs about 3,300 matches per arm without pairing, and about 700 to 1,300 pairs with it, if paired matches correlate at 0.6 to 0.8. That correlation is not measured on this engine. The flag comparison can measure it today.
  • Pairing needs the stream split. Paired seeds only cancel noise while both engines draw the same numbers for the same events. The planned split into one stream per module, player, and kind of action is a precondition of the whole change check.
  • The sensitivity family becomes a library of stated relations, for example "raise finishing, goals must not fall; unrelated bands stay the same", each checked across several levels instead of one before-and-after pair.

Realism beyond averages

  • Whole distributions: the scoreline table, pass counts, and chain lengths compared as shapes, not only as means.
  • A movement realism score from tracking data, as the Waymo self-driving challenge scores its simulators: how likely real speeds, accelerations, distances to others, pressing distances, and line heights are under the engine's distribution, summed into one score per version.
  • Blind clips: real and simulated clips in the same 2D view, several raters, and a pass when they cannot tell them apart (about 50 percent correct).
  • Nash averaging for the round-robin: it rates each style against the whole field and finds a dominant style that a plain win rate or Elo hides.
  • A back-test like Out of the Park Baseball's: simulate seasons from real squads and compare with what happened.

Fast iteration

TierWhat runsAt today's speed
SecondsDeterminism replays, a few short smoke matches, crash and sanity checks, a cached baseline.Under 10 seconds
MinutesThe emulator predicts every band; a small paired batch on the touched gate bands stops early.300 paired matches: about 50 seconds
About an hourAll gate bands with the sequential test, the touched sensitivity relations, and the touched round-robin pairings.About 45,000 matches
OvernightEvery band and family, fresh seeds as a second check, the history dashboard, and the next emulator wave.About 540,000 matches in 12 hours: a third of the planned 1.5 million
  • Tune many settings at once. Screen which settings matter first (Morris screening: about 510 runs for 50 settings). Then run an optimiser (CMA-ES, or SPSA as chess engines use) against the emulator, where a trial costs milliseconds, and confirm its best proposals on the full engine. Use the worst band's distance as the loss, so no band hides behind the others.
  • A fidelity ladder: the fast model, then shortened matches, then the full engine, so most search runs are cheap.
  • Never play the same baseline twice: store each run under a hash of engine version, content, and seeds.
  • Guard against gaming the bands: keep a set of bands that no tuning run may read, and judge every tuned setting on them.

Where it meets the new architecture

  • The staged refactor splits the random streams and freezes a new baseline first. That is exactly what paired change checks need, so the harness work rides on it.
  • Each module's card names its calibration source and band. With one band registry, the card, the harness, and the slow tests read the same band by name.
  • The off-switch run and the swap test are already change checks: each switch or swap is a paired run against the baseline.
  • The quick engine emits the same events, so every band can run on it too, and the fast-model equality check compares the two with the same pass rule.

Open tensions

  • The rule that every band is an output and never a tuning target pulls against the emulator's role as a tuning tool.
  • The design documents state bands as fixed real ranges; the board set tolerance as the real spread plus sampling noise.
  • A release run of about 1.5 million matches takes about 33 hours at today's speed on 8 cores, before the realism work slows each match.

Main sources: the harness code (content/realism-bands.json, crates/engine-cli/src/report/), the engine benchmark, Fishtest mathematics (github.com/official-stockfish/fishtest/wiki), Dixon and Coles 1997, Baio and Blangiardo 2010, Vernon, Goldstein and Bower on history matching (arxiv.org/pdf/1405.4976), the Waymo Open Sim Agents Challenge, Balduzzi and colleagues on Nash averaging (arxiv.org/pdf/1806.02643), Loeppky, Sacks and Welch on design size, and Morris screening. Every finding with its source is in the board document.

What we believe now

You started from one thought. The in-progress plans for Touchline build a lawful, tactical, calibrated match, and you wanted to know what they still miss on realism. Six sessions later, the answer is a whole product. Touchline is a football-management game, and the match engine is its first component. The engine workflow keeps its charter and its sixteen slices. The realism work runs as a separate programme after it, measured against the shipped engine with a performance budget of its own. Your twelfth brief, on traits, personality, and chemistry, is now covered in full: a trait is a habit of play with a measured rate, thirteen personality attributes move cards, setbacks, leadership, and professionalism, and chemistry is capped so a top player still stands out. The top risk is stacked effects swamping ability. Your thirteenth brief, on psychological factors, is covered too: momentum lifts and dips and fades, pressure grows with the stakes, players react to the score within the manager's mentality, and comebacks come from real mechanisms. Your fourteenth brief, on rules, officiating, and formats, is covered as well: the complete Laws with each league's differences, graded fouls, referee profiles, a video referee by competition, and a rule pack per competition. Your fifteenth brief, on goalkeepers, is covered: the nine keeper attributes stay, style comes from role, traits, and attributes, and keepers are amplified so quality shows. Your sixteenth brief, on the environment, is covered: weather and pitch are the core, and home advantage scales with the crowd inside its band. Your seventeenth brief became its own work group, placed last: expected threat and possession value in players' choices, kept only if a comparison test shows them closer to real. Your eighteenth brief, on technical robustness, is covered: exact replays everywhere, matchday and season limits, versioned saves and replays, and developer tracing. Your nineteenth brief, on engine architecture, is covered: every part becomes a module behind a typed interface, and one refactor to that contract now comes first.

What we believe now, area by area:

  • The player. A player becomes a person. The single position code becomes a competence rating per position, and each foot gets its own rating. Players gain new performance attributes, a personality with hidden traits, and habits that training can change. Under pressure or low mood, a strong habit can override an instruction, and the manager can see why.
  • The club. On the pitch, a club's style comes from its squad and its tactic. Club identity acts off the pitch: it shapes who the club hires and buys, and what the board and fans expect, blended with the owner type. A club also has a home ground, a reputation, and a team mood that personality shapes.
  • Tactics. Each team has an in-possession shape and an out-of-possession shape, set-piece routines, and live changes that wait for the next stoppage. Both managers can make those changes.
  • The match. The engine resolves every kind of action from the situation first and the player second. It uses the height the ball already has for play in the air. It has a full referee with a video assistant, and injuries have a severity. The player's own match plays in real time or as key moments, and the other matches of the day run beside it.
  • The realism test. The test measures against real match data, and its bands are far tighter than the plan's first guess. Where the game departs from real data on purpose, the band has a real target and a design target. People watch matches and seasons at milestones to review them. The test keeps its StatsBomb and Transfermarkt sources: you accept the licence risk, because the game ships none of the data. Cups get one band of their own, the upset rate by the gap in divisions.
  • The season. The season layer mirrors the real European structure. It adds careers with age curves, a market that values players from visible data only, and a club business with a board and an owner who can sack the manager. The fast model that runs the other leagues is set out under engine structure. A new save may take a few minutes to create. Money is kept in euros inside the game, and the player chooses the currency on screen.
  • The staff. Staff are people who also carry a rating, and each club sees that rating through its own judgment. Every role a real club employs is in the game, from coaches and scouts to the groundsman, the psychologist, and the loan manager. Each one has attributes, a personality, specialisms, a playing idea, a contract, and a market. Numbers and quality differ by league and tier. Staff are full people only in the active leagues, and the player chooses per department whether to hire directly or to delegate. Staff speak through reports, a word during the match, and meetings. Their advice can be wrong through noise, a personal leaning, an agenda, or a blind spot. Overruling them has a cost, and the game remembers who was right. Staff follow a manager through an inner circle and their own loyalty, other clubs can poach them for a fee, and they have careers: retired players become coaches, and coaches become managers. Staff know some places better than others, form bonds with players, tire under too much work, and need facilities the club pays for. On the pitch, staff decide how fast the squad learns set-piece routines and tactics, how the team fades late in a match, and how well an opposition plan lands. Session 8 settled their attributes: staff and managers share one list of 32 attributes, and each role reads a weighted part of it, so suitability for any role is computed; staff also carry the players' twelve traits, five traits of their own, three hidden values led by consistency, a languages list, and up to ten leaning tags.
  • Contracts and the dressing room. A contract is negotiated between the agent, the director of football, the manager, and the player's family, and the player weighs the role, the project, and life off the pitch as well as money. Every agent is a person, but only a few large agencies hold real power. A contract carries money, bonuses, clauses, and promises; most promises are verbal and move trust, and a few are written. The years left on a contract lower the price, and a release clause caps it. The dressing room has standing, groups, a culture, an icon, and a view of the manager. Unrest spreads through leaders and groups, escalates from a private word to a refusal to play, and has a small effect on the pitch. The manager disciplines within each country's real rules, with warnings, fines, dropping, training apart, and public pressure, and strong standing at the club stretches those limits. A manager who crosses the line faces the board and a tribunal that can award anything up to a free exit.
  • Supporters and the press. Supporters are groups with their own expectations, and the club's fan mood is the balance between them. Fans react to the club's identity, and they hold power through the board, real ownership models, money, and the manager directly. The crowd moves the referee, nervous players, and the home team's late belief. Rivalries come from the world data, geography, and events in the save. The manager attends every press conference, can delegate one, and picks the players where a competition requires them, as UEFA does. Stories build over weeks from real events and invented rumours. Journalists are people, and the manager can give exclusives, leak, freeze someone out, and learn who is reliable. How media pressure reaches the manager's job depends on the owner.
  • Where new players come from. Academies follow each country's real structure, and the full player model starts at the scholarship age, about 16; younger players form a rough pool that clubs learn about slowly through staff, time, tests, and other clubs' interest. A young player's future is a range that narrows, with no fixed ceiling. Clubs over-rate early-born players, as real academies do, and good staff can correct it. Players also arrive as late bloomers, through the lower leagues, and from beyond UEFA. The generator copies measured patterns: birth quarters, late maturers, growth over decades, each nation's output, and dual eligibility. A player's style comes from where the player develops, not from nationality. B teams and squad registration follow real rules by country and competition. The world may drift over a long save, and the season test keeps it inside real ranges.
  • International football. The calendar, national teams, and players on duty are in the game; managing a nation is a later addition. Call-ups follow club form, the national coach's taste, loyalty, and the nation's depth. The manager can play a player into a call-up or ask for rest, players choose between nations and can retire from duty, and duty brings fatigue, injuries with compensation, mood, and changes in value and fame.
  • The manager's week. Team talks before, during, and after the match move mood and focus, and they depend on reading the room, on not repeating one approach, on words to single players, and on the manager's standing. Delegation works the same way in every area. Information arrives in a daily inbox the player can filter, periodic reports from delegated work, and urgent escalations from staff. Reasons behind events show in a mixed way. Time is limited by the structure of the week: training slots, one briefing per match, team talks, press conferences, a few one-to-one talks, a staff meeting, and trips, with no hour budget; a task done personally uses the manager's own attributes and builds bonds faster. Clubs can fall into crisis, and the realism test reaches off the pitch with bands for measured facts and design targets for the rest.
  • The data model. Every attribute keeps a named job in the engine. An action runs in stages, and each stage blends a main attribute with a few supporting ones, weighted from real data where it exists. Each overlapping pair has its own split, and pace is held back three ways. Attributes are stored in tenths behind the familiar 1-to-20 display. A convex curve makes the top of the scale count more; its strength is set so a top player looks better in about two of three runs of five matches, and the same convexity prices development and the market. The world's spread of ability is wide on purpose. One effective-ability layer moves a player within bounds through body, mind, familiarity, and surroundings, so an 18 has poor patches and hot streaks but never becomes poor or unstoppable. Consistency and injury proneness are hidden and shown in words, and personality traits use the same 1-to-20 scale. A player's grasp of each tactic is personal, and the team's familiarity is computed from the players on the pitch. About 40 roles each set where a player stands, what the player prefers, and how teammates use the player, and a duty scales the role. A sensitivity test of its own changes one attribute, setting, or role at a time and checks that only the related statistics move. Team numbers act on actions that need several players, such as pressing chains, and club identity acts only off the pitch, through hiring and through the pressure of the board and the fans. Every rating in the game, for staff, managers, referees, and agents too, uses the same scale. Height gives reach at a small cost in turning, age slows recovery, and a player new to a country adapts over months. A relationship between two people keeps on-pitch understanding apart from personal liking.
  • Action resolution. Each contact, meaning a tackle, a first touch, a keeper's handling, a strike, a pass, or an interception, draws from a chance or a spread built from the situation and the attributes, and physics carries the ball between contacts. A strike takes two draws, quality first and then error. A keeper's reach is physics and the handling a draw, and a penalty adds a guessing game. A duel runs in two steps and ends four ways. Errors come from three sources, the tails of the draws, concentration lapses, and a few named error events, and each source has a fixed share of the error band. The checks judge shots with an xG model fitted on real data, and band the shot split, pass curves, duels, Opta's errors leading to goals, and season variance with real and design targets.
  • Calibration. Targets come from five recent seasons of real data (2021-22 to 2025-26) in three layers: each league with data, each tier, and banding groups for the other leagues. A division's style emerges from its players, not from settings by hand. Each band's tolerance is the real spread widened by sampling noise. The check compares every scoreline and whole distributions, not only averages, and bands the league table in full. Every report is stored with its version and compared with the last. Each change runs about 1,000 matches. Each milestone runs the touched parts of the heavy checks, and a full release run finishes overnight on a Mac mini and a VPS.
  • Tactical counterplay. Costs, counters, and squad fit emerge from play: a high line is exposed because a slow defender loses the race, and a press tires the players who run it. There is no counter table and no fit number. Rest defence follows the duties, and the gaps between the lines emerge from where roles stand. A round-robin of eight named styles with identical squads and a grid over the settings prove that every style has a clear weakness. AI managers read the same noisy staff advice as the human, remember what worked, and carry a tactical fingerprint that analysts can reveal. Paired squads prove that fit matters. Beyond the brief, the AI learns during a match and an exploit hunt runs before each release, players manage games themselves with the manager's instructions on top, the manager can shout from the touchline, and conditions change which tactic works.
  • Engine structure. The full engine runs every match in the active leagues, and a fast model runs the rest. The fast model is refitted from the full engine after each change, and it must match the full engine both in season bands and per pairing of team strengths on a fixed batch. A written state machine names every phase's entry and exit, and a check confirms where players stand at each restart. Grounds differ in size within the laws. Added time prices each kind of event, including video reviews. A match replays identically on every supported machine. A skipped match finishes on the fast model from its current state, background matches run first and reveal their events in step with the player's match, players near the ball think on every tick while the others think at a human rate, and a mid-match save finishes on the engine version it started with.
  • Player decisions and off-ball movement. Each player works from a picture of the pitch that timed scans refresh, holding where others are heading and what team-mates call. A choice takes decision time and an action takes commit time, and pressure arriving inside either hurries or blocks it. The carrier chooses from the whole catalogue of 53 real actions plus game extras, scored as expected value with weights fitted to real choices; a poor decision-maker misjudges values and picks loosely. Without the ball, players move from an anchor through role patterns and an off-ball utility, and share space and defensive jobs by claims and calls, inside the team's marking system and counter-press window. Positional instructions are limits, choice instructions are weights followed as faithfully as the player's decisions and teamwork allow, and traits show as repeated choices and styles. Bands check shape, crowding, runs, pressure, handoffs, frozen players, and passes through crowds.
  • Output and presentation. The engine writes a log of true causes, and every figure, visualisation, commentary line, rating, and staff sentence comes from the one match record, is recomputed by a test, and traces back to its moments. Figures are always true; staff readings and advice can be wrong by staff quality, and better analysts unlock more depth. Commentary is text with sound, from AI-written, reviewed banks that know player history, the season, the match's own stories, and the causes. Ratings value every action and off-ball contribution, fairly for each role. Highlights rank by win-probability swing, moments the manager can act on, and story. A 180-item catalogue of statistics and visualisations fills designed, customisable screens, with a provider-style xG, one definition per metric, and plain names. Staff explain live, at half-time, after the match, and across matches. The playtest asks testers why a match was won or lost and checks their answers against the true causes.
  • In-game management. Substitutions and formation changes wait for a stoppage, while shouts and instructions start at once and spread through the players over time. The manager has team instructions, opposition targets, player instructions, and prepared game-state plans, and sees each player's state, the analysts' read of the opponent, what each change did, and the assistant's costed proposals. The match pauses freely or on chosen events. Each competition has its own dated rule pack, concussion substitutes included where allowed, and the manager decides on a head knock after the medics advise. The AI manager has the same rules, information, and reaction time, and uses every tool as well as its skill allows. Every change has a cost, forked matches with paired seeds band its effect against real game-state data, and the exploit hunt tries sequences of changes. After a skip, the manager's plans and triggers apply.
  • Set pieces. The manager builds routines from a library, an editor, and the set-piece coach's proposals, names takers per kind and side, and sets defensive jobs within a zonal, man-marking, or mixed system. First contact comes from the delivery's flight, each player's run, height, and jumping, and a heading duel, with blocks and holding the referee may punish. Throw-ins include long-throw, retention, and quick throws. Routines need drilling, a squad holds only a few well, and opponents learn a favourite routine. Each routine's rates are banded, the set-piece goal share has a band per league that may drift as the world copies what works, penalties are banded per league with a shoot-out pressure curve, and a coach's effect stays inside the real spread.
  • Fatigue, injuries, and congestion. A player tires through a short sprint reserve, a slow reserve, and mental fatigue, and paces himself when low, but late tiredness stays as modest as real data shows. Between matches, condition recovers over about three days while an accumulated load, seen only through the staff, raises injury risk. Injuries come from a hazard on each tackle, sprint, collision, landing, and turn, with real types, layoffs, and recurrence, plus training injuries, at real frequency. Returns pass through phases, knocks carry over and can be hidden, and history raises risk by site. Congestion costs mostly injury risk, as in real football, and rotation is also pushed by squad morale, sharpness, the calendar, and staff advice. Training follows a daily rhythm and a preseason base, sharpness comes from matches by level and match-simulation sessions, travel adds jet lag, and illness can spread through a squad. Serious injuries offer treatment choices in which the player and agent have a say, and a return affects confidence and caution. Medicals and market values read injury history, and an injury crisis reaches the board, the players, and the press. Background leagues carry the same state. Season bands check counts, burden, types, recurrence, and the congestion effect on full-engine seasons.
  • Long-term integration. Each match feeds the wider game: young players grow from training, minutes at the right challenge, and good performances; form runs at two speeds; reputation and value follow level-weighted ratings and big stages; and the board, the fans, and income read results against expectation. Loops are dampened by modest, banded mood, complacency, opponents who lift, real league rules, wage pressure, ageing, poaching, and rivals who react, plus a soft pull in AI choices. Every match shows its stakes. A multi-season check bands title turnover, fortune churn, and promotion and relegation rates, and guards stop long-save drift.
  • Match preparation. Knowing the opponent carries preparation. Separate analyst attributes set how well the report predicts the line-up, finds habits, and states its confidence, and analyst workload sets when it arrives. The staff prepare the plan, the training week, and the briefing by default, and the manager edits. Rehearsal gives a grasp of this match's instructions that fades after it, and the briefing moves how faithfully players follow the targets, what each knows of his opponent, and mood. Preparation helps only where the squad fits the plan, costs staff workload and training time, and has a wide band set after playtests. Surprises come from real change, adaptability, disguise, and leaks, AI managers read the manager's whole fingerprint, and the post-match report shows which prepared points worked.
  • Challenge and learning. The game serves genre veterans and newcomers, chosen at the start. Difficulty comes from the club the player picks and a help setting that moves only explanation and pauses, never staff accuracy, results, or the AI. Every area can go to staff; for a newcomer, areas open over the first season, introduced by the staff who run them; and the assistant points each week to what matters. A sacked manager looks for a new job and the save goes on, and AI clubs copy what wins, so a winning idea stops being new.
  • Watching the match. The base view is the top-down pitch with height, players as circles for now, with overlays, zoom, and follow. Match length comes from a five-mode ladder changeable mid-match, a dynamic mode, build-up before every moment, and custom rules, and the pitch fast-forwards between highlights. The player can rewind while paused and replay any part afterwards; other grounds show live scores and highlights after the whistle. Occasion comes from crowd sound, a pre-match build-up, and the pitch itself, and the realism test bands movement against tracking data.

The work runs in order. Engine realism comes first, starting with the test harness and then team shape and the block. The event-contract redesign comes next, and the season layer comes last. All twelve pieces are now written as three design documents.

Surprises come from real change, adaptability, disguise, and leaks; AI managers read the manager's whole fingerprint. Core: all of it. Scope: keep all, in the first version.

Decisions 14
  • Match preparation must avoid four faults of the genre: it must not become a weekly chore, its effect must be visible, it must not become an exploit, and both sides prepare. Why: The person named all four as true of management games today. core
  • Preparation has four parts: knowing the opponent (report, likely line-up, injuries, habits), the match plan (selection, tactic, opposition targets, set-piece plan), the training week (rehearsal against the opponent, drilling routines, rest), and the briefing (the team meeting and each player's job). core
  • Preparation helps only where the squad can carry out the plan: a plan beyond the players does nothing. Why: It ties preparation to the squad fit that already emerges from play, instead of a free bonus. core
  • Preparation costs staff workload and training time: analysts and coaches have limited capacity, and opponent work displaces fitness, development, and routine sessions, so even a rich club gives something up. core
  • Knowing the opponent carries the area: the report and the analysts' read are the part the game builds best and shows most, and the plan, the training week, and the briefing follow from what the staff find. core
  • When the week gets heavy, the training week is handed over first: the coaches set the sessions from the manager's plan. Accepted risk: The balance of rest and rehearsal is the coaches' choice, not the manager's. core
  • Every week the staff prepare every part by default, and the manager changes only what the manager wants, as delegation works in every other area. Why: It stops preparation from becoming a weekly chore without cutting any of the four parts. core
  • A better analyst's report predicts the line-up and shape more often, finds more of the opponent's habits, says honestly how sure it is, and arrives earlier: opposition analysis sets the line-up and the habits, calibration sets the confidence, and timing comes only from how busy the analysts are. core
  • Surprises that beat a good report come from four sources: real change (injuries, suspensions, rotation, a new manager), the opponent's hidden adaptability, strong managers who hide their plan on purpose, and leaks both ways through the press and players. Accepted risk: The manager's own press conferences and unhappy players can leak the manager's plan, and the report is least reliable in the biggest matches. core
  • The post-match report lists each prepared point and whether it worked, traced to its moments through the true-cause log. core
  • AI managers prepare against the manager by reading the manager's whole fingerprint over seasons, as the manager's analysts read theirs. Accepted risk: An established style becomes predictable, so a long-serving manager faces opponents who know the manager's habits. core
  • Preparation has no cap in the mechanism, but the forked-match check measures its effect on paired seeds and flags any value outside a wide design band set after playtests. Why: It keeps preparation inside the rule that every tool's effect is banded, without fixing a number no study supports. core
  • Rehearsal against one opponent gives each player a short-lived grasp of this match's specific instructions, which fades after the match, on top of the lasting grasp of the tactic. Accepted risk: One more short-lived value per player for each match. core
  • The briefing acts three ways: it raises how faithfully each player follows the opposition targets, it adds what each player knows about his direct opponent to his picture of the pitch, and it moves mood and focus as part of the pre-match team talk. core
Findings 1
  • No public study measures the effect of opponent-specific preparation on its own; real home advantage, about 0.3 goals per match, is the nearest scale. general knowledge of the home-advantage literature; no preparation study known

Challenge and the player's experience

exploredproblem side

difficulty, learning the game, and how much realism the player sees

The game answers overwhelm, losing without knowing why, easy once solved, and early sacking. A new save asks whether the player knows the genre. Difficulty comes from the club and a help setting that moves only explanation and pauses. Every area can go to staff; newcomers see areas open over the first season; the assistant points each week. A sacked manager finds a new job, and AI clubs copy what wins. Core: all of it. Scope: keep all, in the first version.

Decisions 7
  • The game must answer four faults of deep management games: overwhelm at the start, losing without knowing why, a game that is easy once solved, and a new player punished with a sacking before learning the game. Why: The person named all four as true. core
  • The game serves genre veterans and football fans new to the genre: a new save asks how much the player knows and sets the defaults to match. Accepted risk: Two first-hour paths to design and test. core
  • Difficulty comes from two levers: the club the player chooses, as in Football Manager, and a setting for how much help and explanation the player gets. Neither moves the match engine, the results, or the AI. core
  • Every area can be handed to staff; for a player who said at the start that the genre is new, areas open over the first season, each introduced by the staff member who runs it, while a veteran starts with everything open; and for everyone the assistant points each week to what matters. core
  • The help setting moves how much the game explains (plain summaries, why a figure matters, what to look at) and how often it pauses to point at a key moment; it never changes how accurate staff readings are. Why: Staff readings stay wrong in proportion to staff quality, so the staff market keeps its value. core
  • A sacked manager keeps the career and looks for a new job, as real managers do, and the save continues. core
  • AI clubs copy what wins across the world, as real football copies trends, so a winning idea stops being new; this widens the set-piece drift the board already allows to tactics. Accepted risk: The world's style drifts over a long save, so the season test must keep league styles inside real ranges while trends move. core

Player traits, personality, and chemistry

exploredproblem and solution side

decision weights, personality effects, familiarity, and how strong they are

Traits are habits of play from the genre list of about fifty; a player carries any number but never two opposite traits, and each trait happens about 1.5 to 2 times as often as in similar players without it. Personality attributes move cards within the real spread, setback dips, leader calls, and professionalism, each with its own check. Understanding between players and grasp of the tactic grow through training and matches, capped by the five-match test; a squad change costs little and recovers fast, checked by a rotation band. Core: all three equally. Scope: keep all, in the first version. Top risk: stacked effects.

Your brief, criteria covered 11
  • Traits adjust decision weights, for example a player who tries long shots chooses shooting from distance more often. covered
  • Personality affects card risk. covered
  • Personality affects reaction to setbacks. covered
  • Personality affects leadership. covered
  • Personality affects professionalism. covered
  • Chemistry develops through familiarity between teammates, built up through training and matches. covered
  • Chemistry develops through familiarity with the tactical system, built up through training and matches. covered
  • Effects are modest and understandable: players see their impact without these factors overpowering ability. covered
  • Traits that are purely cosmetic. covered
  • Chemistry systems so strong that squad changes feel punished. covered
  • Players with a trait show the associated behaviour at a measurably higher rate than those without it. covered
Decisions 21
  • The word trait means a habit of play, such as shooting from distance, as in the brief and the genre; the thirteen personality values are called personality attributes. Every earlier use of trait for a personality value now reads personality attribute.
  • All eleven criteria of the traits, personality, and chemistry brief are in scope, and the gaps are walked in the brief's order.
  • Each trait is sized so that its behaviour happens at a set multiple of the rate of similar players without it, for example about 1.5 to 2 times; the multiple is a design target per trait, and the check compares players with and without the trait in the same role and quality.
  • A player may carry any number of traits; every habit a player has is a trait, but no player carries two opposite traits, such as shoots from distance and never shoots from distance. Accepted risk: A player with many traits stands out less, because stacked traits blur.
  • The game offers the genre list of about fifty traits, as Football Manager has, such as shoots from distance, dives into tackles, and plays one-twos. Accepted risk: Many genre traits have no real rate to compare against, so their targets are design values measured against the game's own players.
  • A trait has no cost beyond the choices it changes: it costs exactly the worse options it sometimes picks, which the true-cause log shows.
  • The check for personality and cards bands how card rates spread across players against real data, so hot-heads and calm players appear in real proportion.
  • The whole spread of card rates from calm to hot-headed players matches the real spread between players of one position, and the league card total stays in its band.
  • An in-match setback, such as a goal against, going behind, or an own error, dips the confidence of the player at fault most and of the rest a little, each sized by pressure handling and determination, and the dip fades over minutes. Accepted risk: One more short-lived state per player during a match.
  • The setback effect is checked by the existing bands on real game-state data: how teams play and score when behind must still hold.
  • A leader on the pitch makes defensive calls land, shortens nearby teammates' setback dips, and relays the manager's shouts so new instructions spread faster through the team.
  • Leadership is checked by the sensitivity test: changing the captain's leadership may move only defensive organisation, setback recovery, and how fast shouts spread.
  • Professionalism acts three ways: how fast a player grows in training; how well he looks after himself (recovery between matches, sharpness kept, and fewer knocks hidden); and off-pitch conduct (fewer fines and incidents, and a steadier dressing-room presence).
  • Professionalism is checked two ways: the career bands (peak age, decline, youth conversion) must hold with professionalism explaining part of the spread, and a season sensitivity check changes one player's professionalism and lets only his growth, condition, and conduct move.
  • Training together builds on-pitch understanding between two players only up to a middle level; the top level needs minutes together in matches.
  • Chemistry with the tactical system is checked against a design target for how many weeks a squad needs to learn a new tactic, measured on season runs.
  • The caps of the mind and familiarity families are sized by the five-match test: whatever the mood, personality effects, and chemistry, a top player still looks clearly better than an average player in the same role in about two of three runs of five matches.
  • The player reads mood, personality, and chemistry effects two ways: each player shows one short state line, such as settled, low after the error, or new to the system, with its effect in words; and the assistant names the biggest of these effects before and after the match.
  • A squad change stays fair two ways: losing all understanding and tactic grasp costs at most a small share of what a real quality gap costs, so a better new player helps at once; and understanding and grasp rise fastest at the start, so most of the loss returns within a few weeks.
  • Squad-change fairness is checked by a rotation band: teams that rotate a lot must not do worse than real data shows for heavy rotation.
  • Traits, personality attributes, and chemistry are the core of the area equally, and all three go into the first version.
Findings 2
  • The engine today has no traits, personality, morale, familiarity, relationships, or cohesion. Aggression is the only personality-like value: it raises the foul and yellow-card chance. Players differ in decisions only through role and plan offsets, and decision noise falls as decisions and composure rise.
  • The top risk of the area is stacked effects: mood, personality attributes, traits, and chemistry together could swamp ability even when each cap holds alone. The five-match test must run with every family switched on at once, not one family at a time.
Assumptions 1
  • Real match data can measure how much heavy rotation costs a team, so the rotation band has a real target.
Tensions 1
  • The word trait means two things on the board: a habit of play such as shooting from distance, and one of the thirteen personality traits. The brief's traits are habits; its personality is the traits.
Stacked effects 4
  • The four families of effective ability (body, mind, familiarity, surroundings) combine softly: each further effect in the same direction counts for less than the one before, so stacked effects fade out without a hard edge. This amends the rule that the family caps add. Accepted risk: A soft combine is harder to explain and to tune than a plain sum.
  • Partnerships and cohesion keep their own separate cap in the team layer; only the four families share the soft combine.
  • The worst case of stacked effects is checked by a tail band: season runs count how often a top player has a run of very poor matches, and the rate is banded against real data.
  • Stacked effects show without the internals: the state line and the assistant speak in words about how the player is, and the game shows no sizes, no list of effects, and no net number.

Psychological factors

exploredproblem and solution side

momentum, pressure from match importance, game-state behaviour, and comebacks

Momentum: good events lift confidence and setbacks dip it, a home crowd adds to the lift, and both fade within minutes, checked by a next-goal band. Pressure: match importance comes from the stakes, rivalry, and what the match means to the player; it harms nervous players and lifts big-match players a little, checked by a big-match band. Game state: players read the score by personality, leaning within the manager's mentality, checked by possession and shot shares cut by margin, period, and importance. Comebacks come from eight mechanisms with no script, checked by a comeback band. Core: all equally. Scope: keep all, in the first version. Top risks: double counting, thin data, and a stacked mind family.

Your brief, criteria covered 7
  • Momentum after key events is small in effect and fades within minutes. covered
  • Pressure scales with match importance and affects players according to their composure and big-match temperament. covered
  • Game state changes behaviour: teams leading typically cede possession and defend deeper, while teams behind take more risks. covered
  • Comebacks happen occasionally through realistic mechanisms, not scripting. covered
  • Excessive momentum that makes matches feel predetermined. covered
  • Teams behaving the same regardless of the score. covered
  • Possession and shot shares for leading and trailing teams follow real-world patterns across a batch of matches. covered
Decisions 13
  • Momentum comes from the mind family: good events (a goal, a big save, a near miss) lift confidence as setbacks dip it, sized by the same personality attributes, and both fade within minutes; a home crowd adds to the lift after a good home event. Accepted risk: A second short-lived state per player, and a crowd term that must stay inside the measured home advantage.
  • Momentum is checked by a next-goal band: after a goal, how often the same team scores the next goal within a set time is banded against real data, so momentum can never make matches feel decided.
  • Match importance has three parts: the stakes (a final, a knockout tie, a title, relegation, or promotion decider, rising late in the season); rivalry (derbies, the crowd, and the audience); and what the match means to one player, such as facing a former club or a first start. Accepted risk: A per-player importance value for every match adds work before each kick-off.
  • Pressure acts by pressure handling: a player with low pressure handling gets wider error tails, slower decisions, and a lean to safer options (the short pass over the shot) in important matches; a player with high pressure handling is lifted a little, a big-match player.
  • Pressure is checked by a big-match band: how finals, derbies, and deciders differ from ordinary matches in goals, cards, and errors is banded against real data.
  • Players read the score and the clock themselves, not only through the manager: how much each player reacts is set by personality attributes, for example determination pushes a player harder when behind.
  • A comeback comes from four mechanisms, with no script: the trailing team takes more risk; the leading team's focus drops a little, more for players with low concentration or professionalism; substitutes bring fresh legs and a new shape; and a goal back lifts the trailing team and dips the leading one.
