  • The reaction to the score is not fixed: the mentality the manager sets matters, some teams keep pressing when ahead to kill the match, and the reaction fades rather than lasting all match.
  • Comebacks are checked by a comeback band: how often a team that trails by one or two goals draws or wins, by the minute it fell behind, is banded against real data.
  • Four more comeback mechanisms join: a red card changes the balance and the side with more players pushes; a home crowd pushes a trailing home team in added time, and late goals cluster in added time as real data shows; a leading team with low pressure handling tightens up late in an important match; and a trailing team sends its keeper up for a late set piece, more often in cups and on the final days of a league.
  • The manager's set mentality is the base of a player's reaction to the score, and each player's personality only leans a little within it; a player never overrides the mentality on his own.
  • The acceptance check bands possession and shot shares by score margin (behind by two, by one, level, ahead by one, by two), by 15-minute period, and by match importance, with team strength removed. Accepted risk: Three-way cuts leave thin cells, so the band needs large batches and real data split the same way.
  • Momentum, pressure, game state, and comebacks are the core of the area equally, and all four go into the first version.
Findings 2
  • In the engine today, the score changes behaviour only through the AI manager: from minute 70 a trailing team's mentality rises one step and pressing goes high, and from minute 80 a leading team's mentality drops one step with time-wasting. Players themselves do not read the score, and nothing models momentum or match importance.
  • The area has three top risks: double counting, because players, the manager's mentality, momentum, and pressure all push the same way when behind, so the game-state effect could come out too large; thin data, because the next-goal, big-match, and three-way shares bands need finely split real data; and a stacked mind family, because momentum and pressure add two more mind effects under the soft combine, so the tail band may fail.
Assumptions 2
  • Real event data gives how often the scoring team also scores the next goal within a set time, with team strength removed, so the next-goal band has a real target.
  • Real data shows a measurable difference between big matches and ordinary ones in goals, cards, and errors, large enough to band.

Match engine architecture

exploredsolution side

modules, interfaces, state, modifiers, configuration, and testing

Every part of the engine becomes a module behind a typed interface, registered by name and chosen per slot in configuration. Modules read a read-only view and return proposed changes that only the loop applies; each optional module has an off version; ability factors are modifiers grouped in the four families. The quick engine stays a fitted model but emits the same event types. Each module carries a card with its calibration source. Types enforce the read-only view, an off-switch run and a swap test check the contract, and one staged refactor to the contract comes before the realism work, after the match-engine branch ships: streams split and a new baseline first, then parts one at a time, pure parts first and referee rules and possession last. Scope: keep, first in the order of work. Top risk: effects that cross module lines.

Your brief, criteria covered 16
  • The match engine is made up of separate modules, each responsible for one part of the simulation. covered
  • Modules communicate only through defined interfaces, not by calling each other's internals. covered
  • Any module can be replaced with another version that meets the same interface, without changes elsewhere. covered
  • Every optional module has an off version so its effect can be measured in isolation. covered
  • A single shared match state holds all match data; only the central match loop changes it. covered
  • Modules return results and proposed changes rather than editing the match state directly. covered
  • Player abilities used in calculations pass through a sequence of modifiers (such as fatigue, pressure, weather), and new factors are added as new modifiers. covered
  • The engine's output is a stream of match events; statistics, commentary, ratings, and replays are built from that stream separately. covered
  • Which modules are used, and their tuning values, are set in configuration rather than in code. covered
  • The same seed, inputs, and configuration always produce the same match. covered
  • Each module uses its own random number stream, so replacing one module does not change the others' results. covered
  • The quick engine and the full engine share the same core calculation modules and produce the same types of events. covered
  • Every module can be tested on its own, using constructed match situations. covered
  • Every change is checked against realistic match statistics over a large number of simulated matches. covered
  • Two versions of a module can be run side by side on identical matches and their results compared. covered
  • Each module documents its purpose, inputs, outputs, tuning values, and the real-world figures it was calibrated against. covered
Decisions 6
  • Every part of the engine is a module behind a typed interface, registered by name. A module reads a read-only view of the match state and returns results and proposed changes; only the central loop applies them. Each optional module has an off version, so its effect can be measured alone, and any module can be replaced by another that meets the same interface. Accepted risk: Proposed changes and a read-only view add a copy or an apply step on every tick, which presses on the performance budget.
  • Each factor that changes an ability (fatigue, pressure, weather, momentum, and so on) is a modifier module that names its family; each family (body, mind, familiarity, surroundings) combines its modifiers, and the families combine softly. A new factor is added as a new modifier.
  • The quick engine stays a statistical model fitted from full-engine results, but it emits the same event types as the full engine, so every consumer (statistics, commentary, ratings, reports) reads both. It shares no calculation modules with the full engine, a deliberate departure from the brief.
  • A configuration file names the module and version used for each slot; each module carries a card (purpose, inputs, outputs, tuning values, and the calibration source and band) that a check requires to be complete.
  • The module contract is checked three ways: the interface gives a module only a read-only view, so the compiler refuses a module that writes the state; an off-switch run switches each optional module and each modifier off in turn, and the match must still finish and emit valid events; and a swap test puts a stand-in module in each slot, and the rest of the engine must run unchanged.
  • One refactor moves the engine to the module contract before the realism work starts, so every later work piece builds on it. Accepted risk: The realism work waits for the refactor.
Findings 2
  • The engine today splits its parts into files (fatigue, fouls, offside, steering, decisions, the AI manager), many written as pure functions that take players and a draw and return a result; one central step in the simulation owns the match, though steering still moves players in place. It has a three-hook plugin boundary (decision, rule, commentary), feature flags in the tuning file that switch a candidate model for a paired run, tuning values and rule packs in configuration, and a scenario module for constructed situations. It has no module interface or registry, no way to switch a module off by configuration, a modifier step only for fatigue, and one random stream in the code, although split streams are decided.
  • The area's top risk is effects that cross module lines: real football couples fatigue, pressure, and decisions tightly, so strict modules may hide or double an effect that spans two modules.
The refactor 4
  • The engine is about 16,200 lines. Match logic sits in impl blocks across nine files that write the simulation directly: about 35 writes in the referee rules, 17 in the ball and possession code, 9 in decisions, plus fatigue, the AI manager, tactic changes, snapshots, steering, and discipline. Fouls, offside, shot maths, fatigue maths, steering maths, and the AI's pre-match setup are already pure functions and move easily; the referee rules, possession, blocks and saves, and decisions (whose scoring draws noise inside itself) are tangled.
  • The engine has one random stream (ChaCha8) shared by 31 draw sites in decisions, rules, the simulation, and fatigue, so adding or moving one draw shifts every later draw. Splitting it into a stream per module changes every match outcome at once: it breaks the 12 test files that script referee or injury draws, breaks snapshot resume, and moves every realism band, so calibration must be redone.
  • The module refactor stays one piece of work before the realism work, done in stages: first split the random stream into one stream per module and per player and kind of action, recalibrate, and freeze a new baseline; then move the parts to the contract one at a time, each move holding that baseline exactly (same seeds, same events); the pure parts (fouls, offside, shot, fatigue, and steering maths, the AI's setup) move first, and the referee rules and possession last. Hidden settings, such as the environment variable read during a tick, are removed in the refactor.
  • The match-engine workflow finishes its review and hands off its branch as it is; the module refactor starts afterwards as its own workflow on the merged engine.

Technical robustness

exploredsolution side

determinism, performance, exploit testing, data sourcing, versioning, and debugging

Matches replay identically on every machine from fixed steps, a stored seed, and split random streams. One matchday of every active league must finish within the matchday wait and a full season within its limit on the reference laptop, and a release scan fails on licensed data. Replays store their frames, seed, and version; saves migrate forward; every release loads old saves and plays old replays. Developers get a draw trace, a bisect tool, and in-game bug reports. Retired people archive, and a 50-season check guards speed and save size. The exploit hunt runs before release. Core: all equally. Scope: keep all, in the first version. Top risk: none named.

Your brief, criteria covered 10
  • Determinism: fixed time steps and stored seeds allow exact replays. covered
  • Performance: background matches run quickly enough to simulate many leagues without slowing the game. covered
  • Exploit testing: automated searches for tactics or player combinations that break realism. covered
  • Data sourcing: a clear decision on real versus fictional players, with licensing considered. covered
  • Versioning so saved games and replays remain compatible after updates. covered
  • Logging and debugging tools to trace unexpected results. covered
  • Non-reproducible bugs. covered
  • Slow simulation as the database grows. covered
  • Exploits found by players after release. covered
  • Performance benchmarks are met for a full season of background fixtures, and replays reproduce exactly. covered
Decisions 6
  • A replay stores its recorded frames and also its seed and engine version, so it plays back without the engine after any update and can be re-simulated on the old engine for debugging; saves carry a version number and migrate forward with one migration per change. Accepted risk: Stored frames make replays larger, and old engine versions must stay available for debugging.
  • Developers get three tools: a debug mode that records every random draw with its stream, the option scores, and the rule that fired, so any moment traces back to its causes; a bisect tool that re-runs a seed on two engine versions and finds the first tick where they differ; and an in-game bug report that attaches the save, the seed, and the version so a player's bug replays at once.
  • Retired and inactive people move to compact archive records, and old match detail compacts to summaries, as the history window already does for events, so the active database stays small over decades.
  • The benchmark requires that one matchday of every active league finishes within the player's matchday wait on the reference laptop and that a full season of every league fits the season-advance limit; a release check scans the build and fails if it contains licensed data.
  • Three checks guard compatibility and growth: every release loads a stored save from each past version, migrates it, and plays a season; every release plays stored replays from each past version and re-simulates their seeds on the old engine to match the frames; and the 50-season check fails if season speed or save size after 50 seasons grows past a set margin over season one.
  • All parts of technical robustness are the core equally, and all of it goes into the first version. The person named no top risk.
Findings 1
  • The engine today already runs a fixed-timestep loop whose match starts from a stored seed, and the same seed and build replay byte-identical on one machine.

Advanced analytical models

exploredown work group

thirteen models for play, AI managers and staff, and analysis

A separate work group of thirteen models. In play: expected threat and possession value, pitch control in each player's scanned picture, pass completion, off-ball scoring opportunity, receiver prediction, and a pressing model, plus defensive ghosting. For AI and staff: win probability, an Elo-style team rating, and style and role detection. For analysis: action value and decision quality. Each model starts on real data and is refitted on the engine's matches after each change. Models that change play must pass the comparison test (closer to real on progression and choice match, inside the budget); display models pass the one-record check. The display and staff models come early; the in-play models and ghosting come last. Full models run in the watched match and a stand-in elsewhere. Top risks: threat may make choices less real, cost, and thin freeze-frame and tracking data.

Your brief, criteria covered 7
  • Pitch control models help players assess space when making passes and runs. covered
  • Expected Threat (xT) or Expected Possession Value (EPV) helps the AI judge whether a pass or carry improves the attack. covered
  • These models can be displayed to the player in post-match analysis. covered
  • Computation is efficient enough for real-time use or approximated where needed. covered
  • Models too computationally expensive for background matches. covered
  • Models used for display but not reflected in behaviour. covered
  • AI decisions guided by xT or EPV produce more realistic progression patterns than simpler rules, confirmed through comparison testing. covered
Decisions 13
  • Advanced analytical models are their own separate work group, an attempt to improve emergent play; the rest of the realism work does not wait on it.
  • The work group builds two versions: expected threat as the 'value gained' term inside the existing option score, and a full possession-value score; the comparison test picks the winner. Accepted risk: Two models to build and fit, one of which is thrown away.
  • Pitch control feeds each player's picture of the pitch, limited by what that player scanned, so a player can misjudge space; passes, runs, and defending read space only through that picture.
  • The full models run in the watched match, and a cheap stand-in runs in background matches, with a check that results match.
  • The comparison test plays the same matches with each version and simpler rules, and compares three things: progression against real data (progressive passes and carries, entries to the final third and the box, possession chains); how often each version picks the option a real player picked in the same situation, from event data with freeze frames; and whether it stays inside the performance budget. The winner must be closer to real on the first two and inside the budget.
  • Within the analytical-models work group, the full models run in the watched match and a cheap stand-in runs everywhere else. This is an exception to keeping the full engine in background matches. Accepted risk: A result may depend on whether the match was watched.
  • The analytical-models work group comes last in the order of work, after all the other realism work, as a later improvement. Amended in session 8: only the in-play models and ghosting stay last; the display and staff models come early.
  • Four more models join players' choices in the work group: a pass-completion model (the chance a pass reaches its target from positions, pressure, and pass type, the chance half of the option score); an off-ball scoring-opportunity model (pitch control times the chance the ball arrives times the scoring chance from there, which drives runs); receiver prediction (which team-mate the carrier will likely pick, so defenders anticipate and intercept); and a turnover and pressing model (the chance a press wins the ball here and now, which decides when to press).
  • Four models serve AI managers and staff: defensive ghosting (a learned model of where a typical defender stands, a positioning benchmark and a 'where he should have been' view); in-match win probability (AI substitutions and risk, staff advice, highlight swings); an Elo-style team strength rating (board expectations, AI, odds, and the fast model); and style and role detection from events (analysts, scouting, and player similarity).
  • Two models serve the analysis the player sees: action value (VAEP or on-ball value, crediting every action including defending, as the base of player ratings); and decision quality, which compares each choice with the best option in the player's own picture and shows missed options, possible only because the simulation knows the truth.
  • Each model starts fitted on real event and tracking data, then is refitted on the engine's own matches after each engine change; the comparison test watches for drift away from real.
  • Only models that change what happens in a match pass the comparison test and the budget: the in-play models, ghosting when defenders use it, and win probability for AI managers. Display models (action value, decision quality, style and role, and the team rating on screen) pass the one-record check only.
  • The display and staff models (team rating, win probability, style and role detection, action value, decision quality) come early, in the work that needs them; the in-play models and ghosting stay in the last work group.
Findings 2
  • The board's option score already follows the real choice model, which weighs completion, a running receiver, and a forward option far above threat; a player who chased threat alone would choose less like real players, so a threat model must earn its place in the comparison test.
  • The work group has three top risks: real players weigh completion above threat, so both models may choose less like real players and leave nothing to merge; pitch control and possession value for 22 players may cost too much for the budget; and the choice-match test needs freeze-frame event data, which is thin in public data.
Assumptions 1
  • Enough public tracking data exists to fit pitch control, off-ball scoring opportunity, receiver prediction, pressing, and ghosting on real data; the board's public tracking so far is thin, for example 7 DFL matches.
Tensions 1
  • A cheap stand-in for background matches pulls against two earlier decisions: no realism lever is pulled, so background matches keep the full engine; and a result never depends on whether the match was watched. Resolved: Within this work group only, the full models run in the watched match and a cheap stand-in everywhere else; this is an accepted exception to the no-lever rule, and the risk that a result depends on watching is accepted.
Tracking data 4
  • Public full tracking of all 22 players and the ball adds up to about 74 matches: PFF's 64 matches of the 2022 World Cup at about 30 frames a second with synchronised events (free by request, terms not published), the DFL IDSSE set of 7 Bundesliga matches at 25 frames a second (CC-BY 4.0), and 3 Metrica sample matches (no formal licence). SkillCorner adds 10 A-League matches of broadcast tracking with partial visibility and pressing and run labels (MIT). StatsBomb 360 has 292 matches of event freeze frames, not continuous tracking, under a non-commercial agreement. Other sets (Last Row, Alfheim, SoccerNet, SoccerTrack) do not fit these models.
  • Public data is enough for pitch control (few parameters, fits on IDSSE and Metrica), pass completion and receiver prediction (about 15,000 passes on clean licences, over 60,000 with PFF), pressing (SkillCorner labels plus IDSSE), and the pitch-control half of the off-ball model. Deep defensive ghosting needs PFF; the 7 clean IDSSE matches support only a simple regression ghost.
  • The PFF World Cup 2022 tracking set is used under the same accepted licence risk as StatsBomb and Transfermarkt: only derived statistics and fitted models reach the game, and no data is shipped. The models fit on PFF, IDSSE, SkillCorner, and Metrica together. Accepted risk: PFF's terms are not published and may forbid commercial use.
  • Defensive ghosting aims for the deep imitation model from the start, fitted on the 64 PFF matches. Accepted risk: One tournament of national teams may not describe club defending, and deep models cost more to run.

Environmental factors

exploredproblem and solution side

weather, pitch quality, and home advantage with crowd size

Rain and wind act through the ball physics, and a cap keeps each effect small. A poor pitch widens touch and pass errors, pitches wear through the season, and a club can prepare its pitch for its tactic. Home advantage scales with attendance and fullness and falls with away fans; travel distance has only a very small effect. Each condition has its own band, with a limit on its distance from the dry band. Core: weather and pitch. Scope: keep all, in the first version. Top risks: caps with no real source, and home advantage counted twice.

Your brief, criteria covered 6
  • Weather has modest effects: rain speeds up the ball and increases slips; wind affects long passes; heat increases fatigue. covered
  • Pitch quality affects technical play. covered
  • Home advantage is calibrated to real data and adjusts with crowd size. covered
  • Weather effects too strong and random-feeling. covered
  • Home advantage much larger than in reality. covered
  • Home and away win rates match target ranges, and weather effects are measurable but small. covered
Decisions 5
  • Rain and wind act through the ball physics: rain lowers ball friction so the ball skids faster and raises the slip chance, and wind pushes the ball in flight, most on long and high balls; a cap keeps each condition's effect on goals and passing small. Accepted risk: The caps are design values, because no public source measures rain or wind.
  • Pitch quality acts three ways: a poor pitch makes the ball bounce and roll less truly, which widens touch and pass errors, more for players with low technique; the pitch wears through the season by use and weather, which the groundsman slows; and a club can prepare its pitch (long grass, watered, narrow within the Laws) to suit its tactic.
  • Home advantage scales with the crowd: attendance and how full the stadium is set the home effect, from the measured empty-stadium level to the normal full level; the away support share lowers it; and travel distance lowers the away team's condition only very slightly. Jet lag across time zones stays as the travel decision sets it.
  • Weather stays small by condition bands: each condition (dry, wet, windy, worn) keeps its own band, and a limit caps how far apart the dry band and each other band may be.
  • Weather and pitch are the core of the area; the crowd and travel support them, and the effect of travel distance is very small. All of it goes into the first version.
Findings 2
  • The engine today has no weather, no pitch quality, and no crowd: every match plays in the same conditions, and home advantage exists only if the realism test measures it from play.
  • The area has two top risks: the rain, wind, and pitch caps are design values with no real source, so their size is a guess; and the crowd, the referee's leaning, nervous players, late belief, and travel all add to home advantage, so the total could pass the home-advantage band.

Goalkeeper specifics

exploredproblem and solution side

keeper attributes, rebounds, distribution, save rate, and keeper styles

The nine keeper attributes stay, and the profile shows positioning, distribution, and sweeping. A keeper's style comes from the keeper role, traits, and attributes. Distribution follows the team's build-up instruction and a keeper instruction for targets. Keeper effects are amplified like other players', so a top keeper stands out, while the league save rate stays at 65 to 75 percent. Four bands check keepers: save rate, goals prevented, rebound shots by handling, and sweeper actions. Core: all equally. Scope: keep all, in the first version. Top risk: none named.

Your brief, criteria covered 7
  • Separate attributes for positioning, reflexes, handling, command of area, one-on-ones, distribution, and sweeping. covered
  • Handling affects rebounds, which create second chances. covered
  • Distribution style links to the team's build-up tactics. covered
  • Save percentages broadly in the region of 65 to 75 percent across a league. covered
  • Goalkeepers being too dominant or too weak. covered
  • No distinction between shot-stopping and sweeping styles. covered
  • Save percentage and goals conceded against xG vary sensibly with goalkeeper quality. covered
Decisions 6
  • The nine keeper attributes stay: positioning is the shared attribute, distribution is kicking and throwing, and sweeping is rushing out, shown under those names on the keeper's profile.
  • A keeper's style comes three ways: the manager's keeper role (line keeper or sweeper keeper) sets the base, traits such as comes off the line, rushes out, or eccentricity lean the choices, and attributes decide how well the keeper does it.
  • Keeper distribution follows two instructions: the team's build-up instruction (play out short, mixed, go long) sets the base, and a keeper instruction chooses the targets (centre-backs, full-backs, the target man, fast throws); the keeper picks within them by pressure and his kicking and throwing.
  • Keepers are checked by four bands: the league save rate at about 65 to 75 percent; goals prevented (expected goals on target minus goals conceded) per keeper against the real spread; how often a save gives a rebound shot, by keeper handling; and sweeper actions outside the box per match, by keeper.
  • Keeper effects are amplified like other players', so a top keeper stands out within a few matches; the goals-prevented band records a departure from the real spread on purpose, while the league save rate stays at 65 to 75 percent and the league goal bands hold. Accepted risk: The goals-prevented band departs from real data on purpose.
  • Shot-stopping, style, and distribution are the core of the area equally, and all go into the first version. The person named no top risk.
Findings 1
  • The attribute file today has nine goalkeeping attributes: handling, reflexes, aerial reach, one-on-ones, kicking, throwing, command of area, communication, and rushing out. Positioning is the shared outfield attribute, distribution is split into kicking and throwing, and sweeping is rushing out.
Assumptions 1
  • Public data gives goals prevented, rebound shots after saves, and sweeper actions per keeper per season, enough to band the spread between keepers.

Data for the new bands

session 8research

Six data needs from briefs 12 to 14 were looked up. Referee card spread, video-referee misses, and the cost of rotation have real sources. The next-goal rate and big-match differences are covered only in part, and poor runs have no study, so they are counted from rating-site data.

Decided 3
  • Momentum keeps its lift and dip, and the next-goal band decides their size: if the data puts momentum near zero, the effect on scoring is near zero, but the state is still shown in words.
  • The poor-run rate for the tail band is counted from player ratings on public rating sites, used only inside calibration; the data is not repackaged, shown, or sold. Accepted risk: The rating sites' terms of use may not allow collection, the same accepted licence risk as the other calibration data.
  • The big-match band covers only what is measured: fewer goals in finals, home advantage lost in derbies and halved in cups. Cards and errors in big matches stay unbanded.
What the research found 6
  • Referees differ by about 1.5 times in yellow cards once teams are removed: across 7,203 top-five-league matches (2018-19 to 2021-22), the strictest referee showed 1.23 times the league rate and the most lenient 0.81 times (Royal Statistical Society journal, 'Yellow fever'). Per-referee fouls exist only as raw figures on statistics sites, with no team adjustment.
  • The Premier League's key match incidents panel counted 17 missed video-referee interventions in 2023-24 and 9 in 2024-25 over 380 matches each, about 0.02 to 0.045 a match, beside about 30 on-field errors a season.
  • Real evidence for momentum is weak: the same team scores the next goal more often than the opponent across 3,433 matches, but with no strength adjustment; a strength-adjusted survival study found a goal changes the timing of the next goal the same way for the team that scored and the team that conceded; no effect follows a goal just before half-time; and teams two goals up concede more soon after. No paper gives a next-goal rate with strength removed.
  • Big matches differ only partly: English cup finals average about 20 percent fewer goals than league matches (a press source, not strength-adjusted); Bundesliga derbies from 1999 to 2009 differ from other matches only in that home advantage disappears; and home advantage in cup matches is about half that in league matches (0.27 against 0.51 goals). No measured effect of derbies on cards was found.
  • No public study counts runs of very poor matches for top players; the tail band needs its own count from player-rating data, which sites such as WhoScored, SofaScore, and FotMob publish but do not license.
  • Rotation costs a little and less for rich squads: across 16,720 matches in four leagues (2010-11 to 2021-22), more rotation lowered points in England and France with negligible effect in Italy and Spain, and the cost shrank as squad value rose; a Chinese study found each step of rotation cut win chance by 6.4 percent in congested weeks; Bundesliga coaches who kept winning line-ups won no more often.

Rules, officiating, and competition formats

exploredproblem and solution side

the Laws, referee profiles, the video referee, and competition rules

The engine implements the complete IFAB Laws with each league's differences, including every offside edge case, checked by a scenario per clause, bands on offsides, penalties, and free kicks, and a review of sampled calls. Each foul is graded careless, reckless, or excessive force, context cards apply, and the referee's strictness moves the lines; tackling is set by team and player instructions; a referee spread band checks cards and fouls. The video referee is checked on overturns, delay, and missed errors. Each competition has a full rule pack and its real tie-breakers. Core: all equally. Scope: keep all, in the first version. Top risk: none named.

Your brief, criteria covered 7
  • The Laws of the Game are implemented, including offside with correct timing, advantage, foul severity leading to appropriate cards, and second-yellow dismissals. covered
  • Referee profiles vary in strictness and consistency. covered
  • VAR is available by competition, with reviews for goals, penalties, and red cards. covered
  • Competition formats support extra time, penalty shootouts, varying substitution rules, squad registration limits, and tie-breakers. covered
  • Missing offside checks in edge cases. covered
  • Card rates that do not respond to referee strictness or tackling style. covered
  • Card and foul rates vary appropriately with referee profile and team aggression settings. covered
Decisions 9
  • The card follows the foul: each foul is graded careless, reckless, or excessive force from the tackle's speed, angle, and timing, and the referee's strictness moves the line between grades; context cards also apply, for denying an obvious goal-scoring chance, stopping a promising attack, and persistent fouling.
  • The engine implements the complete IFAB Laws of the Game, with each league's and competition's own differences, including every offside edge case: interfering with an opponent without touching the ball, a defender's deliberate play resetting offside while a deflection or save does not, a rebound off the post, bar, or keeper staying the same phase, and a defender off the pitch without permission still counting. Accepted risk: The complete Laws are a large rule surface, and every clause needs its own scenario.
  • Tackling style is set two ways: a team instruction (stay on feet, normal, get stuck in) and a per-player instruction to tackle harder or ease off; players lean within them by aggression.
  • Cards and fouls are checked by a referee spread band: how card and foul rates spread across real referees in one league is banded, and the tackling setting must move the rates in the real direction.
  • The Laws are checked three ways: one set-up scenario per clause of each Law, and a Law counts as done only when all its clause scenarios pass; bands on real rates of offsides, penalties, and free kicks per match; and a review of sampled calls, where replays of random offside and foul calls are checked against the Law. Accepted risk: The sampled-call review needs a person or a rule oracle to judge each replay.
  • The video referee is checked three ways against real data: an overturn band per match split into goals, penalties, and red cards; a band on how long reviews take and how much time they add; and a band on the clear errors it still misses.
  • Each competition breaks ties with its real order, for example goal difference first in England, head-to-head first in Spain and Italy, and UEFA's own rules for groups and knockout ties.
  • Each competition has its own full rule pack: substitutes and windows, bench size, concussion substitutes, extra time, shoot-outs, the video referee, tie-breakers, and registration. This replaces the one fixed pack the engine uses today.
  • The Laws, the referee, the video referee, and competition formats are the core of the area equally, and all four go into the first version. The person named no top risk.
Findings 2
  • The engine's rules today: offside is judged when a team-mate plays the ball and called when an offside player is the next to touch it, with level onside and no offside from throw-ins, goal kicks, or corners; the referee can play advantage and hold the card; a second caution sends a player off. The card comes from one draw raised by the tackler's aggression, not from how bad the foul was, and the referee has no strictness value. Extra time and shoot-outs run from the rule pack, with one fixed substitution pack.
  • The engine's offside has no edge cases: no interfering with an opponent without touching the ball (blocking the keeper's view), no reset when a defender deliberately plays the ball, no rebound off the keeper or post counted as the same phase, and no handling of a defender who has left the pitch.
Assumptions 2
  • Public data gives card and foul rates per referee over whole seasons, enough to band the spread between referees in one league.
  • Published reviews, such as a league's panel of key match incidents, give how many clear errors the video referee still misses per season, so that band has a real target.

The data model as an engine contract

exploredproblem and solution side

attribute jobs, scale, visible and hidden, effective ability, role weights, sensitivity

Every attribute keeps a job; actions run in stages with blended, fitted weights. Tenths behind 1 to 20 for every rating in the game, a convex curve set by the five-match target. A bounded effective-ability layer, hidden values in words. About 40 roles with three behaviours. Team numbers act on team actions; identity acts off the pitch. Height, age, and adaptation act; build is a word. Relationships carry two numbers. A sensitivity test covers attributes, settings, roles, team numbers, and, over seasons, staff ratings. Core: all of it. Scope: keep all; eight design-document pieces are stale, to rewrite at the next distil.

Decisions 44
  • Attributes intermingle in their effects: a calculation reads several attributes together, not one attribute per calculation. Why: Football actions draw on several qualities at once, and a blend stops any single attribute carrying an outcome alone. core
  • Pace is kept from dominating three ways together: it acts mainly in long runs while acceleration decides short races, every sprint costs stamina so a fast player who runs often fades late, and the sensitivity test fails pace when its effect is out of proportion. Why: Real top speed is rare, 0.7 percent of live time above 7 metres per second. core
  • The blend mixes three rules: an action runs in stages (see, choose, execute, under pressure), each stage has one main attribute with two or three smaller supporting weights, and the weights are fitted from real data where it exists and set by design where it does not. Why: Stages give each mental attribute its own moment, a main attribute keeps the rule readable on screen, and fitted weights keep it honest. Accepted risk: Several stages per action add calculations on every tick, which presses on the engine's CPU tripwire that the other realism additions already strain. core
  • Hard skills are gated three ways together: each skill has a technique threshold and an agility threshold; ball skills such as flicks, volleys, and chips lean on technique while body skills such as overhead kicks, diving headers, and slide tackles lean on agility; and technique decides whether the player tries the skill while agility decides whether the body pulls it off. core
  • The curve that makes the top of the scale matter more acts in both places: on each attribute before the blend, and on the blended result of each stage. core
  • The engine stores attributes in tenths from 1.0 to 20.0, and the screen shows whole numbers from 1 to 20. Why: Development and ageing move smoothly, while the screen keeps the genre's familiar scale. Accepted risk: Two players shown as 15 can differ slightly in play. core
  • A player's overall level follows a normal curve centred on 10 with a spread of about 3.5 on the 1-to-20 scale, wider than the research suggestion of 2.8; single attributes spread wider still, each tier has its own mean, and the largest drop is from the first tier to the second. Why: Tiers overlap more, so a lower-league gem is common. Accepted risk: The world shows more upsets than real football, which presses on the cup upset band and the per-match realism bands. core
  • The strength of the convex curve is set against the five-match target: a top player looks clearly better than an average player in the same role in about two of three runs of five matches. The factor of two for the top gap is only a starting value. Why: The factor of two was picked, not measured, and the five-match target is already the board's measure of felt quality. Accepted risk: The target sets the whole curve, not the top gap alone, so the top gap can end at any ratio the target allows. core
  • Each state acts on its own attributes: tiredness hits the physical attributes first, low morale the mental ones, a knock the physical ones, and sharpness the timing of technical actions. core
  • Match sharpness is its own state that rises with minutes in matches and falls without them, and it also feeds carried fitness and form. core
  • Consistency and injury proneness are hidden values, beside the twelve hidden personality traits; injury proneness replaces the visible injury-resistance attribute. Accepted risk: The manager learns them only by watching and through staff, over seasons. core
  • For the manager's own players, the visible attributes are exact once the player signs; hidden values stay hidden. Accepted risk: Scouting matters only for the market, and a signing shows its true visible numbers on the first day. core
  • A hidden value shows itself to the manager in words, for example 'thrives on big occasions', with a confidence set by the staff, as personality already does. core
  • Every role ships with all three behaviours together: where the player stands in and out of possession, which actions the player prefers, and how teammates treat the player (a target forward draws long balls). Accepted risk: It is the largest piece of work and the most tuning in the data-model area. core
  • A duty (defend, support, attack) scales the role's own behaviour, for example an attacking full-back overlaps more and tracks back less. Accepted risk: Every role needs three tuned variants. core
  • The game offers the full genre list of about 40 roles. Accepted risk: With three duties and three behaviours each, about 360 tuned variants, and many roles differ only slightly. core
  • The data rules of the player (a job for every field, one scale, and a sensitivity check) are examined for four more kinds of data: team-level numbers, the other rated people, relationships and history, and the physical and fixed facts. core
  • Each kind of wider data gets its own data rules, decided as the kind is examined, rather than the player's rules applied unchanged. Accepted risk: Slower, and the kinds may drift apart in scale and in how they are checked. core
  • Team numbers act on actions that need several players at once: pressing chains, the offside line, and combinations between pairs. Why: A team can be more than its players, for example in coordinated pressing. Accepted risk: A second place to tune beside the player layer. core
  • The player layer keeps what is personal, the position rating and the player's grasp of the tactic; partnerships and cohesion move to the team layer and act only on actions that need two or more players. Why: Each quality then counts once. core
  • Club identity acts only off the pitch; it reaches play only through what the club does off it. core
  • On the pitch a club's style comes from two layers, the squad and the tactic. Club identity shapes both from outside by two routes: it shapes who the club hires and buys, and the board and fans expect it, so a manager who plays against it loses standing and the AI manager weighs it in its philosophy. Accepted risk: Two slow loops to tune, and style on the pitch no longer carries a separate identity input for the realism test to check. core
  • A club's identity at the start of a save is seeded from the real club's history and reputation, with a fallback where no source exists. Accepted risk: It needs a data source for the clubs of every active league. core
  • Identity drifts slowly towards how the club actually plays when it wins, and jumps at big events such as a takeover, a legendary manager, relegation, or a trophy. Accepted risk: The most tuning of the three ways identity can change. core
  • When a manager wins against the club's identity, three reactions mix, and each club sets their strength: results buy time, supporter groups split between results and style, and at clubs with a strong identity a winning manager can still lose standing over time. Accepted risk: At some clubs a manager can be sacked while winning, which some players of the genre find unfair. core
  • Where no real history exists, identity is computed from the simulated past seasons the save generates: how the club played and won. core
  • Height sets a player's standing reach in the air, jumping adds to it, and heading decides what happens on contact; tall players also turn a little slower, so height is a trade-off. Accepted risk: Agility and height both touch turning, so the sensitivity test must separate them. core
  • Build is a description such as slight, athletic, or powerful, generated from the attributes, with no effect in the engine; weight is not stored. Accepted risk: Build is a field with no engine job, the kind your brief warns against, although it is shown as a word and derived from attributes that do have jobs. core
  • Besides the age curves, age acts directly: older players recover more slowly between matches, carry more injury risk under congestion, and fade faster late in a match. Accepted risk: Age overlaps stamina, natural fitness, and injury proneness, so the sensitivity test must separate them. core
  • A player new to a country adapts over months: language, climate, and the league's style move the mind family of effective ability, beside the off-pitch effects of nationality. Accepted risk: One more state to track for each player. core
  • A relationship between two people carries two numbers: on-pitch understanding, earned from minutes together, and personal liking, moved by personality, groups, and events; two players can combine well and dislike each other. core
  • The game stores every pair at every club in the active leagues, and none at the light clubs. Accepted risk: Memory and save size grow with the number of active leagues, on top of the full staff and agent records already chosen for them. core
  • Each player keeps every event of recent matches, for replays and deep reports, and match summaries for older ones. Accepted risk: Large saves, which adds to the load on the player's machine that session six already named as the sharpest risk. core
  • Both numbers of a relationship fade when two people are apart, at different speeds. core
  • Every rating in the game, for staff, managers, referees, and agents as well as players, uses the 1-to-20 scale stored in tenths. Why: One scale to learn. Accepted risk: The referee's numbers are sized from published data in the engine's own units, so each must be converted to and from the 1-to-20 scale. core
  • Every staff, manager, and agent rating has a named job, and a season-long sensitivity check runs before each release. Accepted risk: Season-long batches are slow, on top of the match batches at every milestone. core
  • The referee's numbers are hidden: the manager sees a description such as 'strict with cards' before the match and learns more from past matches. core
  • Tactic familiarity lives with the player: each player holds a grasp of each tactic. The team value is computed from the players on the pitch and never stored; actions of one player read the player's grasp, and team actions read the team value. core
  • What the board and the fans expect of a club's style blends club identity with the owner type, and each owner type sets its own weight, for example a state-backed owner weighs its own targets more. Accepted risk: Two inputs to one expectation, with a weight per owner type to tune. core
  • Every overlapping group of attributes has one split. Vision sets which passing options a player sees, decisions how well the player picks among them and when, and anticipation how early the player reads an opponent. Pace is top speed in long runs; acceleration wins short races. Jumping is height and timing, heading is what the player does on contact, and strength holds position in any contact. Aggression is how often and how hard a player commits, with the foul risk; tackling is success once committed. Concentration is rare sudden lapses that grow late in a match; consistency is the hidden everyday spread of each action, within and between matches. Technique shapes how every action is done and which skills a player tries; first touch is control on receipt. Agility is turning and gates hard body skills; balance is staying up and striking off balance. Composure acts on each action under pressure; the pressure trait acts on big occasions. Accepted risk: Lapses are rare, so the sensitivity check needs larger batches to see concentration move. core
  • One effective-ability layer holds four families of temporary state: body (in-match energy, carried fitness, match sharpness, minor knocks), mind (morale, confidence, form), familiarity (the position rating and the player's grasp of the tactic), and surroundings (crowd, pitch, weather). Each family has its own cap, the caps add, and body and mind have the largest, so an 18 has poor patches and hot streaks but never becomes a poor player and a hot player never becomes unstoppable. Partnerships and cohesion sit in the team layer, not here. core
  • The sensitivity test is a test family of its own beside the realism test. Over a large batch it changes one thing at a time and checks that the related statistics move in proportion and unrelated ones stay still: one attribute on one player, one tactical setting, one role against a neutral default, and one team number such as cohesion or a partnership, which must move only team actions. When and how often each part runs is still open. Accepted risk: Two close roles can both pass while playing almost alike, and the batches are the largest part of the check load. core
  • Every one of the 36 attributes keeps a named job in the engine; none is cut or merged. Consistency and injury resistance keep their jobs as hidden values, and injury resistance becomes injury proneness. Accepted risk: Teamwork, work rate, and communication act on the team, so they need team-level mechanisms that do not exist yet. core
  • The convex payoff lives in three places: on the pitch through the curve before the gap, in development where top points cost more to gain, and in the market where value and wages grow about 1.45 times per point of the level a valuer can see, never per point of a true attribute. Accepted risk: Three curves to tune, and their effects compound. core
Findings 12
  • Only 18 of the 36 schema attributes reach a match calculation. The other 18 (crossing, heading, long shots, technique, anticipation, work rate, concentration, teamwork, strength, agility, balance, jumping, handling, aerial reach, throwing, command of area, communication, rushing out) feed only the AI manager's role-fit score, or nothing. crates/engine/src/player.rs:71; crates/engine/src/ai.rs:86; crates/engine/src/rules/mod.rs:730
  • Attribute effects are linear on a 1-to-100 scale: for example, top speed is a base plus a fixed amount times pace divided by 100, so the step from 90 to 100 is worth the same as the step from 50 to 60. crates/engine/src/player.rs:88
  • The engine already keeps a player's base values apart from the effective values play reads, but fatigue is the only thing that lowers the effective values today. crates/engine/src/player.rs:131
  • A role today is a set of attribute weights that the AI manager uses to pick a player for a slot, and a duty is only a depth offset and a risk offset. Neither changes how a player behaves with or without the ball. content/tactics.json:135; content/tactics.json:205; crates/engine/src/ai.rs:86
  • The six team instructions already map to numbers the engine reads, for example pressing sets how many players press and how far they go. content/tactics.json:76
  • Football Manager shows attributes on 1 to 20 and keeps a hidden current ability on 1 to 200. An attribute point costs more of that ability budget above about 17, so top points are scarce because they are expensive, not because the display is curved. https://www.fmscout.com/a-guide-to-current-ability-in-football-manager.html?pg=3
  • Football Manager is reported to move attributes in hidden fractions behind the whole-number display. A young player gains at most about one visible point per attribute per season, and two to three visible points across all attributes; the best tested season was about 11 ability points at age 21 against about 1 at age 28. https://fm-arena.com/thread/2671-player-development-and-training/
  • Across 18,207 FIFA 19 players the overall rating is close to normal (mean 66.2, spread 6.9): 3 percent sit at 80 or above, 0.6 percent at 85 or above, and 14 players at 90 or above. Single attributes spread wider and lean left because of specialists. Market value grows about 1.21 times per rating point. https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/javagarm/fifa-19-complete-player-dataset
  • Club Elo puts the English tiers at about 1839, 1608, 1491, and 1387, so the largest drop is from the first tier to the second (about a 79 percent win expectancy) and the drops shrink further down; elite clubs sit near 2030 to 2046. http://clubelo.com/ENG
  • The world holds about 128,700 professional male players at about 3,990 clubs. https://inside.fifa.com/legal/news/fifa-publishes-professional-football-report-2023
  • No published study shows that underlying skill is heavy-tailed. The honest reading is that skill is close to normal, while what depends on it (value, wages, goals) is convex and right-skewed, as superstar economics predicts; player value models show a thin elite over a dense middle. https://milescorak.com/wp-content/uploads/2018/09/rosen-1981-aer-economics-superstars.pdf; https://tomdecroos.github.io/reports/xt_vs_vaep.pdf
  • A contest decided by the gap between two ratings on a logistic curve, as chess Elo does, gives 20 against 18 the same odds as 12 against 10. To make the top gap matter more on the pitch, each rating must first pass a convex map, for example an exponential, before the gap is taken. Baseball's 20-to-80 scouting scale sets 10 points to one standard deviation and stops at three. https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bradley%E2%80%93Terry_model; https://blogs.fangraphs.com/scouting-explained-the-20-80-scouting-scale/

The in-progress plans and what they miss

exploredproblem side

The engine workflow keeps its charter and sixteen slices. Realism runs as a separate programme after it, with its own performance budget. Core: realism bands are outputs, never tuning targets. Order: test harness, then team shape, then the event contract, then the season layer. Scope: twelve pieces routed to three design documents.

Decisions 11
  • Every realism band is an output that the mechanisms must produce, never a value the engine is tuned to hit directly. The watched-match reviews catch what the bands miss (a review after each new mechanism, where the reviewer compares a simulated match with a real match replayed in the same 2D view, works through a checklist of named qualities, and judges openly what looks wrong), and a failed review reopens the question of whether more detail in the planned model produces football that reads as real. Why: Asked how to settle whether more detail produces real-looking football, the person chose a principle plus the watched review rather than waiting for a proof.
  • The person's thought is three readings at once: the planned model needs more detail inside it, the numbers can pass while the football is still missing, and the realism test is too weak to catch either. Why: Asked which reading of the realism gap was theirs, the person answered with a mix of readings rather than a single one.
  • Every idea on this board belongs to a second workflow, a realism programme started separately from the match-engine workflow already in flight. Why: Form and season context need a season model that the engine workflow excludes, so the ideas are kept out of the build in flight.
  • The distillation produces several candidates split by dependency, one per coherent piece, rather than one large candidate. Why: So the cheapest pieces can start first.
  • The realism work splits into two programmes: engine realism (the player as a person, the club and squad, Tier 3 tactics, conditions and officials, the rebuilt realism test, and both managers as entities) and the season layer that earns form. The engine-facing pieces start while the season layer is still being shaped. Why: The season layer had grown into a full management game with a calendar, cups, a table, and season-long availability, larger than the engine it was meant to follow, so one second workflow no longer fitted.
  • Touchline is one product, a football-management game, and the match engine is its first component; the workflow in flight is stage one of a longer programme rather than the whole thing.
  • The match-engine workflow in flight keeps its charter and its exclusions unchanged. The exclusions describe that workflow correctly even though they no longer describe the product, and splitting the work into an engine-realism programme and a season-layer programme is what keeps both statements true. Why: The exclusions still describe that workflow correctly, and the two-programme split keeps both descriptions true, so the work in flight pays nothing.
  • The engine-realism programme builds team shape and the block first, following the measured law that the team slides with the ball and changes width by phase, so the first watched-match review judges the cheapest mechanism that changes how every match looks. Why: It is the cheapest mechanism that changes how every match looks, so the first review judges something visible early.
  • The season-layer piece of work no longer stays one card: it splits into smaller cards that can each start on their own, and the split is written at the next distillation.
  • Engine realism runs first and the season layer follows, as the decisions to put the realism work in a second workflow and to split it into two programmes already set it, so the four season cards (competitions, squad and careers, the market, and club business) build on a realistic match. Why: So the season cards build on a match that is already realistic.
  • The card that asked when the player record changes shape becomes a recorded decision rather than a card, because the board already answered it: the record changes when the realism programme starts. The card to rebuild the realism test is rewritten at the next distillation to the test the board now holds: bands for per-match rates, scorelines and home advantage, pass completion, and physical output; a separate test-only output with per-player data; one internal match record behind both outputs; a watched-match review after each new mechanism with three comparisons; and bands plus a person's reading for player development and for club finances. Why: The player-record question was already answered, so it needs no investigation run, and the realism-test card no longer matched what the board had decided.
Findings 5
  • The board believed the engine bounced the ball off the touchlines and the goal lines like walls, with no throw-ins, corners, goal kicks, or offside. The code now shows otherwise: the protocol's event list (crates/protocol/src/event.rs) carries throw-in, corner, goal-kick, and offside events. crates/protocol/src/event.rs:25 (throw-in, corner, goal-kick, and offside events now exist)
  • This board runs ahead of the build: the football-manager-match-engine workflow is at the plan and implement stages of its stream-protocol slice, so every candidate here lands as later scope and none corrects work already in flight. .ai/workflows/football-manager-match-engine/00-index.md:9
  • The match-engine build workflow places leagues, seasons, competitions, transfers, and finances out of scope, and it parks injury persistence because no season model exists. .ai/workflows/football-manager-match-engine/02-shape.md:239
  • The stream-protocol slice is complete and four later slices depend on it, so the event contract is a second completed contract, after the player record, that the realism work wants changed. .ai/workflows/football-manager-match-engine/00-index.md:137
  • The engine is done when a person can watch and manage one match from start to finish, exactly as the sixteen-slice roster describes; calling the product a football-management game does not change the engine's definition of done. .ai/workflows/football-manager-match-engine/00-index.md:134

The player as a person

exploredproblem side

attributes, personality, body, positions

A player becomes a person: a rating per position, a rating per foot, new performance attributes, twelve hidden personality traits, and learnable habits. Attribute effects are amplified beyond real sizes so quality is felt over about five matches. Personality is seen through scouting noise. Open: how the attribute and trait overlaps split in the engine.

Decisions 23
  • The new performance attributes are marking, off-ball movement, set-piece skills (corners, free kicks, penalties, and long throws), and flair, which with 36 attributes today stays under the 50-attribute limit. Consistency joins the hidden values. Big-match nerve is left to the pressure-handling trait. core
  • A player carries at least eight personality traits (professionalism, temperament, ambition and loyalty, leadership, determination, adaptability, sportsmanship, and pressure handling), each a hidden value on the 1-to-20 scale stored in tenths, and still more traits are wanted. core
  • Where an attribute and a personality trait overlap, they split by scope: the attribute acts on each action in a match (composure on a shot under pressure, aggression on a tackle), and the trait acts on occasions and choices (a final, a penalty shoot-out, whether to dive or to foul cynically). Why: Otherwise the engine would read two values for one behaviour without saying what each one moves that the other does not.
  • Four more traits join the eight already chosen, for twelve in all: versatility (willingness to play out of position), controversy (trouble off the pitch), money motivation (how much wages decide moves), and bravery (readiness for a 50-50, a header in traffic, or a block).
  • The player record gains a fifth attribute group, personality, with its own slots outside the 50-attribute limit, so the twelve traits stop competing with new performance attributes for the 13 free slots. The record changes when the realism programme starts, as the decision on when the player record changes shape allows. Why: The twelve traits alone would take 12 of the 13 free slots and leave one slot for every new performance attribute.
  • All four absent player fields matter: personality and traits; condition, morale, and form; age, height, and preferred foot; and positional familiarity.
  • A player carries a competence rating for every position the player can fill, not one position code, so picking a lineup is a trade-off rather than slot-filling. Why: Picking a lineup should be a trade-off between players rather than filling slots.
  • The gap between a strong player and a weak one is three things together: fewer errors, a higher ceiling of attempted actions, and more consistency. Why: Any one of the three on its own would read as a flat multiplier.
  • A player carries a mood that the match itself moves, and that mood drives the player's decisions.
  • Personality decides how strongly the player's own mood and the team's mood change that player's decisions; personality is the mediator, not a third mood.
  • Clubs disagree about a player's worth for two reasons at once: each club sees a noisy estimate of the attributes, set by its scouting quality, and each club weighs the attributes differently by its playing identity and its manager's attributes. Why: If every club saw every attribute exactly, no club could value a player differently from another, and the market would have no disagreement to trade on.
  • The player record changes shape when the realism programme starts and not before, following the precedent set for the event contract, which gains its player identifier only when the season-layer work starts. The match-rules and tactics-and-ai slices are therefore built against a record that will change under them. Why: It follows the same timing already chosen for the event contract change.
  • Attribute effects are amplified on purpose beyond the sizes measured in real football, where skill moves pass, shot, and save outcomes only a few points, because a management game must reward a better player. The per-player residual band, which the realism test gained once it could separate the situation from the player, is expected to fail by design. Why: A management game must reward a better player, and an engine faithful to the measured sizes may make attributes feel as though they do not matter.
  • A manager must see that a better player is better over a run of about five matches, through form and match ratings, rather than inside one match or only over a whole season.
  • Consistency narrows the spread of every action, finishing and saving included, but by a different degree for each action. Why: Real finishing and saving barely repeat within a season, so a graded effect makes the departure from real data deliberate rather than accidental.
  • Personality is hidden: the manager sees a description such as professional or volatile, whose accuracy is set by scouting quality and by time at the club, in the same way each club sees only a noisy scouting estimate of a player's attributes.
  • A player carries at least four personality traits (professionalism, temperament, ambition and loyalty, and leadership), and more traits are wanted beyond these four.
  • Each foot carries its own rating, the strong foot is always 100, and two-footedness matters in play but must never become the dominant quality.
  • Four limits keep two-footedness from dominating: a good weaker foot is rare and costs more in the market; the weaker foot costs something only when the ball is on that side or there is no time to shift it; the gap between the feet grows with pressure and distance; and defenders and AI managers show a one-footed player onto the weaker foot.
  • A player's rating for a position rises with minutes played there and with training for it over months, so a manager can retrain a player for a new position.
  • A player carries a few habits of play, such as cutting inside or shooting from distance, separate from attributes and personality, and training and coaching can add or remove a habit.
  • Under pressure or low mood a strong habit can override the manager's instruction, and the manager can see why it happened. This amends the rule that an instruction is always carried out as given: it is carried out as given unless a strong habit overrides it at such a moment. Why: If a habit always yielded, habits would vanish under any instruction; if it always won, the manager's instruction would be weakened.
  • A better player shows in four places: match ratings, statistics, visibly better choices and touches on the pitch, and staff reports whose accuracy depends on the staff's own quality.
Open ideas 1
  • New performance-related and personality-related attributes should be created beyond the 37 the schema was believed to carry at the time.
Findings 17
  • The board believed the attribute schema held 37 attributes in four groups, with no personality attribute, no hidden trait, and no positional-familiarity value. The schema file shows 36 attributes, nine per group, when it was read again in session 5. content/attributes.json:1 (36 attributes, nine per group, at session 5)
  • A player record carries an identifier, a name, a shirt number, one position code, and attributes. It carries no age, no preferred foot, no height, and no starting condition. crates/engine/src/data/team.rs:89
  • A player has exactly one position code, drawn from ten: GK, CB, LB, RB, DM, CM, AM, LW, RW, ST. crates/engine/src/data/team.rs:17
  • The board believed the attribute schema accepts 30 to 50 attributes in four fixed groups with 13 free slots, so any personality attribute would have to be declared in an existing group such as mental. The schema file holds 36 attributes, so 14 slots are free, as read in session 5. content/attributes.json:1 (36 attributes, so 14 slots are free, at session 5)
  • An outfield player covers 9.6 to 10.3 kilometres per match, spends about 70 percent of the time below 2 metres per second, and sprints above 7 metres per second for 0.25 to 0.44 percent of the match, covering 100 to 200 metres at that speed. Measured from two Metrica matches of 25-frames-per-second tracking plus events. .scratch/out/metrica2.json (2 matches, 25 fps tracking plus events)
  • Peak sprint velocity has a narrow spread across a league: the tenth percentile is 26.6 kilometres per hour, the median 28.3, the ninetieth percentile 29.9, and the fastest player 31.8. Measured from 20 A-League matches of 2024/25 SkillCorner tracking. .scratch/data/skillcorner (20 A-League matches 2024/25)
  • Players cover more high-speed distance out of possession than in it, 1052 against 805 metres per 90 minutes for high-speed running and 261 against 212 metres for sprinting, so defending costs more at high intensity than attacking. Measured from 20 A-League matches of 2024/25 SkillCorner tracking. .scratch/data/skillcorner (20 A-League matches 2024/25)
  • Every outfield role covers 26 to 43 metres of the pitch's length between its tenth and ninetieth percentile. Attacking midfielders roam widest laterally at 42.9 metres while full-backs stay narrowest at about 30, and central midfielders sit nearest the ball at 18.7 to 19.6 metres and within 20 metres of it for 56 to 58 percent of the match. Measured from 20 SkillCorner matches, 3.87 million player samples. .scratch/out/sc_player_frames.csv.gz (20 matches, 3.87M samples)
  • Players move about twice as fast within 5 metres of the ball as beyond 40 metres, and attackers show the steepest gradient: a striker runs 3.62 metres per second near the ball and 1.81 far from it, against a centre-back's 2.86 and 1.87. Measured from two Metrica matches at 25 frames per second. .scratch/out/metrica_roles.csv (2 matches at 25 fps)
  • An outfielder covers 10.25 kilometres per ninety minutes on official Bundesliga tracking, and the difference between roles lives in intensity rather than volume: a centre-back covers 84 percent of a wing-back's total distance but only 46 percent of the wing-back's high-intensity distance, 276 metres against 595 per half. Measured from 7 DFL matches, 247 full player-halves at 25 frames per second. .scratch/out/idsse_players.csv (7 DFL matches, 247 full player-halves at 25 fps)
  • A player sprints above 7 metres per second for 0.7 percent of live time and runs below 2 metres per second for 45 percent of it, and no more than about one outfielder in twenty exceeds 5.5 metres per second at any instant, so top speed is an attribute the match almost never uses. Measured from 7 DFL matches, 247 full player-halves at 25 frames per second. .scratch/out/idsse_players.csv (7 DFL matches, 247 full player-halves at 25 fps)
  • Every outfield role runs faster without the ball than with it, 2.49 metres per second against 2.20 for centre-backs and 2.86 against 2.59 for defensive midfielders, so defending costs more running than attacking and costs the deepest roles the most. Measured from 7 DFL matches, 247 full player-halves at 25 frames per second. .scratch/out/idsse_players.csv (7 DFL matches, 247 full player-halves at 25 fps)
  • Pass completion is a property of pitch position rather than of the person: among comparable players in one pair of matches, flat-pass completion runs from 98.6 percent for goalkeepers and 95 for centre-backs down to 73.2 for centre-forwards, so a uniform passing attribute would make defenders too error-prone and forwards too accurate. Measured from 1650 DFL passes joined to tracking at 9 milliseconds. .scratch/out/idsse_sync.csv (1650 passes joined to tracking at 9 ms)
  • The late-match fall in running is dead time rather than tired legs. From minutes 0 to 30 to minutes 60 to 90, distance per minute falls 17 percent and high-speed running per minute 26 percent, but the ball is in play 27 percent less, and high-speed running per minute of live play does not fall at all; forwards are the one exception, at minus 12 percent. Measured from 7 DFL matches with tracking joined to events, recorded in the findings document. .scratch/out/gap_idsse_an.txt (7 DFL matches, tracking joined to events) and FINDINGS.md §16
  • The attribute schema already carries composure, aggression, work rate, teamwork, and injury resistance in its mental and physical groups. content/attributes.json:1
  • Skill is small beside the situation for passing, shooting, and saving, and large for duels. After the situation and binomial noise are removed, players spread by 3.6 points of pass completion against an 18.3-point situational spread, and by 2.5 points of shot conversion and 2.4 of save rate with split-half correlations of only 0.17 and 0.14; but they spread by 10.0 points in aerial duels against a 5.8-point situational spread, and by 7.6 in attacking ground duels. Measured from 1826 Wyscout league matches, recorded in the findings document. .scratch/out/gap_wy.txt (1826 Wyscout league matches) and FINDINGS.md §16
  • About 21 to 27 percent of elite players are left-footed, and players use the dominant foot for about 80 percent of actions. Professionals show no significant success gap between their feet where amateurs do, and two-footed players earned a wage premium of about 20 percent with full controls. No real-data share of two-footed players exists; EA's ratings put about 1.3 to 1.8 percent of its database at the top weak-foot level. Sources: a London School of Economics Centre for Economic Performance paper and a Psychology Today article, found by a research sub-agent. https://cep.lse.ac.uk/pubs/download/cp312.pdf and https://www.psychologytoday.com/us/blog/the-asymmetric-brain/202212/messi-magic-left-footedness-in-professional-soccer-players (research sub-agent, batch 72)

The club and the squad

exploredproblem side

identity, venue, mood, cohesion

A club has a style from identity, squad, and tactic, a home ground, a reputation, a squad familiarity per tactic, and a team mood that personality shapes. Style clash follows the measured additive possession rule. Open: none central.

Decisions 8
  • The club carries a home ground with a home advantage, a reputation and stature, and a playing identity. Tactic familiarity lives with the players, and the team value is derived from them. core
  • A team carries a mood of its own, separate from the mood of each player.
  • Team mood is moved by match events, by the score and the clock together, by the crowd and the venue, and by the context before the match.
  • Team mood is also moved by recent form, by the season so far, and by team context such as a club known for late goals or for slumps.
  • Team mood shifts how players behave inside the chosen tactic and gates the error rate; it does not change the tactic itself.
  • The squad has leadership, partnerships between pairs of players, and a cohesion value; cohesion is measured for the whole squad and again for the eleven on the pitch.
  • The measured additive possession rule is an output, not an input: tactic, squad, and identity drive each match, one match may land far from the rule, and the season results must reproduce it. This amends the earlier reading that applied the rule as each match's default, so the default is now emergent rather than applied. Why: After a worked example, the person read the measured rule as the average: it was measured on real clubs whose managers already chose a tactic for every match, so adding a tactic on top of it would count that adaptation twice.
  • Until the season layer exists, each test match sets form, table position, and the season situation by hand, so the mood mechanism and the AI manager can be tested in extreme states such as a relegation fight. Why: The season layer that would supply these values does not exist yet.
Findings 8
  • A team file today carries a club identifier, a name, a short name, and two kit colours; it carries no reputation, no home ground, and no crowd data, so two clubs differ only in their players and every fixture is played on neutral ground. content/teams/default-a.json:1
  • The share of time the ball is above 1 metre differs between competitions, 12.4 percent in European elite matches against 19.4 percent in the A-League (measured on 20 SkillCorner A-League matches from 2024/25), so ball height is a measurable signal of playing identity rather than a constant. .scratch/data/skillcorner (20 A-League matches 2024/25)
  • Club-to-club style varies far more than league-to-league: across 98 clubs in 1826 Wyscout matches from five leagues, pass accuracy spans 73 to 90 percent against 1.1 percentage points between leagues, pass volume varies 2.57 times, mean pass length spans 17.9 to 23.0 metres, and shot conversion spans 4.9 to 17.5 percent. .scratch/out/wyscout_rates.json (1826 matches, five leagues)
  • Physical output separates neither divisions nor clubs: in seven DFL matches (247 full player-halves tracked at 25 frames per second), Bundesliga outfielders cover 110.0 metres per minute against 115.4 in the second division, top speed and sprint share are identical between the divisions, and across fourteen team-matches the spread is 20 percent, against the 73 to 90 percent club-to-club spread in pass accuracy measured across 98 clubs. .scratch/out/idsse_players.csv (7 DFL matches, 247 full player-halves at 25 fps)
  • When two styles meet, the measured additive possession rule (match possession equals 0.487 plus 0.949 times the difference in the two clubs' season means, plus 0.027 at home) is the default, and the manager's tactic for that match moves the result away from it, so a possession club may choose to give the ball up in one match; the realism test bands the default. The Wyscout data verifies the default rule only; the effect of the tactic is unverified, and the default was later made an emergent output rather than a rule applied to each match. .scratch/out/gap_wy.txt:166 (the default rule only; the tactic effect is unverified)
  • Team strength has a measured size in 1826 Wyscout league matches: the best attack in a league scores about twice the league average and the worst about 0.6 times it, the home factor runs from 1.19 to 1.35, 71 to 84 percent of the spread in points per game is team quality, a top-quarter club beats a bottom-half club 71.0 percent of the time and loses 10.4 percent, and evenly matched games draw 4 to 6 points more often than two independent Poisson scorers predict. .scratch/out/gap_wy.txt (1826 Wyscout league matches) and FINDINGS.md §16
  • Club style has two layers: one axis carries 59 percent of style variance and is mostly strength, with possession correlating 0.74 with points per game, while crosses and take-ons are almost independent of strength. The opponent sets match possession as much as the club does, since match possession equals 0.487 plus 0.949 times the difference in the two clubs' season means plus 0.027 at home, and a club's possession swings 10.3 points from match to match against a 7.2-point spread between clubs (1826 Wyscout league matches). .scratch/out/gap_wy.txt (1826 Wyscout league matches) and FINDINGS.md §16
