  • Quality scales as small execution gaps in every family of action: seven DFL matches show no measurable gap between the Bundesliga and the 2. Bundesliga, while inside one league (1826 Wyscout matches) the top quarter of clubs beats its situational expectation by 1.7 points of pass completion, 2.0 of shot conversion, and 2.5 in duels and take-ons, and the bottom quarter falls short by 1.2, 2.1, and 1.2. .scratch/out/gap_wy.txt (1826 Wyscout league matches) and FINDINGS.md §16; verified .scratch/out/gap_div.txt

Tactics beyond Tier 2

exploredsolution side

phases, transitions, set pieces, live changes

Each team has an in-possession and an out-of-possession shape, set-piece routines, per-player and opposition instructions, and triggers. Live changes wait for the next stoppage, and both managers make them. Team shape and the block follow the measured slide with the ball. Open: the nine real phases against the two chosen.

Decisions 7
  • The tactic has two phases, in possession and out of possession, each with its own shape and its own instructions, so the formation becomes two formations and the switch between them needs timing.
  • Set pieces are designed routines with assigned takers, and the opponent sets a defensive routine against them, so a set piece is a duel rather than a dice roll.
  • Tier 3 is per-player instructions and opposition instructions together, plus manager-set triggers for pressing, the defensive line, and similar situations. The person added the triggers themselves; they were not among the options offered.
  • The manager authors two phases and the engine derives which of the nine paired phases it is in from its own state, so the tactic interface stays at two while the model runs nine. Why: It keeps the tactic interface simple while the model still runs the nine phases that real data shows.
  • A set-piece routine changes four things: who wins first contact, where the ball goes (near post, far post, or short), where players stand for the second ball, and how many stay back against the counter. Why: Real corner data shows a set piece is decided by first contact, so the routine's levers are sized around it.
  • The manager sets two team transition choices, to counter-press or drop back on losing the ball and to counter-attack or keep the ball on winning it, and each player carries an own transition instruction as a Tier 3 per-player instruction, which defaults sensibly from the team choices until the manager changes it.
  • The manager sees the nine derived phases after the match only: the post-match report shows time spent in each phase and how the team did in each, and the match view does not show the current phase.
Findings 14
  • The planned tactics model is Tier 2: a formation, a mentality, six team instructions, and a role and duty for each player, as written in the tactics-and-AI slice of the match-engine workflow. .ai/workflows/football-manager-match-engine/03-slice-tactics-and-ai.md:41
  • Real phase-of-play data uses nine paired phases rather than two: build-up, create, finish, direct, transition, quick break, set play, chaotic, and disruption, each locked one-to-one to the opponent's high block, medium block, low block, and the matching defending phases. Measured on SkillCorner data from 20 A-League matches of 2024/25. .scratch/data/skillcorner (20 A-League matches 2024/25)
  • The two phases differ by a measurable amount: in possession a team is 46 to 49 metres wide with a defensive line 34 to 37 metres up the pitch, and out of possession 36 to 38 metres wide with the line at 31 to 35 metres, while depth barely changes. Measured on Metrica tracking and event data from 2 matches at 25 frames per second. .scratch/out/metrica2.json (2 matches, 25 fps tracking plus events)
  • The shape does not move uniformly between phases: on broadcast tracking from 20 matches (3.87 million samples), when a team wins the ball the back line advances about 3.5 metres while full-backs, wing-backs, and wingers advance 9 to 12 metres, so the team stretches from the back rather than sliding. Ground-truth tracking later halved these magnitudes and reversed the back line's direction. .scratch/out/sc_player_frames.csv.gz (20 matches, 3.87M samples)
  • The block tracks the ball at a fixed ratio, about 0.63 metres up the pitch for every metre the ball advances and 0.30 to 0.39 metres sideways for every metre it moves across, with the same slope in and out of possession; what changes between phases is width, 45.1 metres in possession against 35.4 out of it. Measured on SkillCorner broadcast data from 20 matches (1.76 million team-frames). .scratch/out/sc_team_frames.csv.gz (20 matches, 1.76M team-frames)
  • Only 1.62 of the twenty outfielders stand within 5 metres of the ball and 3.71 within 10, the ten-metre zone is balanced at 1.84 attackers to 1.87 defenders, and 51.5 percent of live frames hold at most one player inside 5 metres, so the ball is uncontested more often than it is contested (2 Bundesliga matches, 154 thousand live frames). These figures were corrected after a position-code fix restored two wingers; the first version read 1.55, 3.55, 1.77 to 1.78, and 54.8 percent. .scratch/out/idsse_crowd.csv (2 Bundesliga matches, 154k live frames)
  • Ground-truth tracking confirms the block law first measured on broadcast data: the team centroid moves 0.558 metres up the pitch per metre of ball advance in possession and 0.555 out of it, and 0.35 and 0.39 sideways, staying within 0.44 to 0.68 across 52 team-halves from 7 German league (DFL) matches, while width changes from 46.1 metres in possession to 36.8 out and depth changes by under 3 metres. .scratch/out/idsse_shape.csv (52 team-halves, 7 DFL matches)
  • Ground-truth tracking corrects the broadcast finding that the back line advances about 3.5 metres and the wide players 9 to 12 metres when a team wins the ball. The ordering survives, with wide midfielders advancing 7.4 metres and full-backs 5.5 against a centre-back's zero, but the magnitudes halve and the back line moves the other way: the deepest outfielder drops 1.4 metres deeper while the centroid advances 2.5, so the team stretches rather than sliding forward (52 team-halves, 7 DFL matches). .scratch/out/idsse_shape.csv (52 team-halves, 7 DFL matches)
  • Both managers decide live: the AI manager changes its tactic during the match, the human can pause and change as well, and the human's pre-set triggers for pressing, the defensive line, and similar situations are an extra tool rather than the only one. The code confirms the protocol already queues a tactics change or a substitution during a match (crates/protocol/src/command.rs); the AI side is not yet verified. crates/protocol/src/command.rs:1 and crates/protocol/src/command.rs:11 (the change queue; the AI side is unverified)
  • Every live change, tactical or a substitution, takes effect at the next stoppage, for both managers; the plan already applies each queued change at a qualifying stoppage through the match-rules slice, so pausing gives the human time to think but no earlier effect. crates/protocol/src/command.rs:2
  • Goals come from both short and long possessions: possessions of 0 to 2 passes give 34 percent of goals and possessions of 10 or more give 25; a ball won in the final third leads to a shot 27.0 percent of the time against 9.7 in the own third; 34.1 percent of the 200 open-play losses a match are regained within 5 seconds; and a SkillCorner quick break leads to a shot 46 percent of the time. Measured on 200 StatsBomb matches and 20 SkillCorner matches. .scratch/out/gap_sb.txt (200 StatsBomb matches) and FINDINGS.md §16; verified .scratch/out/gap_sc.txt (20 SkillCorner matches) and FINDINGS.md §16
  • Pressing has measured triggers and the block a measured shape: a player receiving a forward pass is engaged 88.5 percent of the time against 59.5 after a backward pass, 78 percent when wide against 56 in the centre, and 82 percent against a low block; the defending block keeps a near-constant length of about 30 metres, its last defender advancing 0.49 metres and its midfield line 0.61 per metre of ball advance. Measured on 20 SkillCorner matches and 7 DFL matches with tracking joined to events. .scratch/out/gap_sc.txt (20 SkillCorner matches) and FINDINGS.md §16; verified .scratch/out/gap_idsse_an.txt (7 DFL matches, tracking joined to events) and FINDINGS.md §16
  • Marking tightens near goal and hands over quickly: the nearest defending outfielder stands a median 2.4 metres from an attacker in the box-depth zone against 6.3 metres far from goal, and the nearest defender is still the same player 5 seconds later 64.0 percent of the time overall but only 51.3 percent near goal and 37.8 percent over 10 seconds. Measured on 7 DFL matches with tracking joined to events. .scratch/out/gap_idsse_an.txt (7 DFL matches, tracking joined to events) and FINDINGS.md §16
  • Set pieces are first-contact contests: a corner leads to a goal 3.45 percent of the time and to a shot 38.0 percent, the attacking team wins first contact on 37.2 percent of long corners and then shoots 68.9 percent of the time against 20.5 when the defenders win it, corners, free kicks, and penalties together give 36 percent of goals, and a direct free kick scores 3.2 percent of the time. Measured on 200 StatsBomb matches. .scratch/out/gap_sb.txt (200 StatsBomb matches) and FINDINGS.md §16

How the match plays

exploredsolution side

actions, choices, duels, the ball in the air, officials, injuries

Every action family resolves from the situation first and the player second. The duel is one contested action among several. Play in the air uses the ball height the engine already has. A full referee with a video assistant, and injuries with a severity. Open: three action families have no outcome evidence.

Decisions 18
  • The referee is a full official, with a foul tolerance, a card threshold, how often the referee plays advantage, how consistently the referee applies that threshold, and a leaning towards the home side that the crowd can move. Why: Asked whether the referee is a character, the person took the fullest option: a full official with a home leaning.
  • Pitch condition and weather both exist and they interact, so rain on a worn pitch is worse than either one alone. Why: Asked whether conditions exist, the person took the fullest option: pitch and weather, interacting.
  • An injury carries a severity, and its risk comes from what happens in play (hard tackles, fatigue past a threshold, a collision) rather than from a flat chance per minute. Why: The person took the fullest option over binary injuries; driving risk from hard tackles also finally gives the aggression attribute a real effect.
  • The aerial model supports flight arcs, heading duels, goalkeeper claims, and spin and swerve, so a player's technique and preferred foot change where the ball ends up. Why: The person took the fullest option, using the ball height the engine already carries and that nothing yet uses.
  • The engine build's benchmark tripwire (10 percent CPU and 25 percent memory over the match-rules baseline) guards the engine build against its own baseline and stops there; the realism programme measures against the shipped engine and sets a performance budget of its own. Why: The realism programme should pick a budget it can live in rather than inherit one written for a different purpose.
  • The duel is a first-class contested action with types for ground attacking, ground defending, aerial, loose ball, and tackle, resolved from both players' attributes, their mood, and the ball's height and speed. It was first framed as the single place where player quality becomes an outcome; the person later corrected that part, so the duel is one such place among many. Why: Asked whether the duel becomes a primitive, the person said yes, a first-class contested action; the data had shown the duel is the most common contested action and the engine modelled none.
  • Every action family is a place where player quality becomes an outcome, and the duel is only one of them: passes, through balls, positioning, off-ball movement, dribbles, shooting, goalkeeper actions, blocking and shielding, tackles and duels, and carrying and progressing the ball each need their own resolution. Why: The person corrected the board for treating the duel as the single place quality shows, naming the rest of the game; the data had already shown the pass has a completion surface a duel model does not produce.
  • The gap an attribute makes between players lives in four places at once: in the choice a player makes (the pass picked, the moment to shoot), in the duel, in the body (pace, strength, stamina), and in the repeat, so a strong player also has fewer bad days. Why: Having chosen to amplify attribute effects so a management game rewards a better player, the person placed the gap in all four places.
  • When the realism programme changes the event contract, the contract copies a real provider schema in full rather than a core subset, so the engine's output can feed any tool that reads that schema. Why: The person accepted the true size of the contract change rather than a core subset, so the output could be read by existing tools for that schema.
  • The event contract mixes two providers: it keeps every DFL event kind and attribute and adds what StatsBomb carries that DFL does not, such as the freeze frame of player positions at each shot; this amends the earlier choice to copy a single provider schema in full.
  • The engine keeps one internal record of each match, with one definition per event kind, and both the test-only output and the event contract are written from that record, so they can differ in what they show but never in a fact. Why: The person asked for a worked example first, then chose one record with two views so the realism test can never pass a match whose reported events disagree with what it measured.
  • Where DFL and StatsBomb define the same event kind differently, the DFL definition wins, and StatsBomb only adds what DFL lacks. Why: Providers count the same action very differently, so a merged contract must pick one definition for each shared kind.
  • An in-match knock is handled by all three parties according to severity: the medical staff take a severe injury off at once; for a minor knock the player's personality decides whether to play on, and the manager sees the knock and can overrule at the next stoppage; playing on raises the chance the knock becomes an injury and lowers the player's output. Why: It answers the long-open question of whether a manager may keep a hurt player on the pitch, and the person confirmed this severity split as their reading.
  • A severe injury is carried across seasons more gently than in real football: a long absence, a shorter dip in form, and no career ended by injury, with personality and the medical staff moving a single player's return around that average.
  • Full match simulation covers every match in the leagues the player chose; the simulated past and every other league keep the fast result-only model used at save creation and for unchosen leagues, so the rule that every other match is a full simulation amends neither. Why: Running every match everywhere as a full simulation would cost minutes per season before any realism addition, so the person limited it to the active leagues.
  • The referee's home leaning, the rate of injuries during a match, and the effect of weather and pitch wear are all sized from published data rather than set by design, so the seventh build step needs a research pass before its design targets exist. Why: The person chose data over design where data is missing.
  • The game has a video assistant referee: some goals, penalties, and red cards are checked and overturned, with the delays real football has, and whether a competition uses it can depend on the competition.
  • The event contract uses the game's own names and structure from the start, with a mapping to the DFL and StatsBomb schemas, so no licence can block it. This amends the earlier choices to copy a provider schema and to merge DFL with StatsBomb, so no outside tool reads the output directly, while the rule that DFL wins each definition conflict still decides each definition. Why: Writing the contract in the game's own words removes the licence risk.
Findings 44
  • The tactics-and-ai slice of the engine build carries a benchmark tripwire: it fails if CPU time per match rises more than 10 percent, or memory more than 25 percent, over the match-rules baseline. .ai/workflows/football-manager-match-engine/03-slice-tactics-and-ai.md:73
  • The ball already carries a three-dimensional position and velocity, so height exists in the engine today and an aerial model has somewhere to live. crates/engine/src/ball.rs:9
  • A real match carries about 1683 events. Wyscout types them with 10 types and about 35 subtypes, Metrica with 9 types and subtypes, and kloppy's interoperable model with 19 types (measured over 1826 Wyscout matches from five leagues in 2017/18). .scratch/out/wyscout_rates.json (1826 matches, five leagues 2017/18)
  • Pass completion depends on the kind of pass rather than on one rate: simple passes complete 91.7 percent, headers 62.5, launches 53.8, high passes 53.7, through balls 39.2, and crosses 30.7 percent (1826 Wyscout matches, five leagues, 2017/18). .scratch/out/wyscout_rates.json (1826 matches, five leagues 2017/18)
  • Metrica tracking agrees with the completion-by-pass-kind figures when passes are split by length: under 10 metres 82 to 89 percent, 10 to 25 metres 92 percent, 25 to 40 metres 70 to 72 percent, and over 40 metres 58 percent (2 matches, 25 frames per second tracking plus events). .scratch/out/metrica2.json (2 matches, 25 fps tracking plus events)
  • The duel is the most common contested action in football at 456 a match, 87 of them aerial, and the engine models no duel at all (1826 Wyscout matches, five leagues, 2017/18). .scratch/out/wyscout_rates.json (1826 matches, five leagues 2017/18)
  • The ball sits above 1 metre for 12.4 percent of tracked frames in nine European top-league matches and 19.4 percent in twenty A-League matches, and above 2 metres for 7.6 and 10.6 percent, reaching 12 to 15 metres at its highest (SkillCorner tracking, A-League 2024/25). .scratch/data/skillcorner (20 A-League matches 2024/25)
  • Ball height is standard in tracking data rather than exotic: kloppy (version 3.19.0) types every tracking frame's ball position as a three-dimensional point and fills in the height for Tracab, Sportec, StatsPerform, SkillCorner, PFF, Second Spectrum, Signality, and HawkEye. kloppy v3.19.0 kloppy/domain/models/tracking.py:38
  • The minimum event fields that kloppy and floodlight agree on are the event type, the period and timestamp, the start coordinates and (where the event has an end) the end coordinates, the team identifier, the player identifier, and an outcome. kloppy v3.19.0 kloppy/domain/models/event.py:686
  • The ball is in play for 59 percent of recorded frames, about 55 minutes, and its speed has a median of 5.9 metres per second, a ninetieth percentile of 15.1, and a maximum near 57 (2 Metrica matches, 25 frames per second). .scratch/out/metrica2.json (2 matches, 25 fps tracking plus events)
  • Most possession is brief: a match holds about 475 possession chains, 67.8 percent of them carry no pass or one pass, the median chain lasts 2.9 seconds, and only one chain in 20 ends in a shot (2 Metrica matches). .scratch/out/metrica2.json (2 matches, 25 fps tracking plus events)
  • A player on the ball has an opponent within 5 metres for 56 percent of touches, with the nearest opponent a median 4.4 metres away, and has on average only 1.16 teammates within 10 metres (3366 StatsBomb 360 freeze frames). .scratch/data/sb360/f360.json (3366 freeze frames)
  • High intensity concentrates into attacking sequences rather than spreading across the match: during goal-scoring sequences players average 3.16 metres per second and spend 8.8 percent of the time above 7 metres per second, against a match average of 1.67 and 0.25 to 0.44 percent (19 goal sequences at 20 frames per second, Last Row data). .scratch/data/lastrow (19 goal sequences at 20 fps)
  • Across all passes, pressure appears not to matter, moving completion by only seven points and not in order, because the purpose of a pass is confounded with pressure; restricted to flat passes the curve is monotone and spans twenty points, from 79.8 percent with an opponent inside two metres to 100 percent beyond twelve (1650 passes joined to tracking within 9 milliseconds). .scratch/out/idsse_sync.csv (1650 passes joined to tracking at 9 ms)
  • The duel is the second most common on-ball event at 201.7 a match in DFL data, which reconciles the earlier figure of 456 a match because Wyscout logs one row per participant while DFL logs one per contest; a duel turns the ball over in only 29.4 percent of cases (7 DFL matches, 9961 events). .scratch/out/idsse_events.csv.gz (7 DFL matches, 9961 events)
  • The ground duel is sharply asymmetric: when the ball carrier wins, possession changes hands 6.4 percent of the time, and when the defender wins it changes 51.4 percent, while the aerial duel is nearly symmetric at 31.2 against 36.6 percent, so a model with one win probability and an automatic turnover would roughly double real turnover rates (7 DFL matches). .scratch/out/idsse_events.csv.gz (7 DFL matches, 9961 events)
  • Fouls are an outcome of the duel rather than a separate mechanism: 10.6 percent of duels resolve as a foul, giving 21.4 a match against the 22.9 foul events the schema records, so an engine that models duels obtains fouls, free kicks, and cautions without a separate foul generator (7 DFL matches). .scratch/out/idsse_events.csv.gz (7 DFL matches, 9961 events)
  • The duel looked dominant partly because the DFL schema files several action families inside one element: take-ons at 14.6 a match with 65.7 percent success, shielding as retained control at 17.6, and beaten defenders at 7.3 all live inside the TacklingGame element, so its 201.7 contests a match are a container rather than a single action (7 DFL matches). .scratch/out/idsse_events.csv.gz (7 DFL matches, 9961 events)
  • Evidence now covers the pass, through ball, cross, shot, dribble, duel, shot block, positioning, and aggregate off-ball movement, while three families have no outcome evidence at all: the carry, which no schema read records as an event; the ten off-ball run types that SkillCorner names but nothing has counted; and shielding outside a recorded duel. .scratch/out/idsse_events.csv.gz (7 DFL matches, 9961 events)
  • The full action set is 53 actions, 38 outfield and 15 goalkeeper; public data measures 39 in full and 10 partially, and feints only thinly, and nothing public measures marking and cover assignments, keeper organisation, or keeper dive movement. .scratch/out/ACTIONS.md
  • The carry is measured on three providers: 273 to 287 carries of at least 5 metres a match (corrected from an earlier 271), at about 4 metres per second, with 79.9 percent kept, longer carries faster and riskier, and centre-backs carrying the most because they receive with the most space (7 DFL matches among the sources). .scratch/out/idsse_control_segments.csv and idsse_gk_positions.csv.gz (7 DFL matches)
  • Pressure at the moment of receiving is the common input across actions: from no pressure to very high pressure, possession loss rises from 1.0 to 32.6 percent, pass completion falls from 93.4 to 67.0 percent, and one-touch play rises from 5.6 to 31.3 percent; DFL tracking gives the same gradient from geometry alone, 93.4 percent kept with no opponent inside 10 metres against 60.9 with one inside 1 metre (20 SkillCorner matches). .scratch/out/sc_dynamic_events.csv.gz (20 SkillCorner matches) and ACTIONS.md
  • Off-ball runs happen 495 times a match across ten subtypes, and a run's danger and its completion move in opposite directions: safe runs such as coming short and dropping off are received 91 to 98 percent of the time when targeted and almost never lead to a shot, while runs in behind and runs to receive a cross are received 40 to 52 percent of the time and lead to a shot 20 to 29 percent of the time (20 SkillCorner matches). .scratch/out/sc_dynamic_events.csv.gz (20 SkillCorner matches) and ACTIONS.md
  • A single pressing engagement is credited with a regain only 18.5 percent of the time, yet 80.6 percent of pressing chains end in a regain, and pressing in the opponent's third regains 32.6 percent against 25.4 in midfield, so the value of pressing is collective (200 StatsBomb matches). .scratch/out/sb_events.csv.gz (200 StatsBomb matches) and ACTIONS.md
  • Bodies between the ball and the goal decide shots: with no defender in the shot cone a shot scores 12.4 percent of the time and is blocked 20.9 percent, with two defenders it scores 5.4 and is blocked 40.4, and a shooter has an opponent inside 5 metres 88.2 percent of the time (4671 non-penalty shots with StatsBomb 360 frames). .scratch/out/sb360_actions.csv (4671 non-penalty shots with 360 frames)
  • A keeper's save rate falls with shot quality, from 89.1 percent below 0.05 expected goals to 27.2 percent above 0.4; two saves in three are not held (a fifth go behind and a fifth are parried into danger), and 87 percent of goals are conceded without the keeper touching the ball (200 StatsBomb matches). .scratch/out/sb_events.csv.gz (200 StatsBomb matches) and ACTIONS.md
  • Keeper depth follows a measured rule: out of possession with the ball in the keeper's own half, depth is about 0.23 metres per metre of ball distance from goal, the keeper spends 42 percent of live time beyond the depth of the box, shifts 0.17 metres sideways per metre the ball moves across, and stands a median 2.2 metres off the line at the moment of a shot, where depth has no effect on conversion once shot quality is known (7 DFL matches). .scratch/out/idsse_control_segments.csv and idsse_gk_positions.csv.gz (7 DFL matches)
  • Keepers distribute 66 times a match between them at 73.5 percent completion, with short passes and throws above 97 percent and punts at 29.1; short goal kicks are 45.3 percent of goal kicks and complete at 99.4 percent against 44.1 for long ones, and the 2019 goal-kick law halved the median goal kick from 65.6 metres in 2017/18 to 31.3 metres in 2020 to 2024 (200 StatsBomb matches). .scratch/out/sb_events.csv.gz (200 StatsBomb matches) and ACTIONS.md
  • Dribbles happen 27.4 times a match at 53.0 percent success, success falls up the pitch from 61.5 percent in a team's own third to 48.2 in the final third, and role changes how often a player dribbles but not how often the dribble succeeds: wingers attempt six times as many as centre-backs, at about the same success rate as full-backs and forwards (200 StatsBomb matches). .scratch/out/sb_events.csv.gz (200 StatsBomb matches) and ACTIONS.md
  • Shielding is common but barely coded: event feeds record 1.3 shields a match, while tracking shows about 80 possessions a match held against an opponent inside 2 metres, 10 of them stationary, with about two in three kept (7 DFL matches). .scratch/out/idsse_control_segments.csv and idsse_gk_positions.csv.gz (7 DFL matches)
  • The decision to shoot is a steep threshold at the edge of the box: 87.7 percent of on-ball releases inside 6 metres are shots, 72.1 at 6 to 11 metres, 35.3 at 11 to 16.5, 20.2 at 16.5 to 22, and 6.2 at 22 to 30, and pressure halves the shooting rate outside 11 metres; under pressure the share of releases that are passes falls from 85.5 to 52.9 percent as clearances, dribbles, and dispossessions appear (200 StatsBomb matches). .scratch/out/gap_sb.txt (200 StatsBomb matches) and FINDINGS.md §16
  • Players choose the safe open option more often than the dangerous one: over 70,059 passes with freeze frames, a four-term score (space around the receiver, clearance of the passing lane, distance, and forward progress) picks the real receiver 55.5 percent of the time against 14.9 by chance, and among SkillCorner's tracked options the safest is chosen 45.0 percent of the time and the most threatening 31.7, below the 36.0 chance rate (200 StatsBomb matches and 20 SkillCorner matches). .scratch/out/gap_sb.txt (200 StatsBomb matches) and FINDINGS.md §16; verified .scratch/out/gap_sc.txt (20 SkillCorner matches) and FINDINGS.md §16
  • The match changes with the clock: minutes 75 to 90 produce goals 41 percent faster than minutes 0 to 15 and added time 70 percent faster, passing tempo falls from 10.3 to 8.2 passes a minute and to 7.0 in added time, and cautions rise fivefold (1826 Wyscout league matches). .scratch/out/gap_wy.txt (1826 Wyscout league matches) and FINDINGS.md §16
  • The score changes behaviour once team strength is removed: a team trailing by one goal scores at 1.10 times its own expected rate and a team leading by one at 0.92; the trailing team's possession rises above its expectation from 2 points early to 12 points after the 90th minute; the leading team takes its throw-ins, goal kicks, and free kicks at a median 22.0 seconds against 15.1 for the trailing team; and the team that scores first wins 69.4 percent of the time (1826 Wyscout matches, strength-adjusted, and 7 DFL matches). .scratch/out/gap_wy_state.txt (1826 Wyscout league matches, strength-adjusted) and FINDINGS.md §16; verified .scratch/out/gap_idsse_an.txt (7 DFL matches, tracking joined to events) and FINDINGS.md §16
  • On the ground, local numbers decide duels: with all 1,412 DFL duels joined to tracking, the defender wins 42.7 percent when outnumbered within 5 metres, 44.2 when level, 53.2 with one extra teammate, and 66.7 with two, while in the air support makes no difference once the defender is not outnumbered (7 DFL matches, tracking joined to events). .scratch/out/gap_idsse_an.txt (7 DFL matches, tracking joined to events) and FINDINGS.md §16
  • Loose balls are a real phase: a clearance is next controlled by the clearing team only 26.5 percent of the time, the team of a player who wins a header controls the next ball only 48.0 percent of the time against 39.3 for the loser's team, and 6.2 percent of shots are rebounds within 5 seconds, which convert at 16.4 percent against 11.3 (200 StatsBomb matches). .scratch/out/gap_sb.txt (200 StatsBomb matches) and FINDINGS.md §16
  • The ball is dead for 42.2 minutes a match, and each restart has a measured stoppage: a throw-in takes a median 13.8 seconds, a goal kick 23.2, a corner 31.8, a free kick 32.5, and the kick-off after a goal 68.2 (7 DFL matches, tracking joined to events). .scratch/out/gap_idsse_an.txt (7 DFL matches, tracking joined to events) and FINDINGS.md §16
  • Referees favour the home side measurably: in Serie A 2007-08 matches played behind closed doors, home bias was estimated at 23 percent for fouls, 26 percent for yellow cards, and 70 percent for red cards; Champions League referees gave away teams 25 percent more yellow cards than home teams and Europa League referees 10 percent more, after controlling for dominance. https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S0165176510001497 and https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/abs/10.1080/02640414.2017.1377911 (research sub-agent, batch 72)
  • The crowd drives the referee's leaning: in Bundesliga matches without spectators during COVID-19, the home team's foul advantage of about 0.6 a match reversed to about one foul against, home cards rose significantly, the bias scales with crowd size, and home advantage in results shrank but did not vanish. https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0165176520303815 and https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8011816/ (research sub-agent, batch 72)
  • Added time leans towards the home side: referees add about 44 seconds less when the home team leads by one goal than when the away team does, about 60 seconds less than an unbiased benchmark with fans present, and the gap disappears without a crowd (from a study summary only). https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S2773161825000011 (summary only) (research sub-agent, batch 72)
  • A video assistant referee overturns a decision about once every three to four matches: 109 overturns in the 2019-20 Premier League season from about 2,400 checks, and 123 in 2020-21, split into 34 goals awarded, 42 disallowed, 29 penalties awarded, and 22 penalties overturned, with an average delay of about 50 seconds per review; LaLiga reviews about 12.55 incidents a match. https://global.espn.com/football/story/_/id/37587139 and https://www.espn.com/soccer/story/_/id/37501924 (research sub-agent, batch 72)
  • Elite match injury incidence is about 21 injuries per 1000 match hours in 2022-23, down from 27.5 in 2001 to 2008, which is roughly one match injury per team every two to three matches; hamstring injuries are 24 percent of all injuries and rising. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12406901/ and https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9985757/ (research sub-agent, batch 72)
  • Injury risk rises late in each half, with fixture congestion (5 of 8 studies find more match injuries with 4 days or fewer of rest, and muscle injuries rise within 5 days of the previous match), and with age, at an odds ratio of about 1.1 a year and 1.4 a year for hamstring strains. https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC9758680/ and https://doi.org/10.1177/0363546503258912 (research sub-agent, batch 72)
  • Conditions are measured only in part: altitude moves the expected goal difference by about half a goal per 1000 metres of difference, heat cuts running distance sharply (about 26 percent at 43 degrees against 21 degrees, weakly sourced), artificial turf shifts the injury mix toward ankle sprains (4.83 against 2.66 per 1000 match hours) without raising elite injury totals, and no source quantifies rain, wind, or pitch wear. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/18156225/ and https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC10139885/ (research sub-agent, batch 72)

The realism test

exploredsolution side

bands, design targets, watched reviews

The test bands measured per-match rates, scorelines, pass completion by kind, and joint spreads, with a real target and a design target where the game departs on purpose. People watch matches and seasons at milestones. StatsBomb and Transfermarkt stay, with the licence risk accepted. Open: none central.

Decisions 22
  • The realism test adds shape measures to the three aggregate bands: pass completion, where shots come from, possession in each third of the pitch, and goals by phase of play. Why: Three bands are not enough; with shape measures that the event stream already carries, a match that passes the bands and still looks wrong now fails something.
  • Calibration groups runs by condition (dry, wet, worn) and sets a band for each, so rain is expected to lower goals rather than to fail the test. Why: Pitch wear and weather interact, so the bands either had to hold across every combination of conditions or calibration would fix the conditions and never test them; a band per condition lets a condition move the numbers without failing the test.
  • The realism test reaches the season as well as the match: the shape of the league table, the shape of the market and of wages, and a watched season that a human reads and judges. Why: Transfers, wages, and budgets are the largest part of the product, and nothing tested whether a market, a wage bill, or a season of results reads as football; the person took every season measure offered.
  • A human watches matches as part of the realism test, as a milestone review rather than as a gate on every build. Why: The argument that justifies a person watching a season also justifies watching a match, and a watched match is the first instrument that can check whether a fully detailed model actually reads as football.
  • The realism test bands the spread as well as the average: how often a match finishes goalless, how often it finishes five-one, and how far a team's worst performance sits from its best. Why: Weak players swinging more and mood moving decisions both widen match-to-match variance, so the test has to measure the spread those mechanisms actually add.
  • Mood, personality, and player quality change a team's scoring rate within a match and never the spread around it, so goals stay Poisson given that rate; the rule that a strong player differs from a weak one by fewer errors, a higher ceiling, and more consistency, and the rule that personality decides how strongly mood changes decisions, are both mechanisms for the rate rather than for the variance. Why: Real football's goal distribution is already Poisson, so the observed variance is fully explained by the scoring rate; extra variance would move the engine away from real football rather than towards it.
  • The realism test bands four things: the measured per-match rates across five leagues, the scoreline distribution and home advantage, pass completion as a curve by kind of pass and by pass length, and the physical output of players (distance covered, time at each speed, and peak sprint speed). Why: The planned three bands are several times wider than the real spread between leagues, so they would pass an engine that is visibly wrong; the person took all four families.
  • The best player's season totals, such as the top scorer's goals and the most assists, stay inside the range real leagues produce, so the season realism test bands them. Why: It caps the deliberate amplification of player effects from the season side.
  • The realism test reads a separate, test-only output in which the engine writes per-player data; the event contract that the game reads stays as planned, and the rule that the contract gains its player identifier only when the season-layer work starts still holds. Why: The chosen bands need per-player data that the planned event contract does not carry, and a separate output lets the test run against the engine that ships without bringing the contract change forward.
  • The realism bands measured with StatsBomb definitions are converted to DFL definitions, one mapping per shared event kind, and the 7 public DFL matches check each conversion. Why: DFL definitions win for every shared event kind, but the public DFL set is only 7 matches, too few to measure most bands on its own; converting keeps the large StatsBomb sample of 200 matches.
  • The first watched-match review runs as soon as the first new mechanism runs, for example the choice model for when to shoot and which pass to pick, and a review follows each mechanism after it, so a failed mechanism is caught before other work builds on it. Why: A review run by hand only helps if it catches a failed mechanism before the rest of the model is built on it.
  • The watched-match reviewer uses all three comparisons: a simulated match side by side with a real match replayed in the same 2D view, a checklist of named qualities (shape, tempo, decisions, set pieces), and open judgement of what looks wrong.
  • The realism test checks player development two ways: it bands what the research measured (peak age by position, speed of decline, conversion of youth players, and the after-effects of injury), and a person reads a set of simulated careers for what no band covers. Why: The person asked for the real data first, then chose to band what was measured and add watched careers, the same pattern as the season test.
  • The realism test checks finances two ways: it bands what the research measured (the revenue mix, the spread of broadcast income, league wage ratios, losses, transfer flows, and prize money), and a person reads a set of simulated club accounts for what no band covers. Why: The person asked for the real data first, then chose to band what was measured and add watched accounts, the same pattern as the career test.
  • The career test's injury band is re-sized on purpose to a softer target, and the game is banded against that target rather than against the real after-effects of a severe knee injury (a mean 216 days out, two seasons below par, and 20 percent of professional careers ended within three seasons). Why: The game deliberately carries severe injuries more gently than real football, with a shorter dip in form and no career ended by injury, so a band on real after-effects would fail by design.
  • Every band that departs from real football on purpose carries two numbers, the real target and a design target; the test passes on the design target and reports the distance from real data beside it, and this applies alike to the per-player band broken by the deliberate amplification of player effects, to the band for how consistency narrows each action, and to the softer injury band. Why: Otherwise a reader of the test would see one deliberate departure as a red failure and another as a green pass, with nothing to say that both are choices.
  • Only build steps that change play get a watched review, so the first review follows team shape and the defensive block, and the test harness and the player record are checked by numbers alone; this answers the ninth open question of the engine realism design document.
  • The design target for the deliberate amplification of player effects is that a top player looks clearly better than an average player in the same role in about two runs of five matches out of three, close to real football, so a manager needs more than one run to judge; this sets the size for the rule that a manager sees a better player over a run of about five matches. Why: It stays close to real football while still letting a manager see quality over a few runs.
  • The usual top scorer and top assister stay inside the real range, and a record season beyond it is possible about as rarely as real record seasons happen; this sets how strict the limit on the best player's season totals is, and the real range was still unmeasured when this was chosen.
  • The licence risk of the StatsBomb and Transfermarkt sources is accepted: the realism test keeps its current sources, because it uses derived statistics and the game ships none of the data. Why: You are not worried about the risk, so no band is measured again on other data.
  • The realism test bands the cup upset rate: how often a lower-division club beats a higher one, by the gap in divisions, measured from openfootball and Wikipedia results. Why: A cup tie is a match, so the match bands already cover play; what cups add is the upset.
  • The realism test reaches the world off the pitch: it bands the measured off-pitch facts, such as sackings, manager tenure, the share of free transfers, and agent fees, and gives every design value a design target that a test checks, such as transfer requests per season. Why: A growing part of the game rests on design values, and a design value with no target is never checked.
Open ideas 1
  • Each band records the data set it was measured on, so a band can be measured again on another set later at low cost if the licence position ever changes.
Findings 15
  • The realism test the engine plan already holds is three aggregate bands: goals per match from 2.4 to 3.2, shots per team from 8 to 16, and possession from 35 to 65 percent. .ai/workflows/football-manager-match-engine/03-slice-calibration.md:33
  • Across 1826 matches in five top leagues in 2017/18, goals per match average 2.709 with a standard deviation of 1.675, and the distribution of total goals is Poisson to within 1.5 percentage points at every count, with a variance-to-mean ratio of 1.035. .scratch/out/wyscout_rates.json (1826 matches, five leagues 2017/18)
  • Home teams score 1.530 goals a match and away teams 1.179, a home advantage of 0.351 goals, and results split 45.3 percent home wins, 24.5 percent draws, and 30.2 percent away wins (1826 matches, five leagues, 2017/18). .scratch/out/wyscout_rates.json (1826 matches, five leagues 2017/18)
  • Per-match rates are tight across five different leagues: passes 839 to 888, pass accuracy 82.2 to 83.3 percent, shots 21.0 to 23.2, shot conversion 9.7 to 11.1 percent, duels 442 to 471, aerial duels 76 to 99, fouls 21 to 29, yellow cards 3.1 to 4.9, throw-ins 42 to 46, corners 9.5 to 10.3, goal kicks 16 to 17, free kicks 19 to 27, and offsides 4.0 to 5.0. .scratch/out/wyscout_rates.json (1826 matches, five leagues 2017/18)
  • Shot conversion halves roughly every 5 metres: 51.9 percent inside 6 metres, 21.6 percent at 6 to 11, 12.2 percent at 11 to 16.5, 6.3 percent at 16.5 to 22, 2.8 percent at 22 to 30, and 1.8 percent beyond 30, measured over 40461 shots. .scratch/out/wyscout_rates.json (1826 matches, five leagues)
  • In provider-labelled tracking over 14 halves, the ball sits above half a metre for 29.6 percent of live play, above one metre for 23.9 percent, and above two metres for 17.1 percent, and it is in play for 55.4 percent of the match, so a strictly two-dimensional engine is wrong about a sixth of the live game. .scratch/out/idsse_ball.csv (14 ball halves, provider-labelled)
  • Measured ball speed runs to a median of 6.47 metres per second, a ninety-ninth percentile of 24.9, and an extreme of 49.9, which confirms the earlier discard of a 57 metres per second reading as tracking noise and gives the engine a real speed ceiling in its place. .scratch/out/idsse_ball.csv (14 ball halves, provider-labelled)
  • The official DFL live schema carries 31 event kinds and 139 distinct attributes at 1423 events a match, against the engine's four kinds carrying no player, no coordinates, and no outcome; the earlier estimate that the contract change means about fifteen event kinds plus a player, coordinates, and an outcome understates the gap by half on kinds and by a factor of forty on fields (7 DFL matches, 9961 events). .scratch/out/idsse_events.csv.gz (7 DFL matches, 9961 events)
  • Whether a pass leaves the ground matters more than how far it travels: flat passes complete at 90.2 percent and high passes at 53.4, and a flat long pass completes at 68.6 percent while a high medium pass completes at 52.0, so the engine should branch on pass height before pass length. .scratch/out/idsse_events.csv.gz (7 DFL matches, 9961 events)
  • Only about a third of shots reach the goalkeeper, because 38.4 percent go wide and 22.6 percent are blocked by a defender first, so a shot model that resolves attacker against goalkeeper models the minority case. .scratch/out/idsse_events.csv.gz (7 DFL matches, 9961 events)
  • Data providers count the same action very differently: duels run at 64, 202, or 226 a match and through balls at 4.6, 15.6, or 44.1 depending on the provider, while providers that share a definition agree tightly, with carries of at least 5 metres at 287 against 273 (corrected from an earlier 271) and possession duration at 1.20 against 1.16 seconds; so every realism band must name the definition it copies. .scratch/out/ACTIONS.md
  • The stream crate already serves a recorded fixture over the same protocol the viewer reads, at 50 ticks a second, so a real match converted into a fixture can play in the viewer beside a simulated one. crates/stream/src/replay.rs:1 and crates/stream/src/replay.rs:14
  • The realism test has measured joint bands: goals are Poisson once the club is known, with a within-club variance-to-mean ratio of 0.99, but shots are over-dispersed at 1.64 and crosses at 3.03; one team's shots run from 6 to 17 between the 10th and 90th percentiles, shots and goals correlate at only 0.32, and home and away shot counts correlate at minus 0.34 (1826 Wyscout league matches). .scratch/out/gap_wy.txt (1826 Wyscout league matches) and FINDINGS.md §16
  • Over about 20 seasons the league top scorer's goals run from 19 to 36 in the Premier League, 21 to 36 in Serie A, 16 to 41 in the Bundesliga, and 15 to 38 in Ligue 1, with medians of about 26 to 29, and LaLiga holds the outlier at Messi's 50 in 2011-12; the Premier League assist record is 21, and no source gives the tenth-best scorer's total. https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capocannoniere and https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Bundesliga_top_scorers_by_season and https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Premier_League_Golden_Boot (research sub-agent, batch 72)
  • The calibration sources carry different licences: the IDSSE DFL set and the Wyscout public set are CC-BY 4.0 and SkillCorner's open data is MIT, all usable commercially with attribution, while StatsBomb's open data is framed for research under a user agreement, Metrica's sample data has no formal licence, and the CC0 Transfermarkt dataset is scraped data that Transfermarkt's own terms and the EU database right may still cover. https://github.com/spoho-datascience/idsse-data and https://figshare.com/collections/Soccer_match_event_dataset/4415000 and https://github.com/SkillCorner/opendata/blob/master/LICENSE and https://github.com/statsbomb/open-data (research sub-agent, batch 72)

The manager as a modelled entity

exploredproblem side

Both managers are entities with attributes. The human's tactical attribute moves information accuracy, not instructions. The AI manager picks tactics from philosophy, opponent, match state, and season situation. Open: none central.

Decisions 8
  • The manager is an entity with attributes of their own, and those attributes affect the team's form and mood.
  • Both managers carry attributes, and a manager weak at tactics has their instructions land weaker on the pitch, so the human player's own competence becomes part of the simulation. This mechanism was later replaced by the rule that an instruction is always carried out as given, with a weak tactician making poor choices instead. Why: The person wanted both managers to carry attributes that matter, so that the player's own competence is part of the simulation.
  • An AI manager picks each match tactic from four inputs: its own philosophy, the opponent's strength and style, the match state by score and clock, and the season situation such as table position, a cup match, or a tired squad.
  • No instruction lands weaker because of the manager; a manager weak at tactics makes poor tactical choices and understands the match poorly instead. This replaces the earlier mechanism in which a weak manager's instructions landed weaker, and the instruction a manager gives is always carried out as given. Why: The person corrected the earlier mechanism: there are no weak instructions, only poor choices, because a player told that their own instructions land weaker would read it as the game ignoring them rather than as realism.
  • The human manager's tactical attribute acts on three things and never on an instruction: how accurate the manager's information is (opposition reports, match analysis, and scouting noise, the same noisy estimate of attributes a club's scouting produces), how fast the squad learns a new tactic (the squad's familiarity value for each tactic), and how the squad's mood and trust respond to the manager's choices over time. Why: The human player makes the human manager's choices, so once a weak tactician meant poor choices, the human's tactical attribute had nothing left to act on; the person chose these three things for it.
  • A squad that distrusts a weak manager makes more errors after the same instruction, and that is mood rather than a weakened instruction: the instruction is carried out as given and the players carry it out worse, so the rule that an instruction is always carried out as given stays true. Why: The person judged it acceptable because the extra errors come from mood, not from the instruction itself.
  • The manager gives team talks before the match, at half time, and at full time, and the words move the players' mood and focus. Why: Team talks are one of the manager's most direct levers, and no session had named them.
  • A team talk feels real when the right words depend on the score, the mood, and each player's personality; when repeating one approach loses force and a rare outburst lands harder; when the manager can speak to single players; and when a respected manager gets more from the same words. Why: A talk that is the same pick every time is a routine, not a lever.
Findings 1
  • Substitutions follow the score: across 1826 Wyscout league matches in 2017/18 a team makes 2.85 changes per match at median minutes 61, 72, and 82, a team that trails makes its first change at minute 57 against 66 for a team that leads, midfielders are half of the players taken off, and only 6.5 percent of changes happen at half time. .scratch/out/gap_wy.txt (1826 Wyscout league matches) and FINDINGS.md §16

The season layer

exploredsolution side

competitions, careers, market, club business

The season layer mirrors the European structure, with careers on per-attribute age curves, a market valued from visible data only, and a club business with a board and an owner. The fast model must pass the same season bands. Open: how development effects are sized, since no public data sizes them.

Decisions 31
  • A season layer comes into scope: fixtures, results, and form are modelled properly, so this board describes a product larger than the match engine. Why: Form and season context cannot be supplied as asserted numbers in a team file; they need a season model behind them, so they are earned rather than asserted.
  • The season layer contains fixtures and results, form, a league table with standings, season-long squad availability with suspensions, injury persistence, and carried fitness, and a full club calendar with cups, fixture congestion, and rotation.
  • Form is computed at two scopes, a team form and a per-player form, and the per-player form feeds the player's mood, the mood that the match itself moves and that drives the player's decisions.
  • A new save generates a synthetic backstory of past seasons, so partnerships, form, and club reputation start at believable values in the first match. Why: Partnerships between players cannot be generated for a squad without a shared history to derive them from, so the season layer supplies that history.
  • The event contract gains its player identifier when the work to build the season layer starts and not before; the engine ships on the contract it has, and the realism programme pays the migration across every part of the build that reads the event stream by then. Why: The stream-protocol part of the engine build was already complete with other parts depending on it, and changing the contract later was judged cheaper than disturbing a complete piece of work now.
  • The generated backstory is one past season deep: a form record, a final table position, and partnerships earned from minutes played together. This was later replaced by several fully simulated past seasons.
  • The season layer includes transfers and finances, with a market, budgets, and wages, so the product is a full football-management game rather than a match engine with a season around it.
  • A player's ability changes over seasons along age curves, one per attribute, and the curves differ from player to player with the variance real players show in how they develop. Many other factors move the curves, and which factors they are is still open.
  • The manager runs a training plan between matches, setting focus and intensity, and training moves fitness, injury risk, the squad's familiarity with each tactic, and player development.
  • Five factors move a player's age curves: playing time and the level played at, training and coaching, injuries, personality, and moments. The list stays open, so other factors may be added later.
  • Moments are of two kinds and both move a player's curve: match moments such as a debut, a decisive goal, or a costly error in a final, and career turning points such as a transfer, a loan, a new manager, or a first-team breakthrough.
  • A new save simulates several past seasons in full at save creation, so every player's current ability, partnerships, and reputation agree with a simulated past. This replaces the earlier plan of a backstory only one past season deep. Why: One past season was judged not enough history for a partnership to read as earned.
  • The past seasons simulated at save creation run a fast result-only model instead of the full match, so save creation stays short while the present runs the full engine. Why: Running every past match on the full engine would make save creation too slow; a single league season already takes minutes on one core.
  • A loan's effect varies widely with the club, the coach, and the minutes, and it can help or hurt, while the average effect across loans stays near zero, as the Bundesliga loan study measures. Why: It keeps the only measured evidence on loans true while still letting a single loan matter to a player's career.
  • Each club has a board and an owner type, for example patient, ambitious, or frugal, that sets targets for league position, cups, finances, and style, sets how much patience the board has, and can sack the manager; sackings apply to AI managers as well.
  • A transfer fee is a shared market valuation plus a premium from each club's own view, and the season test bands the resulting fees against real fees and valuations by age and position from the public Transfermarkt career dataset.
  • The shared market valuation comes only from what every club can see (age, minutes, level, goals, recent fees, and reputation), as a Transfermarkt value does, and it never reads the true attributes. Why: A valuation computed from the true attributes would tell every club the truth that scouting noise hides, and clubs would stop disagreeing about a player.
  • A sacked human manager keeps the save: the manager is unemployed, can apply for jobs, and receives offers based on reputation.
  • The player chooses at save creation which countries and divisions are active, so more leagues give more jobs and more clubs to deal with, at the cost of a slower season.
  • A club has four income sources: matchday income from stadium size, prices, and attendance; broadcast and prize money by league position and cup progress; commercial income from reputation and success; and owner money that the club's owner type can put in or take out within limits.
  • Every league the player did not choose runs the fast result-only model used for the simulated past, so its players play, develop, and gain value, and the whole world moves while only the chosen leagues run the full engine.
  • Player-sale income enters the club's finances, and the club's owner type decides how much of it reaches the manager's budgets and how much pays debts or wages. Why: Real selling clubs earn a large share of their income from player sales, and with only four income sources a selling club in the game could not earn what a real one does.
  • Leagues and competitions carry financial rules such as a squad-cost cap, and a breach brings a sanction: a fine, a transfer ban, or a points deduction.
  • The work to build the season layer splits into four cards: competitions (table, cups, calendar, congestion, suspensions), squad and careers (form, availability, training, development, the simulated past), the market (scouting, valuation, fees, transfers, loans), and club business (board and owner, income, wages, financial rules, sacking, the job market). Why: The season layer had grown too large for one piece of work, and smaller cards can each start on their own.
  • A later version of the game loads a real-life database whose players start at the current real season, possibly imported by the player, so the world must accept imported seasons; until then the player chooses how many past seasons a new save simulates.
  • Leagues, cups, and competitions mirror the real European structure in full, starting with the leagues of every UEFA member association, with their divisions, promotion and relegation, domestic cups, and European qualification.
  • AI clubs get the effect of training from their staff quality and their manager, with no day-by-day plan; only the player's club runs the detailed training plan the manager sets between matches.
  • Manager sacking rates and board patience are sized from published data rather than set by design. Why: The person asked for it to be looked up, and then for every open data gap to be researched before the board continued.
  • Creating a new save may take a few minutes, with a progress view that shows the past seasons as they finish. Why: History depth and every UEFA league are worth a short wait at the start.
  • The money bands and the game's accounts use euros, and the player picks the currency the screens show. Why: One currency inside removes the pounds-and-euros mix from every band, and the player still sees a familiar currency.
  • Clubs can fall into crisis: takeovers, owners who run out of money, administration, and points deductions. Why: Real clubs collapse and change hands, and a long save needs that risk.
Open ideas 2
  • The club calendar and season-long squad availability are one piece, because fixture congestion and rotation matter only when fitness and suspensions carry between matches.
  • Over a long save the rules of football change, as they do in real life, for example FIFA's 2027 transfer rules, foreign-player limits, and competition formats. You did not choose this when it was offered.
Findings 22
  • The board believed the match event carried only a team identifier, a score, and four event types (kick-off, goal, full-time, and tactics-change) and no player identifier. The code by the fifth session shows 17 event types and an optional player identifier, in the protocol crate's event definition. crates/protocol/src/event.rs:16 and crates/protocol/src/event.rs:146 (17 event types and an optional player identifier, at session 5)
  • Today's engine plays one match in 387 milliseconds of wall time on one core of an AMD Ryzen 7 9800X3D, so one 20-club league season of 380 matches takes about 147 seconds on one core and about 20 seconds on eight, before any realism addition makes a match more expensive. The match time is measured by the engine benchmark; the season figures are arithmetic. .ai/workflows/football-manager-match-engine/bench-baseline/viewer-pitch/bench-1.stdout.txt:6 (bench.match_wall_ms 387; the season figures are arithmetic)
  • The largest public career dataset is derived from Transfermarkt: 50,000 players, 65 competitions, 1.89 million appearances with minutes, 650,000 market valuations, and 175,000 transfers, released under CC0. It measures playing time, level, moves, and market judgement, and it carries no ratings. https://github.com/dcaribou/transfermarkt-datasets (research sub-agent, batch 43)
  • Performance peaks at about 27.4 across 15,591 top-five-league player-seasons, at 26 to 27 for wingers and full-backs and at 27.5 to 28 for centre-backs and strikers. Wingers fall to half their peak by 31 and centre-backs by 35.2, and an 18-year-old in a top league plays at 50 to 85 percent of the eventual peak. https://macro-football.com/other/aging/ (research sub-agent, batch 43)
  • Physical output has its own curves: across 5,203 GPS match performances speed peaks at 25.7, endurance at 24.8, and explosiveness at 26.0, and high-intensity output falls after 32 while endurance holds. Creative passing holds into the early thirties while shooting and tackling fade. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12551122/ (abstract) and https://www.espn.com/soccer/story/_/id/37467220/ (research sub-agent, batch 43)
  • Of about 200 LaLiga academy players aged 13 to 18, 4 percent reached the top tier within ten years. Across 378 Bundesliga loans, loanees gained no measurable minutes and no measurable market value compared with 6,162 players who were not loaned. https://www.essex.ac.uk/news/2024/06/27/just-four-per-cent-of-teenage-academy-players-make-it-to-top-tier-football and https://ideas.repec.org/p/zbw/umiodp/295740.html (research sub-agent, batch 43)
  • After an ACL rupture 80 percent of elite players return, after a mean of 216 days; they stay below matched controls for two seasons, with attackers declining further, 20 percent of professionals see their careers end within three seasons, and only 47.5 percent keep their playing level. https://doi.org/10.1177/23259671211008892 and https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9859836/ (abstracts; research sub-agent, batch 43)
  • No public data records true attribute histories, training or coaching effects at scale, personality across a career, or the causal effect of a moment. The only attribute-style history is the FIFA 15 to 23 rating set, which is rated by people rather than measured. https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/stefanoleone992/fifa-23-complete-player-dataset (research sub-agent, batch 43)
  • The revenue mix is measured: the top 20 clubs earn 43 percent from commercial income, 38 percent from broadcast, and 19 percent from matchday. The top ten lean on commercial income at 48 percent while clubs ranked 11 to 20 lean on broadcast at 49 percent, and the Premier League as a whole takes about half of its 6.8 billion pounds from broadcast (Deloitte Football Money League and Annual Review). https://www.deloitte.com/uk/en/services/consulting-financial/analysis/deloitte-football-money-league.html and the Deloitte Annual Review pages (research sub-agent, batch 49)
  • In a selling league player sales are a main income source: across nine Eredivisie clubs in 2022/23 they were 29.5 percent of income on average and about 44 percent at Ajax and PSV. https://offthepitch.com/a/player-sales-lifeline-dutch-football-financial-divide-widens (research sub-agent, batch 49)
  • Revenue spread inside a league is set largely by broadcast rules: Premier League central payments run 1.6 to 1 from bottom to top with about 2.7 million pounds of merit pay per place, Bundesliga TV money runs 2.7 to 1, two clubs take 52 percent of LaLiga revenue, and Eredivisie revenue runs about 10 to 1. https://www.football365.com/news/2025-26-premier-league-prize-money-table-tv and https://bulinews.com/bundesliga-money-distribution-for-2025/26-season-how-much-club-get (research sub-agent, batch 49)
  • Wages run at 54 to 80 percent of revenue across the big-five leagues (Bundesliga 54, LaLiga 60, Premier League 65, Serie A 66, Ligue 1 80), and UEFA's squad-cost rule caps wages, transfer costs, and agent fees at 70 percent of revenue from 2025/26. the Deloitte Annual Review pages and https://www.uefa.com/news-media/news/0274-14da0ce4535d-fa5b130ae9b6-1000--explainer-uefa-s-new-financial-sustainability-regulations/ (research sub-agent, batch 49)
  • Losses are normal and owners cover them: European top-division clubs lost 1.1 billion euros before tax in 2024, only 8 Premier League clubs made an operating profit, and Championship clubs lost 355 million pounds with one club in operating profit. Owner-funding totals are only a proxy built from club examples. https://www.uefa.com/news-media/news/02a2-200452a66064-0cfd3f86b94f-1000--new-report-highlights-record-revenues-and-increasing-inv/ and https://theesk.org/2025/07/19/15922/ (research sub-agent, batch 49)
  • The richest league buys and the others sell: in summer 2025 Premier League clubs spent 3.0 billion pounds gross, about 44 percent of league revenue and 51 percent of all big-five gross spending, while the Bundesliga and Ligue 1 were net sellers by 180 and 305 million euros. https://www.deloitte.com/uk/en/about/press-room/deloitte-premier-league-spending-record-eclipsed.html (research sub-agent, batch 49)
  • Prize money is published in full: the Champions League pays 18.62 million euros for the league phase, 2.1 million per win, and rising round payments up to 18.5 million for the final, from a 2.467 billion euro pot, against 565 million for the Europa League. https://www.beinsports.com/en-us/soccer/uefa-champions-league/articles/uefa-confirms-prize-distribution-for-2025-26-champions-league-2025-08-27 (research sub-agent, batch 49)
  • Managers are sacked often and sacking does not work: the median top-flight tenure is about 243 days, 47 English managers were dismissed in 2014-15 and 51 in 2025-26, and across 331 mid-season changes in the top two divisions of five countries from 2017-18 to 2021-22, points collapsed before a dismissal while expected points stayed stable, and the new manager's effect against matched controls was indistinguishable from zero. https://football-observatory.com/Coaches-average-tenure-duration-global-analysis and https://doi.org/10.1080/02640414.2026.2698238 (research sub-agent, batch 72)
  • A sacking follows results against expectation rather than underlying performance: a run of defeats, the relegation zone, and results worse than the pre-season bookmaker expectation all raise the risk, and of 78 Premier League clubs that sacked a manager in the relegation zone, 58 percent went down anyway. No study compares sacking rates by owner type or measures how fast a sacked manager finds work. https://doi.org/10.3390/economies8040082 and https://www.skysports.com/football/news/11095/13218236 (research sub-agent, batch 72)
  • The CIES transfer-value model estimates fees from performance, age, position, contract length, and club, and its estimates correlate with fees paid at 76 to 86 percent. In 34 of 49 cases studied the fee sat within 2 million euros of the estimate, with a long tail of star overpayment such as Mbappe at 87 million euros over. https://football-observatory.com/Transfer-window-analysis-over-and-under-paid (research sub-agent, batch 72)
  • UEFA has 55 member associations, 53 of which place clubs in European competition, with places set by a five-year coefficient. The three club competitions use a 36-club league phase since 2024-25, and the official 2025-26 access list defines which domestic place enters which round. https://kassiesa.net/uefa/data/method5/crank2026.html and https://documents.uefa.com/r/Regulations-of-the-UEFA-Champions-League-2025/26/Annex-A-Access-List-for-the-2025/26-UEFA-Club-Competitions-Online (research sub-agent, batch 72)
  • League formats vary by country beyond a double round robin: championship and relegation splits (Belgium, Scotland, Austria), relegation play-offs (Germany, France), promotion play-offs (England, Spain), spring-to-autumn calendars in the Nordic leagues, different winter breaks, cups with or without extra time and two-legged ties, and a FIFA calendar that merges the September and October international windows from 2026. https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIFA_International_Match_Calendar and https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_football_league_system (research sub-agent, batch 72)
  • Historical league and cup results exist as public-domain JSON in openfootball on GitHub, as free CSV with unclear commercial terms at football-data.co.uk, and as CC BY-SA tables on Wikipedia; RSSSF is the deepest archive and offers no bulk export. https://github.com/openfootball/football.json (research sub-agent, batch 72)
  • Real names reach football games through collective licences (FIFPRO for player image rights, plus league and club licences) or through community real-names files that the player imports into a game that ships fictional names. The import pattern has run for over a decade without a reported successful lawsuit, but no court has tested it, and a broken rights chain cost EA about 100 million dollars with Brazilian players. https://www.fmscout.com/a-fm26-real-names-license-fix.html and https://www.sportcal.com/news/ea-sports-and-fifpro-in-long-term-licensing-deal-extension/ (research sub-agent, batch 72)

The open questions the three design documents left (42 in all)

exploredproblem side

The forty-two open questions of the three design documents were closed in session six, except the technical questions of the event contract, which go to that build step's plan.

Decisions 4
  • Session 5 first clears the open questions that block the first build steps, and then it opens the areas no session has covered.
  • Four areas are missing realism the board never covered: the staff around the squad, contracts and the dressing room, supporters and the press, and international football.
  • The player's own match plays in real time, or as its key moments only, so the manager can act as it happens; every other match stays a full simulation, and realism comes before speed, so the programme sets no speed limit up front. Why: The person put realism first when asked how long they may wait.
  • After the questions that block the first three build steps, session 5 stays on the open questions of the later build steps before it opens any new area.
Open ideas 1
  • The technical open questions of the event contract design document (serialisation, kind names on the wire, the Delete record, freeze-frame scope, the change-queue fields, the envelope version, and the keeper kinds) are questions for the plan of that build step, not for this board.
Findings 2
  • The attribute schema holds 36 attributes, nine in each of the four groups, so 14 slots are free. The board's earlier readings of 37 attributes and 13 free slots are out of date, and the first open question of the engine realism design document is answered. content/attributes.json:1
  • The match event now carries 17 event types and an optional player identifier and second player identifier. The board's earlier readings (that the event carries only kick-off, goal, full-time, and tactics-change with no player identifier, and that throw-ins, corners, goal kicks, and offside do not exist) are out of date, so the event contract redesign starts from a larger contract than the board records. crates/protocol/src/event.rs:16 and crates/protocol/src/event.rs:146

The staff around the squad

exploredproblem side

coaches, scouts, medical staff, as people with their own quality

Every role a real club employs is a person with a rating that each club sees through whoever hires. Staff and managers share one list of 32 attributes, each role reading a weighted part, with suitability computed for every role; the players' twelve traits plus five staff traits; three hidden values led by consistency; a languages list; and ten leaning tags. Staff grow by work, courses, and licences. Core: all of it. Scope: keep all, in the first version.

Decisions 23
  • Staff are people who also carry a rating: coaches, scouts and analysts, medical and sports-science staff, the assistant manager, a director of football, and other football roles beyond these. Why: Every one of these roles changes what the manager knows or what the squad can do, and a rating gives a quick read of a person you still get to know.
  • Staff have a personality, with opinions, temper, and loyalty, so they can clash with the manager and with players. Why: A star rating with no person behind it feels wrong.
  • Staff have trade-offs: a coach has specialisms, for example good with young players and poor with veterans, so a better coach is not better at everything. Why: A rating that is always better removes the choice.
  • Staff have a market: they cost real wages, other clubs can poach them, and they can follow a manager to a new club. Why: Staff that never move are not part of the football world.
  • Staff have a voice: they advise and warn, for example a physio who says a player is close to injury. Why: A real manager hears the staff before deciding.
  • The game also employs the wider staff of a real club: a sport psychologist, a nutritionist, strength and conditioning and rehabilitation coaches, a head groundsman, a team manager for travel and matchday logistics, a welfare officer and a translator for new signings, a loan manager, a head of recruitment, data scientists, and a set-piece analyst. Why: Several of these roles act on mechanisms already on the board, for example the groundsman on pitch wear and the psychologist on mood.
  • Every staff role is a person, with attributes, a personality, a contract, and a career. Why: A department level would bring back the rating that feels wrong.
  • Staff are full people only in the active leagues; a club in a result-only league holds a light record of each staff member, which becomes a full person when it matters, for example when an active club hires that person or the league becomes active. Why: Full staff everywhere would break the save that starts in a few minutes.
  • Staff numbers and staff quality differ by league, division, and tier, as they do in real football, so a lower-division club employs fewer and less specialised staff than a top club. Why: A fourth-tier club with fifty specialists would not be real.
  • The player chooses, department by department, whether to hire staff directly or to delegate the hiring to the head of that department. Why: Hiring fifty people by hand suits some players and not others.
  • Staff advice reaches the manager through three channels: written reports and messages, a word from the assistant and the analysts during the match, and regular staff meetings where heads of department raise concerns and proposals. Why: A real manager hears staff in all three ways, and advice only on request would hide the staff.
  • Staff advice can be wrong in four ways: random error that shrinks with quality, a personal leaning from personality that the manager can learn, the staff member's own agenda, and blind spots outside the specialism. Why: Real advice is wrong in personal ways, and learning each person's leanings is part of the manager's job.
  • Overruling the staff has consequences: an ignored staff member can become unhappy and leave, players hear about clashes and the team mood moves, and the game remembers who was right, so trust grows or falls with the record. Why: A final word that costs nothing would make the staff's voice empty.
  • The manager designs the set-piece routines and may delegate the design to the set-piece coach; the coach's quality decides how well and how fast the players learn the routines and how many routines the squad can run properly. Why: The routine is the manager's idea, but the coach decides how well it reaches the players.
  • Fitness and sports-science staff move how fast players tire and how much the team fades late in a match. Why: The match changes with the clock, and fitness work is the staff's mark on that change.
  • Analysts find the opponent's habits, and the quality of their work decides how well the opposition instructions land. Why: An opposition instruction is only as good as the analysis behind it.
  • Coaches drill the shape and the pressing, so a new tactic takes time to learn and good coaches shorten that time. Why: A tactic that works at full strength on the first day is not real.
  • Each staff member carries a playing idea: staff whose idea fits the club's style get more out of the players, and staff whose idea clashes with the manager's tactic work less well and complain. Why: Real clubs hire staff to fit a style, and a new manager often brings staff who share the idea.
  • Staff follow a manager in four ways at once: each manager has a trusted inner circle that grows over a career; each staff member decides by personality and by the bond with the manager; the old club can block a move or ask for compensation while a contract runs; and the player's own manager can bring trusted staff to a new club. Why: About three coaches leave with each sacked manager, and the movement is personal.
  • Staff can be poached: a staff member weighs club size, pay, role, and ambition; a club that takes staff under contract pays compensation; staff can leave for a promotion, for example to a head-coach post; and the player can approach other clubs' staff under the same rules. Why: Losing a good coach to a bigger club is part of real football.
