  • Staff have careers: attributes grow with experience and fall with age; retired players can start as coaches or scouts and carry traits from their playing days; assistants and coaches can become AI managers, so the manager pool grows out of the staff; and staff build a reputation from results and the players they develop, which sets their market value. Why: Staff are people, so they need a past and a future as players do.
  • Each club sees a staff member's rating through its own judgment, so the rating can be wrong, as a scouting report on a player can. Why: Staff are seen the same way players are, so no club sees the truth for free.
  • Staff carry four more things: knowledge of leagues, regions, and languages that sets where their judgment is sharp; bonds with players, who play better for coaches they trust and may follow them; a workload, so overloaded staff make more errors and a thin department fails in a busy season; and a need for facilities, since staff work better with good training grounds, data tools, and medical equipment that the club business pays for. Why: Each of these is part of how real staff succeed or fail.
Findings 5
  • Staff headcount falls steeply by tier: a Bundesliga club employs about 350 full-time staff and a 2. Bundesliga club about 158, so a second-tier club has about 45 percent of a top-tier club's staff (DFL, 2024/25). A European top-division club averages about 130 full-time-equivalent staff (UEFA, 2024), while large Premier League clubs employ 600 to more than 1,200 people. https://report.bundesliga.de/2425/de/wirtschaftsreport/wirtschaftliche-kennzahlen/lizenzfussball/jobs.html and https://ecfil.uefa.com/2025 (research sub-agent, batch 81)
  • Specialist roles fall off steeply below the top: a top club runs a median of 5 match analysts and 3 sports scientists (32 clubs, 2024); full-time nutritionists work at 14 of 26 Category 1 English academies and at 1 of 63 lower-category academies; and only 14 percent of English non-league clubs have a sports scientist and 4 percent a nutritionist. https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/full/10.1080/24733938.2024.2341837 and https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/35300580/ and https://doi.org/10.3390/app15031050 (research sub-agent, batch 81)
  • Performance staff pay falls by division: Premier League fitness and sports-science staff earn about 6,300 pounds more than Championship staff and about 21,800 pounds more than League Two staff (138 staff surveyed, about 2023). Non-player wages across UEFA top-division clubs reached 5.2 billion euros in 2024, with no split by tier. https://www.sportsmith.co/reports/british-football-performance-staff-survey-2/ (research sub-agent, batch 81)
  • About three coaches leave with each sacked manager: in one recent English season about 150 coaches lost their jobs alongside about 50 dismissed managers. Some managers bring five or more assistants to a new club, and others hire new staff at each club. No study gives the share of staff who move with a manager, and the season of the English figure needs a check. https://www.aol.com/articles/more-200-managers-coaches-lost-144321000.html and https://archive.trainingground.guru/articles/why-norwich-are-defying-convention-with-new-backroom-team (research sub-agent, batch 81)
  • No published figures give average staff headcount for English divisions below the Premier League or for the 3. Liga, how common set-piece coaches, loan managers, or data scientists are by division, or non-player wages by tier. research sub-agent, batch 81
Staff attributes, session 8 34
  • Analysts need more than three attributes: like every staff role, they carry a properly decided set of attributes, personality types, and hidden attributes, each with a named job. Why: The person raised it when asked for the top risk of match preparation: three attributes under-specify a role the area leans on. core
  • Staff personality uses the players' twelve traits plus a few staff-only traits, such as loyalty to a manager, ambition for the top job, and honesty in advice, so a retired player keeps his personality when he becomes a coach. core
  • Of the staff hidden values, consistency carries the most weight: how much a staff member's work varies week to week, as the hidden consistency of players does. core
  • The full attribute lists for each role are decided after research into what real clubs assess for each role, then role by role in the brainstorm. core
  • A club's view of a staff member's ratings comes from the judgment of whoever makes the hire: the manager's own attributes when the manager hires directly, and the head of the department when the hiring is delegated. core
  • Staff grow three ways: work at a club a little above their level grows the attributes fastest; courses and licences the club pays for grow them further, at a cost in time away and fees; and licences are required for some roles and competitions, as real rules demand. core
  • Every staff member carries a shared core of five attributes: football knowledge sets the base accuracy of all advice; communication sets how much of a report or instruction is taken up; man management sets how fast bonds with players form; reflective learning sets how fast the person grows over a career; and work rate sets how much workload the person carries before errors rise. Why: Coaching research splits a coach's knowledge into professional, interpersonal, and intrapersonal kinds, and real data ties communication to player availability. core
  • Staff carry five staff-only traits beside the players' twelve: loyalty to a manager, ambition for the top job, honesty in advice, discretion (whether the person leaks), and openness to new methods. core
  • Staff carry three hidden values: consistency, which weighs most and worsens under workload; awareness of blind spots, whether the person warns when advice falls outside the specialism; and tolerance of long hours, how fast the person burns out over a season of overload. core
  • Work rate sets a staff member's capacity in a week, and tolerance of long hours sets how fast the person burns out over a season of overload. core
  • A staff member has no separate value for adapting to a new club's style: the person's playing idea and openness to new methods decide how fast a style clash fades, and adaptability keeps one meaning, the trait for settling in a new country and culture. core
  • Rapport is not stored: whether a player tells a staff member about a knock or a problem comes from the bond between that player and that staff member, and man management sets how fast bonds form. core
  • The director of football carries six role attributes: squad planning (a view three to five years ahead that flags expiring contracts and gaps), negotiation (fees and wages in talks), financial acumen (budgets and spending rules), coach appraisal (how well the director judges managers and staff, which sets the view of delegated hires), network (early word on available players and loans), and data fluency (the ability to use analytics, split from openness to new methods, which is the willingness). core
  • The head of recruitment carries five role attributes: profile fit (matching targets to the club style), scout management (how many assignments run at once and how well reports combine), succession planning (a plan per position, split from the director, who plans the whole squad), agent and club relations (the odds that a first approach succeeds), and combined judgement (how much noise the head removes when several scouts report). core
  • A scout carries six role attributes: judging current ability, judging potential, and judging character (each sets the noise of one estimate), report structure (criteria against gut feel, which sets bias against random error), birth-date and maturation correction (whether early-born and early-maturing youths are over-rated), and data use (accuracy when scouting by video); the tags carry only regional knowledge and biases. core
  • The loan manager carries four role attributes: judging a loan club's environment (predicts growth and minutes), loan-club relations (whether minutes and role clauses are honoured), player welfare (the remote care of players away from the club, split from man management of the squad), and loan monitoring (the accuracy of progress reports). core
  • The assistant manager's candidate attributes are in-match reading (the quality of suggested substitutions and changes), staff coordination (spreads workload and cuts delays), man management, and tactical knowledge (accuracy of pre-match advice); the trim to one cohesive set removes the overlaps with the core. core
  • A first-team coach's candidate attributes are session design (how fast the squad learns a tactic), teaching style (how fast individual attributes grow), content rated separately for attacking, defending, technical, and tactical work (which training areas improve), and tactical breadth (how many tactics or phases the squad can hold). core
  • The goalkeeping coach's candidate attributes are keeper technique, keeper distribution, keeper profiling, and mental preparation; the set-piece coach's are routine design, routine capacity (how many routines the squad holds), defending set pieces, and reading opposition set pieces. core
  • The fitness coach's candidate attributes are conditioning (late-match fade) and load management (training fatigue against gains); youth coaches carry development coaching (youth growth rate) and learning environment (youth morale and drop-out); the academy manager carries pathway management, curriculum consistency (one club style across the age groups), and talent identification (quality of the youth intake). core
  • Every staff member carries one shared list of attributes; each role reads a weighted part of that list, and a staff member's suitability for any role is computed from the list, as a player's rating per position is. The role walk collects candidates, and a trim merges the overlaps into the list. The core, the traits, the hidden values, and the tags stay. Why: Staff move roles and have a suitability beyond their current role. core
  • The match and opposition analyst's candidates are tactical understanding (finds opponent habits), line-up prediction (a design choice), calibration (how honest the stated confidence is), communication, workload management (report timing), and data interpretation. core
  • The set-piece analyst reads the analyst attributes for dead balls; the data scientist's candidates are modelling (less noise in model-based player ratings), uncertainty (error bars on those ratings), data coverage (which leagues have usable data), and translation (explaining to staff who are not technical). core
  • The club doctor's candidates are pitchside emergency care (accuracy and speed of head-knock advice), diagnosis (how far the shown layoff differs from the true one, and the chance a knock is missed), illness control (how fast illness spreads, a design value), and medical ethics (whether players disclose to this doctor); the head of medical's are department leadership (lowers errors below them), communication with the manager, and communication across departments. core
  • The physiotherapist's candidates are assessment (shows accumulated load, catches hidden knocks, fitness tests, medicals), treatment (shorter layoffs, less recurrence), rehabilitation planning (pacing through return phases), and injury prevention (lower hazard through screening); the rehabilitation coach's are on-field reintegration (sharpness on return, fewer relapses) and progression judgement (speed against recurrence risk between phases). core
  • The sports scientist's candidates are load monitoring, data interpretation (quality of rest and rotation advice), recovery protocols (condition recovery, jet lag and climate plans), and persuading the coaches (whether the manager accepts the advice); the strength and conditioning coach's are programming (raises the sprint and slow reserves, lowers landing and turning hazard), lifting technique (training-injury rate), and physical profiling (shows fitness ceilings). core
  • The nutritionist's candidates are fuelling and recovery, body composition (slows physical decline), and travel nutrition; the sport psychologist's are performance psychology (confidence and pressure handling), wellbeing and referral (catches low morale or a crisis), profiling (shows hidden traits of signing targets), and confidentiality (whether players open up). Their effects are design values, because no study sizes them. core
  • The head groundsman's candidates are turf science, recovery under use, weather management, and surface consistency; the team manager's are travel planning, handling disruption, and rules administration; player care's are relocation support, welfare radar, and transition support. core
  • There is no separate translator: every staff member has a languages list, player care reads it and carries cultural bridging (climate and league-style settling, loneliness). core
  • The specialism tags go through the same trim as the attributes, and a tag survives only where it carries a trade-off no attribute can. core
  • Staff change carries no separate rule: a new fitness coach or doctor starts with no bonds and no knowledge of the players, and the injury cost of the change emerges from that. core
  • Ten specialism tags remain, each a leaning or a trade-off no attribute carries: youth developer, veteran whisperer, early-maturer bias, motivator, disciplinarian, aggressive return to play, conservative return to play, soft-tissue specialist, concussion-cautious, and club-first doctor. core
  • Managers use the shared staff list: a manager is a staff member whose role reads a weighted part of it, so an assistant's suitability to become a manager is computed like any role. core
  • The shared staff list holds 32 attributes. Core (5): football knowledge, communication, man management (players and staff), reflective learning, work rate. Coaching (6): tactical coaching (how fast and how much tactic the squad holds), attacking, defending, technical, and goalkeeping coaching (each sets how fast that area grows), and set-piece coaching. Judgement and analysis (7): judging ability, judging potential, judging people (character and staff), calibration, data analysis, opposition analysis (line-up and habits), in-match reading. Management (5): squad planning (including the player pathway), negotiation, financial acumen, network, administration (logistics and rules). Medical and performance (7): diagnosis (including pitchside care), rehabilitation (including treatment), injury prevention (including load management), physical assessment, conditioning, recovery, sport psychology. Care (1): player care (welfare and settling). Grounds (1): groundsmanship (turf and weather). Why: Merges join attributes that drive the same mechanism or that one role always reads together. Accepted risk: Four risks at once: a season-long sensitivity check for each of the 32 attributes adds to the check load; full staff records add to save size and memory; about 25 roles need weights over 32 attributes, all design values; and only communication and staff continuity have measured effect sizes. core
Findings, session 8 11
  • UEFA coaching licences take about 60 hours (C), 120 (B), 180 (A), and 360 (Pro, over at least one season); the Goalkeeper A and Elite Youth A take 120 hours each; holders revalidate with at least 15 hours of training every three years. In 2017 Spain had 2,379 Pro holders, Germany 861, and England 383. https://www.uefa.com/development/coaches/uefa-coaching-licences/ ; https://sportingintelligence832.substack.com/p/tuchels-appointment-highlights-englands
  • Coaching research splits a coach's knowledge into professional (the sport), interpersonal (people), and intrapersonal (learning from one's own practice), and UEFA's Pro licence has a matching professional-coach area beside the player and team, the training environment, and the match. https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/10.1260/174795409789623892 ; https://www.uefa.com/news-media/news/0268-11f1908f8de9-9feac6d280b8-1000/
  • In a survey of 125 Dutch scouts, scouts felt unable to judge a prospect's potential before age 14 to 15, and they judge by a mix of structured criteria and gut feel. https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/full/10.1080/17461391.2021.1916081
  • Ten of the twenty Premier League clubs had a dedicated set-piece coach before 2025-26, and Chelsea paid about 750,000 pounds in compensation for one. https://www.creativesetplays.com/post/premier-league-set-piece-coaches-2025-26
  • A survey of all 78 top-flight clubs in four leagues found about 11.0 medical and performance staff per club in England, Spain, and Italy against 6.9 in Germany, with 3.6 against 1.8 physiotherapists and 1.8 against 0.5 strength and conditioning coaches. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34225378/
  • In UEFA's elite club injury study, clubs with poor communication between the doctor and the manager had 82 percent match availability against 88 percent and 76 percent training attendance against 83 percent; each grade better of communication between medical and performance staff meant about 4.1 fewer days lost to hamstring injury per 1,000 hours. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6579487 ; https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11877254/
  • In the same study, a change of fitness coach or team doctor went with significantly more hamstring injury, and a new head coach who brought his own fitness staff went with about three times the hamstring burden in that season. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10660205/
  • No study measures the week-to-week consistency of football staff work; the nearest evidence is decision fatigue, where clinicians make worse choices as a session wears on, and in hospitals each extra patient per nurse raised mortality by about 7 percent. https://jamanetwork.com/journals/jamainternalmedicine/fullarticle/1910546 ; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/12387650/
  • Athletes value honesty, trust, confidentiality, and approachability in practitioners, and club doctors and physiotherapists who report to coaches lose players' trust, which lowers what players disclose. https://doi.org/10.1177/1012690217733678 ; https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/full/10.1080/1750984X.2022.2145575
  • An English Category 1 academy must employ at least 18 full-time staff on a budget of at least 2.5 million pounds a year. https://thepfsa.co.uk/understanding-the-english-football-academy-category-system-under-the-elite-player-performance-plan-eppp/
  • Weak evidence from a review chapter says abnormal playing surfaces raise lower-limb injury about 2.6 times, and the same review says natural-turf hardness is not proven to cause injury. https://www.intechopen.com/chapters/83186

Contracts and the dressing room

exploredproblem side

negotiation, promises, agents, squad status, unrest

Contracts are negotiated by agent, director of football, manager, and family, and carry money, bonuses, clauses, and promises. The dressing room has standing, groups, a culture, and a view of the manager. Unrest escalates and has a small on-pitch effect. Discipline follows each country's rules.

Decisions 29
  • A contract is negotiated, with a story behind the signature, not filled in as a form. Why: A wage and a length typed into a form is the first thing that feels wrong.
  • A player weighs more than money when choosing a contract or a club: the role, the manager, the club's project, and life off the pitch. Why: A player who counts only the wage is not a person.
  • A promise has a cost: when the manager breaks a promise about playing time, a signing, or ambition, the player reacts. Why: A free promise is an empty lever.
  • The dressing room has structure: leaders, groups, and a pecking order, so unrest can spread and can calm. Why: A flat squad cannot carry the mood the board already models.
  • A contract carries four groups of parts: money (wage, signing-on fee, loyalty bonus, agent fee), performance bonuses (appearance, goal, clean-sheet, and team success), clauses (release clause, relegation wage cut, promotion rise, option to extend), and promises and status (squad status, playing time, a stated role, and promises about signings or ambition). Why: Each part changes a player's choices or a club's money.
  • A player's standing in the dressing room comes from four sources: time at the club, ability and fame, a leader's personality, and the captain's armband that the manager gives, where a poor pick loses the squad. Why: Real standing comes from all four, and each one gives the manager a different lever.
  • Groups form in the squad by language and nation, by age, by starters and bench, and by shared history such as the academy or joining together; a lone new signing can feel shut out. Why: Groups are how a grievance or a friendship becomes a squad matter.
  • Unrest moves through leaders, who can calm it or spread it; through groups, first inside the player's group and then beyond; into the press, which puts pressure on the manager and the board; and onto the pitch, where a divided squad plays worse together. Why: Unrest that stays in one player's mood is not how a real dressing room behaves.
  • The manager sees the dressing room three ways at once: some issues show at once, some arrive through people such as the captain and the assistant, and some show only as symptoms whose cause the manager must work out. Why: Real managers see the room in part, and each issue reaches them by its own route.
  • A divided dressing room has a small effect on the pitch, smaller than the spread of skill between players, so it shows over a season and not in one match. Why: The measured skill spread is small, and a larger effect would make the dressing room outweigh football.
  • The squad holds a view of the manager, built from results, fairness, and the manager's reputation. Why: Respect is what gives the manager's word weight in the room.
  • Senior players can go to the board, and a squad that turns on a manager can get the manager sacked. Why: Player power is part of how real managers lose their jobs.
  • A club has a culture of standards and a winning habit that lasts across seasons; new players take it on or erode it. Why: A club is more than the squad of one season.
  • A club can have an icon, a player the fans and the board protect, whom the manager cannot easily drop or sell. Why: Some players are bigger than the manager at their club.
  • Four parties shape a contract: the agent, whose skill and greed shape the deal; the director of football, who can negotiate for the club and to whom the player can delegate; the manager, who sells the role and the project; and the family, who can decide a move about country, language, or a child's school. Why: A real deal has more than two sides.
  • A promise is verbal and moves the player's trust and mood when it is kept or broken, and a few promises can be written as clauses, for example a pay rise after a number of games. Why: Real promises are verbal, and only a few terms reach the contract.
  • The last year of a contract plays out four ways: some players run the contract down to earn more elsewhere, a player can agree terms with a foreign club six months before the end, clubs sell a player early rather than lose the player for nothing, and personality decides whether a player renews, runs down, or forces a move. Why: Free transfers are a quarter to a third of big-five signings, so running down is a normal result.
  • The years left on a contract join the shared valuation, as in the CIES fee model, because every club can see them, and a release clause caps the price where one exists. This amends the earlier list of visible inputs to the valuation. Why: A player with six months left sells for far less, and free transfers are common.
  • A new signing paid more than the established players upsets the pecking order. Why: Wages are visible in a real dressing room, and they carry status.
  • Bonuses change behaviour: a selfish striker with a goal bonus shoots more often, and a team bonus pulls the squad together. Why: A contract term that never reaches the pitch is only accounting.
  • The squad status in a contract sets how many matches the player expects, and every missed match counts against it. Why: Squad status is the promise most players care about.
  • Some players play harder in the last year of a contract, and some coast after signing a long one, as their personality decides. Why: Effort that follows the contract is a known pattern in real football.
  • An unhappy player escalates in steps: a private word, a formal transfer request, going public in an interview or a post, poor effort in training and matches that the staff notice first, and in rare cases a refusal to play that costs the player heavily in standing. Why: Unrest needs a path to an end, and these are the real steps.
  • The manager's discipline tools, such as fines, dropping a player, and training apart, work within the real rules of each country, and going past those rules brings disputes. Why: Real football limits discipline, for example a fine of at most two weeks' wages in England.
  • Every agent is a person, with a personality, relationships with clubs, and an agenda, but most agents have little power; a few large agencies represent many players and deal often with the same clubs. Why: The research shows a concentrated market in which agents gain from moves, and a person with an agenda carries that better than a fee.
  • Real limits on discipline apply, but a manager with strong standing at the club can push further before a dispute starts. Why: It keeps real limits and still rewards a manager the club trusts.
  • The manager holds four kinds of discipline tool: a private warning, a fine within the limit, and a suspension for serious cases; dropping a player or sending the player to the reserve team; training apart from the first team; and public pressure through criticism in the press or the transfer list. Why: Each tool lands differently on the player and the dressing room.
  • When the manager goes past the rules, the player can take a formal dispute to a tribunal and the club can lose and pay compensation, and the board can warn or overrule the manager to protect the club. Why: Crossing the line must cost something the manager feels.
  • A discipline dispute goes to a tribunal that can award any outcome from a warning to a free exit with compensation, following the rules of the club's country, and the players' union appears only inside the dispute. Why: It keeps the real remedy without making the union a separate actor in the game.
Open ideas 1
  • Agents follow the same rule as staff: a full person where an active league needs one, and a light record elsewhere, so ten thousand agents do not slow the save.
Findings 15
  • A contract runs at most five years, or three for a player under 18, and a protected period of three years (signed before 28) or two years (signed at 28 or older) bans a player who breaks the contract for four months. Squads in the big five leagues average about 2.3 years left on their contracts. https://jusmundi.com/en/document/rule/en-fifa-federation-internationale-de-football-association-regulations-on-the-status-and-transfer-of-players-2025-fifa-rstp-2025-tuesday-1st-july-2025 and https://football-observatory.com/IMG/sites/b5wp/2017/222/en/
  • Spanish law requires a release clause in every player contract, and Brazil has a similar system; in England, Germany, and France a release clause is optional. No figures show how common or how large release clauses are elsewhere. https://confilegal.com/20200120-la-clausula-de-rescision-por-voluntad-del-futbolista-en-espana-es-unica-en-inglaterra-alemania-o-francia-no-existe/
  • Free transfers are common: 26 to 32 percent of signings in the big five leagues in 2019 and 2020, and 62.5 percent of international transfers in 2025, which carried 0.8 percent of fee value; only 17.7 percent of international transfers had a fee. https://football-observatory.com/IMG/sites/mr/mr58/en/ and the FIFA Global Transfer Report 2025
  • Agent fees are large: clubs paid agents 1.37 billion US dollars on international transfers in 2025, about 10 percent of transfer spending, and Premier League clubs pay about 400 to 460 million pounds a year. FIFA suspended its caps on agent fees at the end of 2023. https://inside.fifa.com/transfer-system/agents/news/club-agent-service-fees-reach-new-heights-2025 and https://www.thefa.com/news/2026/apr/01/agent-fees-and-transactions
  • A relegation wage cut is reported as usually 25 to 30 percent, within a range of 20 to 50 percent; no count shows how many contracts carry one. https://www.foxsports.com/articles/soccer/revealed-tottenham-stars-would-have-wages-cut-by-50-per-cent-if-they-suffer-premier-league-relegation
  • Promises about playing time, squad status, or signings appear to be verbal only, not contract terms; the only written remedy is that an established player who plays in fewer than 10 percent of official matches may end the contract, and rulings on it are rare. Signing-on and loyalty payments are paid in equal yearly instalments in the Premier League. https://www.football-legal.com/content/a-football-rarity-an-article-15-rstp-sporting-just-cause-case-at-the-cas and https://www.espn.co.uk/football/story/_/id/38291291/whats-contract-salary-add-ons-transfer-fee-more
  • No published figures give the spread of contract lengths at signing, the share of pay that bonuses make up, how common each clause is, or how often loyalty bonuses and signing-on fees appear. research sub-agent, batch 87
  • Few licensed agents earn fees: in 2025 there were 10,525 licensed agents, and only about 15 to 17 percent were paid in an international deal. Deals paying more than one million US dollars were about 12 percent of paid cases and 68 percent of the money. https://inside.fifa.com/news/club-agent-service-fees-reach-new-heights-2025 (FIFA Football Agents Report 2025)
  • The agent market is concentrated: in 2010/11 half of big-five players were clients of 83 agents or agencies and a quarter of 24, placed through closed networks with clubs; in 2025 two agencies each represent more than 100 big-five players. https://football-observatory.com/IMG/pdf/report_agents_2012-2.pdf and https://football-observatory.com/Leading-agencies-in-the-big-5-ongoing
  • Agents gain from moves more than renewals: the usual dual role is the player plus the buying club (851 of 962 double representations in 2025), and FIFA's rules, largely upheld by the EU Court of Justice in July 2026, cap fees at 10 percent of the transfer fee from the selling club and 3 to 5 percent of wages from the player or buying club. https://inside.fifa.com/news/club-agent-service-fees-reach-new-heights-2025 and https://www.ashurstperkinscoie.com/en/insights/ecj-rules-on-fifa-agent-regulations-compatibility-with-eu-competition-law/
  • Agents recruit young players heavily: in 2011, 58 percent of agents' clients were not senior professionals, and FIFA's rules let an agent approach a minor only in the six months before the first professional contract, with a guardian's written consent. No study measures how often players change agents. https://football-observatory.com/IMG/pdf/report_agents_2012-2.pdf and https://digitalhub.fifa.com/m/1e7b741fa0fae779/original/FIFA-Football-Agent-Regulations.pdf
  • In England the Premier League standard contract caps a fine at two weeks' basic wage for a first offence and four weeks for later offences within twelve months, unless the players' union approves more; a club can suspend a player on full pay for up to 14 days while it investigates, and the player appeals first to the club board and then to the League, with the sanction paused until the appeal ends. https://static1.squarespace.com/static/579f1eef2e69cf81541f5565/t/57bc6605e58c62993057c155/1471964677468/standard+pl+contract.pdf and https://www.cnn.com/2011/10/28/sport/football/tevez-pfa-fine-manchester
  • Since 2018 FIFA's transfer rules treat conduct meant to force a player to end or change a contract, such as training alone, as abusive, which gives the player just cause to leave with compensation; under a FIFA and FIFPRO agreement, the rules from 1 January 2027 expressly ban forcing a player to train in isolation or demoting a player as pressure. https://www.mills-reeve.com/blogs/sports/april-2018/amendments-to-the-fifa-regulations-on-the-status-a/ and https://www.fifpro.org/en/articles/2026/06/what-the-fifpro-and-fifa-agreement-means-for-players
  • Discipline limits differ widely by country: Spain caps a fine at one twentieth of annual pay and bans keeping a player out of training except as a sanction or for injury; Italy caps a fine at 25 percent of monthly pay and allows training apart only as a sanction confirmed by an arbitration board; France bans fines outright and limits separate training groups; Portugal caps a fine at one third of monthly pay per offence; the Netherlands defaults to half a day's wage per week. In Spain, Italy, and Portugal the usual remedy for abuse is ending the contract with compensation. https://www.boe.es/buscar/act.php?id=BOE-A-1985-12313 and https://www.assocalciatori.it/sites/default/files/attachment/pagina/Accordo%20collettivo%20AIC%20-%20Lega%20Serie%20A.pdf and https://sjogadores.pt/uploads/files/CCT%20Atualizado.pdf and https://www.vvcs.nl/documents/2023/10/CAO-Contractspelers-2023-2027-digitaal-exemplaar.pdf
  • Every source treats team selection as free: a club may leave a player out of matches without limit, and no rule limits public criticism as such, although dignity and harassment clauses in Italy, Portugal, and France come close. The fine cap for top-division German players is not public. https://www.iberley.es/jurisprudencia/resolucion-contrato-trabajo-falta-ocupacion-efectiva-futbolista-profesional-sentencia-ts-sala-social-sec-1-rec-238-2008-28-04-2010-509771 and https://www.lto.de/recht/hintergruende/h/eren-derdiyok-hoffenheim-training-b-elf-beschaeftigungsanspruch-gleichbehandlung

Supporters and the press

exploredproblem side

fan mood, expectations, interviews, media pressure

Supporters are groups whose balance is the fan mood, with power through the board, ownership models, money, and the manager. The crowd moves the referee and nervous players. Press conferences, journalists as people, and stories that build over weeks. Open: press duties by competition.

Decisions 14
  • Supporters are groups with a history, not an attendance figure and a mood bar. Why: A number cannot react to the club.
  • Supporters react to what the club stands for: selling the icon, abandoning the style, or raising ticket prices. Why: Identity is what fans care about beyond results.
  • The press is not a quiz of stock answers: what the manager says has consequences. Why: Repeated questions with no effect feel wrong.
  • Rumours, leaks, and headlines build over weeks into stories and sagas. Why: A single headline with no follow-up does not feel like football.
  • The supporter side has four parts: the crowd in the match, whose noise and pressure move home advantage, the referee, and nervous players; fan mood over a season, with expectations, patience, protests, and attendance; groups of fans such as ultras, season-ticket holders, casual fans, and fans abroad, who want different things; and rivalries and derbies that raise the stakes. Why: Each part is a way real supporters shape a club.
  • The press side has four parts: press conferences before and after matches, whose words reach players, fans, the board, and rivals; journalists as people with sources, slants, and relationships; stories that build, peak, and fade over weeks; and media pressure that moves the manager's job security and the players' mood and form. Why: Each part is a way the real press shapes a club.
  • The crowd moves three things: the referee, players on both sides whose personality handles pressure badly, and the home team's belief late in a match when it chases a goal. Why: The data shows home advantage survives without the referee effect, so the crowd must reach the players too.
  • The manager takes part in every press conference and can delegate one to the assistant; where a competition requires players at a conference, the manager chooses which players go; and the manager, staff, and players meet the press in other situations as well. Why: Press work is constant in real football, and choosing who speaks is part of it.
  • Stories come from real events in the game and from rumours that journalists invent, and the manager cannot always tell which is which. Why: Real transfer gossip mixes truth and invention.
  • Supporters hold power four ways: through the board, which protests can move to overrule or sack the manager; through real ownership models such as member clubs with elections, the 50+1 rule, and supporters' trusts; through money, as attendance, season tickets, and shirt sales fall; and through the manager directly, as chants and boos reach the manager's standing and the players' confidence. Why: Real supporters hold all four kinds of power, and the kind differs by club and country.
  • Each fan group has its own mix of expectations, and the club's fan mood is the balance between the groups. Why: Ultras, season-ticket holders, and fans abroad want different things.
  • Rivalries come from three sources: known real rivalries in the world data, geography with stronger rivalries in the same city, and events in the save such as cup finals, title races, and a star who moves to the rival. Why: A rivalry needs a cause the fans recognise.
  • How media pressure reaches the manager's job depends on the owner: some owners read the papers, and others listen only to fans or only to results. Why: Owners differ, and the owner type already shapes the club business.
  • The manager can use the press as well as suffer it: give exclusives that buy goodwill, leak a story on purpose with a risk that it traces back, freeze out a journalist or an outlet with a risk of hostile coverage, and learn over time which journalists are reliable. Why: Real managers manage the press, and each tool has a cost.
Open ideas 1
  • Member ownership joins the owner types of the club business: members elect a president, candidates make promises about signings and the manager, and an election year changes what the board wants.
Findings 3
  • UEFA's club competitions require the head coach and at least one player at the press conference the day before a match, 15 minutes of training open to media, flash interviews on matchday (the head coach at least 5 and the player of the match at least 4 if asked), several players through the mixed zone, and the coach at the post-match conference. The FIFA World Cup also requires a player: Senegal were fined 10,000 Swiss francs in 2022 and Argentina 300,000 in 2014 when only the coach attended. https://documents.uefa.com/r/Regulations-of-the-UEFA-Conference-League-2026/27/Article-78-Media-activities-on-the-day-before-the-match-Online and https://www.si.com/soccer/2014/07/11/world-cup-argentina-fined-skipping-interviews-alejandro-sabella
  • In the leagues checked, the pre-match conference is the manager's: the Premier League names the manager only, and requires weekly player content sessions, a pre-match player interview, super-flash interviews with two players at full time, a half-time interview or dressing-room filming, at least six players through the mixed zone, the manager at the post-match conference, and a pre-season media day. The Bundesliga and Ligue 1 require club conferences without naming who attends; Ligue 1 players pass through the mixed zone but need not answer. LaLiga and Serie A rules are not confirmed. https://resources.premierleague.pulselive.com/premierleague/document/2025/07/24/99839920-d274-42aa-a2ac-e5612b4f6c61/PL_Handbook_25-26_Digital_24.07.pdf and https://media.dfl.de/sites/2/2024/12/Anhang-XI-zur-LO-Medienrichtlinien-2024-12-06-Stand.pdf and https://www.lfp.fr/assets/Convention_Medias_Presse_L1_L2_2024_2029_9f1339281b.pdf
  • Words in the media carry real consequences: UEFA fined Ferguson 10,000 euros in 2013 for skipping post-match media, the FA fined Chris Wilder 11,500 pounds for comments about officials, and Ronaldo's 2022 television interview ended his Manchester United contract. https://www.aljazeera.com/sports/2013/3/22/uefa-fine-ferguson-and-ban-nani and https://www.cbssports.com/soccer/news/manchester-united-terminate-cristiano-ronaldo-contract-with-immediate-effect-after-explosive-interview/

International football

exploredproblem side

national teams, international breaks, call-ups, tournaments

The calendar, national teams, and players on duty are in the game; managing a nation comes later. Call-ups follow form, the coach's taste, loyalty, and depth. Duty brings fatigue, injuries with compensation, mood, and value changes.

Decisions 6
  • International football must fix four things: breaks cost players fatigue, travel, injuries, and mood; call-ups follow a logic the manager can follow and influence; tournaments change players' value, fame, and confidence; and national teams rise and fall, so a player's nation matters to the club. Why: Each of these feels wrong when it is missing.
  • International football has three parts in the game: the calendar of FIFA windows, summer tournaments, and club release rules; national teams with squads, coaches, rankings, qualifiers, and tournaments simulated as full competitions; and players on duty, with call-ups, caps, travel, fatigue, injuries, and effects on value, fame, and mood. Why: These are the parts that reach the club game.
  • Managing a national team is a future addition, not part of this programme. Why: It is a separate game mode that the club game does not need first.
  • A call-up follows four signals: club form and minutes weighted by league level, the national coach's own style and favourites, loyalty to established internationals, and the depth of the nation, so a player from a small nation is called up at a lower level. Why: The manager can follow a call-up that rests on signals like these.
  • The manager and the player shape international duty four ways: minutes at the club raise the chance of a call-up, the manager can ask the national coach to rest a tired player and the coach may refuse, a player eligible for two nations chooses one and the manager or the family can sway the choice, and an older player can retire from international football. Why: Each is a real lever around international duty.
  • International duty changes four things: fatigue from matches and travel, with long-haul trips costing more; injuries on duty, with compensation to the club; mood and confidence from a first cap, a good tournament, or being left out; and value and fame from a strong or poor tournament. Why: Breaks and tournaments must cost and reward something.

How the player watches the match

exploredsolution side

real time, key moments, other grounds

The base view is the top-down pitch with height, players as circles for now. Overlays toggle, and the player can zoom and follow. Length comes from a five-mode ladder, a dynamic mode, build-up before each moment, and custom rules; between highlights the pitch fast-forwards. Rewind while paused and full replays after. Other grounds: live scores, highlights after. Occasion: sound, then build-up, then the pitch. Movement is banded against tracking data. Core: all of it. Scope: keep all, in the first version.

Decisions 2
  • The other matches of a matchday simulate in the background while the player watches their own match, so the results are ready at the final whistle and live scores from other grounds appear as they happen.
  • Key moments come in tiers that together cover four kinds: chances and goals, moments the manager may act on (a tiring player, an injury, a card, a change of shape), a filter the manager sets, and the moments that tell the story of the result, including pressure that produced no shot.
Watching, session 8 11
  • The other matches of a matchday simulate in the background while the player watches their own match, so the results are ready at the final whistle and live scores from other grounds appear as they happen. core
  • Key moments come in tiers that together cover four kinds: chances and goals, moments the manager may act on (a tiring player, an injury, a card, a change of shape), a filter the manager sets, and the moments that tell the story of the result, including pressure that produced no shot. core
  • Watching the match must answer eight faults: play that is hard to read, matches too long or too short, unreal movement, no way to look back, repetitive commentary, figures cut off from play, no sense of occasion, and no control of the view. Why: The person named all eight as true. core
  • The base view is the top-down pitch with height: the ball's shadow, a lift for players in the air, and a slight tilt for depth. Players stay circles for now, and a tilted pitch with figures may come later. core
  • The player can pause and rewind through the match so far, then return to live, and after the final whistle can replay any part of the match, linked from the report. core
  • Other matches show live scores and key events during the player's match, and their highlights are available after the final whistle; the player does not switch to watch them live. core
  • The sense of occasion comes from three things, built in this order: crowd sound that rises with danger and the size of the match, with commentary on top; a pre-match build-up with the stakes, the story, the line-ups, and the weather; and a pitch that shows the ground's size, weather, wear, and a crowd edge that fills with attendance. core
  • The player controls the view two ways: overlays toggled on the live view (shape lines, pass and run arrows, player state such as tiredness, and heat zones), and zoom with follow of the ball or one chosen player. core
  • Match length is set four ways: a five-mode ladder (key moments, extended, comprehensive, full match, and commentary only) fed by the board's highlight ranking and changeable during a match; a dynamic mode that shows more as the match grows tense; every shown moment starting early enough to show its build-up; and custom rules the player sets, such as all set pieces or only the own team's attacks. Why: Rewind, custom rules, and build-up control would set the game apart; no source shows them in Football Manager. Accepted risk: Four viewing features to design, tune, and test. core
  • Between highlights the pitch keeps playing at high speed, so the player sees the shape of play without detail. core
  • The realism test also bands movement: player speeds, turn rates, and accelerations against real tracking data, so a slide or a snap fails a check. core
Findings, session 8 4
  • The viewer today draws a flat top-down 2D pitch on one canvas, plays at 1, 2, 4, or 8 times speed with its own pause, shows a goal banner timed in real time so it stays readable at 8 times, and warns when the stream cannot keep up. web/playback.mjs:13; web/pitch.mjs:1; web/goal-moment.mjs:1
  • Football Manager 24 offers five highlight modes (Key, Extended, Comprehensive, Full Match, Commentary Only), changeable during a match, with separate speed sliders for play and for highlights; FM26 adds a dynamic mode that shows more highlights as a tense match nears full time and fewer in a one-sided one, and fills the time between highlights with a 2D overview and data cards that link to clips. https://www.ggrecon.com/guides/football-manager-2024-change-highlights/ ; https://www.footballmanager.com/fm26/features/where-storytelling-evolves-fm26s-match-day-experience
  • Football Manager players complain that Extended highlights take about 20 minutes with 2 to 3 minutes of dead time before a highlight, that the FM26 engine looks arcade-like with scorelines that are too high, and some watch whole matches in 2D because it shows movement and positions most clearly. No source confirms a rewind, a post-match full replay, custom highlight rules, or control over how much build-up a highlight shows. SI bug tracker r41240 (search snippet); https://www.altchar.com/reviews/football-manager-2026-review-a-disappointing-step-backwards-a6QHj3y0USb4 ; Steam FM19 discussion
  • FM26 offers seven cameras (2D Classic, Behind Goal, Aerial, Broadcast, Tactical, Dynamic Sideline, Vertical Scrolling) with adjustable height and zoom, an in-match xG graph and momentum widget, assistant advice that uses xG, automatic goal replays with angles by goal type, and a pause on injury. https://www.footballmanagerblog.org/2025/09/fm26-match-day-experience-broadcast-mode-dynamic-highlights.html ; https://www.footballmanager.com/the-dugout/maximising-your-match-days-fm26

New players entering the world

exploredproblem side

youth academies, youth intake, and generated players

Academies follow each country's structure, the full model starts at about 16, and a young player's future is a narrowing range. The generator copies measured patterns: birth quarters, late bloomers, national output. Style comes from where a player develops. Open: versatility and parent rates.

Decisions 14
  • Academies are modelled in full at least down to the under-18 level; below that, the younger players exist as a pool that clubs know only roughly, and over time a club learns which of them are likely to become good or great players; every player of the right age moves up through the age groups. Why: A full model of every child is not needed, but the path into the under-18s must be real.
  • The youth side has four parts: the academy with its grade, facilities, coaches, and age groups; recruiting young players through scouting, the local catchment area, and taking players from other academies with the compensation that follows; the intake into the professional ranks; and the path to the first team through under-21 and reserve football, B teams, loans, and a debut. Why: Each part is a lever a real club pulls on its youth.
  • The world is free to drift over a long save, and the season test bands it, so the quality of the leagues stays inside a real range. Why: Real football shifts over decades, and a test is a better guard than a generator that forces the same shape every year.
  • New players enter the world by four routes: club academies and their rough pool of younger players; late bloomers found in amateur or semi-professional football; players who rise through the lower leagues; and young players from beyond UEFA who arrive by transfer, held as a rough pool outside the modelled leagues. Why: Not every professional comes through a big academy.
  • Generated players differ through the style of where they develop, bodies that follow real spreads by position, rare profiles at real rates, and background such as a professional parent or a choice between two nations; more sources of variety may follow the research. Why: Players that look alike break a long save.
  • A young player has no fixed potential number: each player starts with a wide range of possible futures, and the range narrows as the player grows and the five development factors act. Why: A fixed ceiling from birth would make development a lottery ticket, and a range keeps the future open.
  • A club learns about its young players four ways: through youth scouts and coaches, as good as their own judgment; through time at the club; through physical tests, trials, and strong youth matches; and through other clubs' interest, which is itself a signal. Why: Real clubs learn slowly and from several sources.
  • The full player model starts at the scholarship age, about 16, or at the equivalent point in each country's academy system; younger players form the rough pool. This refines the earlier choice of under-18. Why: The scholarship is the first real decision a club makes about a young player in every country.
  • Clubs over-rate early-born and physically early players and miss late developers, as real academies do, but good youth staff and a manager who knows about the bias can correct it. Why: The bias is real and measured, and correcting it is a skill worth rewarding.
  • B teams follow each country's real rules: in Spain at least one division below the first team, in the Netherlands and Portugal with no promotion, in Germany no higher than the third tier, and in England an under-21 league and cup invitations instead. Why: Real rules keep each country's pathway distinct.
  • Squad registration rules belong: homegrown quotas by competition, each country's limits on non-EU players and England's work permits, and squad lists fixed at set dates; a researcher is finding the real rules before the detail is decided. Why: These rules make a club's own academy worth more and shape every transfer.
  • The player chooses, as with staff hiring, whether to make the yearly decisions on young players (a professional contract, release, or a loan) or to delegate them to the academy head. Why: Some players want to shape every youth decision and others do not.
  • The player generator copies four measured patterns: real birth-quarter shares with a small group of late maturers who have high upside, growth of about one centimetre per decade over a long save, each nation's real output of players in total and per head, and eligibility for two nations at real rates. Why: Each pattern is measured, and together they keep a long save believable.
  • A player's style comes from where the player develops, the league and the academy, and not from nationality. This replaces national styles in the list of sources of variety. Why: League styles are measured, and the nationality differences in rating data may be stereotypes.
Findings 16
  • Academies run in phases: in England, a Foundation Phase from under-9 to under-11, a Youth Development Phase from under-12 to under-16, and a Professional Development Phase from under-17 to under-23; a scholarship is offered by the under-16 year and a professional contract signed at 17 at the earliest. Every German top-two club must run a graded youth centre, and French clubs use aspirant, stagiaire, and elite contracts from 15 to 20. From 2027, FIFA lets in-house trained under-18s sign contracts of up to five years. https://www.premierleague.com/en/footballandcommunity/youth-development/parent-hub/rights-rules-and-responsibilities and https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nachwuchsleistungszentrum and https://www.servulo.com/en/knowledge/FIFA-Transfer-System-2027-The-New-Rules-of-The-Game/9802/
  • Few academy players make it: about 4 percent of academy players aged 13 to 18 at two LaLiga academies reached the top tier within ten years, and only one in six English players signed at 16 is still in the game at 21. Widely quoted figures such as 0.5 percent of academy entrants turning professional are not primary data. https://www.essex.ac.uk/news/2024/06/27/just-four-per-cent-of-teenage-academy-players-make-it-to-top-tier-football and https://www.pressreader.com/uk/the-daily-telegraph-sport/20210330/281651077897760
  • Academies over-select players born early in the selection year: 45 to 57 percent of academy players are born in the first quarter against about 10 percent in the last, and the LaLiga study found no last-quarter births among the players who made it. Confidence, ball reception, dribbling, and coach ratings separated those who made it. https://www.researchgate.net/publication/381051615 and https://www.essex.ac.uk/news/2024/06/27/just-four-per-cent-of-teenage-academy-players-make-it-to-top-tier-football
  • Moving a young player between academies costs compensation: England's fixed fees rise with age and academy grade, with further payments per first-team appearance and a sell-on share; FIFA's training compensation runs from 10,000 to 90,000 euros a year by club category, and solidarity pays up to 5 percent of a later fee to the clubs that trained the player from 12 to 21. Younger English academy players must live within one hour, or 90 minutes from under-12, unless the academy houses them. https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elite_Player_Performance_Plan and https://digitalhub.fifa.com/m/6370b586e046913d/original/Circular-1892_RSTP-categorisation-of-clubs-and-registration-periods.pdf
  • B teams play in the senior pyramid in Spain, the Netherlands, Portugal, and Germany, but must stay below the first team: in Spain at least one division below, in the Netherlands and Portugal with no promotion, and in Germany no higher than the 3. Liga. England uses the under-21 Premier League 2 and invitations to the EFL Trophy instead. https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primera_Federaci%C3%B3n and https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eerste_Divisie and https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Professional_Development_League
  • Academies are graded: an English Category 1 academy needs at least 18 full-time staff and a yearly budget of 2.5 million pounds, and German youth centres are graded up to three stars. Barcelona has 44 to 46 academy graduates in the big five leagues, and 30 clubs there have none. https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elite_Player_Performance_Plan and https://football-observatory.com/WeeklyPost529
  • Brazil, France, and Argentina export the most players (3,020, 2,293, and 2,171 across 135 leagues in 2020 to 2025); per head, Iceland, Montenegro, and Croatia export the most. The foreign share in 31 UEFA top divisions was 42.3 percent in 2022, from 7.9 percent in Ukraine to 74 percent in Cyprus, and only 17 percent of players played at the club that trained them. https://www.cies.ch/research/news/news-detail/article/football-expatriates-100th-report and https://football-observatory.com/IMG/sites/mr/mr79/en/
  • Height spreads by about 6 centimetres within a league; goalkeepers average about 190 centimetres and attacking midfielders about 178; leagues differ, from over 184 centimetres in the Bundesliga to about 181 in La Liga; and English top-flight players grew about one centimetre per decade from the 1970s. https://football-observatory.com/IMG/sites/mr/mr79/en/ and https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16208292/ and https://www.port.ac.uk/news-events-and-blogs/news/elite-footballers-leaner-taller-faster-than-decades-ago
  • Among professionals, birth quarters run 31, 26, 24, and 20 percent, a milder lean than in academies; early-born professionals have no higher transfer values; and in one small Spanish study every player who reached a top-five league was a late maturer, so late developers who make it are a small group with high upside. https://journals.plos.org/plosone/article?id=10.1371%2Fjournal.pone.0192209 and https://www.mdpi.com/2411-5142/10/2/153
  • About 79 percent of players at the 1998 World Cup were right-footed and very few used both feet equally; about 16 percent of players at the 2022 World Cup were born outside the country they played for, with more than 45 percent in the Moroccan, Tunisian, and Senegalese squads. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11695507/ and https://gulfnews.com/qatar-world-cup-2022/teams/fifa-world-cup-qatar-2022-moroccos-foreign-born-contingent-deliver-1.92639526
  • No measured rate exists for a professional parent or sibling, for players who play several positions, for very tall or very short outfield players, or for national playing styles; birthplace size has an inconsistent effect, population density a clearer one, and academy players come more often from higher-income families. https://jhk.termedia.pl/Birthplace-Effect-in-Soccer-A-Systematic-Review,186935,0,2.html and https://www.easm.net/download/2008/7a9ea389e692566e38fcba6483d8ae3a.pdf
  • Registration rules differ by competition: UEFA's club competitions allow 25 players on List A with 8 places for locally trained players (at least 4 club-trained, meaning three seasons between 15 and 21), plus an unlimited List B for young players; the Premier League allows 25 plus under-21s with at least 8 home-grown; Serie A 25 plus under-22s with 8 trained in Italy; the Bundesliga requires 8 locally trained players and 12 Germans with no non-EU limit; LaLiga allows 3 non-EU players and ties registration to a squad cost limit; Ligue 1 allows 4 non-EU players. https://www.uefa.com/uefachampionsleague/news/02a8-216c54c7cf25-c163bc643149-1000--uefa-champions-league-league-phase-player-lists-registration/ and https://www.premierleague.com/en/news/4706140/what-are-the-rules-on-premier-league-squad-lists and https://www.bundesliga-gruppe.de/glossar/local-player-regelung/
  • England's work permits since 2021 follow a points system with up to four exception places for top-two-division clubs; foreign-player limits change over time and by country, for example Turkey moved between 5, 8, 11, and no matchday limit between 2014 and 2025, Russia rose from 8 to 13 registered foreigners in 2022, and the Netherlands sets a minimum salary for non-EU players instead of a quota. https://www.thefa.com/-/media/files/thefaportal/governance-docs/registrations/gbe-2025-26/fa-mens-players-gbe-criteria-202526.ashx and https://www.aa.com.tr/tr/futbol/-turk-futbolunda-son-15-yildaki-yabanci-oyuncu-kurallari/1905742
  • About 55 percent of outfield players in the top five leagues play one position group and just under 30 percent play two, measured from real appearances; rating data suggests most midfielders are versatile, centre-backs and strikers much less so, and versatility peaks at ages 24 to 26. https://www.statsandsnakeoil.com/2020/08/10/finding-the-most-versatile-player/ and the FIFA 22 player ratings (people's ratings, not measurements)
  • Evidence for national playing styles is weak: league styles are measured (the Premier League most direct, the Bundesliga the most counter-attacks), but they describe leagues, not nationality; rating databases show small differences by nationality of 1 to 6 points, which may reflect raters' stereotypes and which players move abroad. https://www.researchgate.net/publication/333330546 and https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1080/17461391.2010.481334 and the FIFA 22 player ratings
  • No base rate exists for a professional parent or sibling: elite athletes are more than 2.3 times as likely as others to have a father who played competitive sport, and famous sons make up roughly 1 percent of World Cup 2026 squads, a lower bound. https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1080/17461391.2018.1551424 and https://bolavip.com/en/world-cup/how-many-sons-of-former-players-are-participating-at-the-2026-world-cup

The manager's week

exploredproblem side

information, time, team talks, and delegation

Team talks move mood and focus; delegation works the same in every area; information comes through a filtered inbox, reports, and escalations, and some messages need a reply by a deadline. Time is limited by the week's structure: training slots, one briefing per match, team talks, press conferences, a few one-to-one talks, a staff meeting, and trips; no hour budget and no burnout. A task done personally uses the manager's attributes and builds bonds. Core: all of it. Scope: keep all, in the first version.

Decisions 5
  • The manager's week is part of the game: how reports, messages, and decisions reach the manager, the calendar of training, travel, and press, and limits on the manager's time. Why: Every new system this session sends something to the manager, so the flow of information needs its own design.
  • Delegation works the same way everywhere: every decision area can be delegated, and the player sets how much to hand over. This joins the separate delegation choices for staff hiring, set-piece design, contract talks, press conferences, and youth decisions into one model. Why: Five separate delegation rules would confuse the player.
  • The manager's week must fix four problems: an inbox flooded with noise, time that costs nothing, days with no rhythm of match day, recovery, training, and travel, and events whose reasons stay hidden. Why: Each makes the week feel unreal.
  • Information reaches the manager through a daily inbox that the player can filter, weekly or monthly reports from delegated work, and urgent issues that staff escalate at once. Why: It keeps the inbox familiar while cutting the flood at the source.
  • The reasons behind events are visible in a mixed way, as in the dressing room: some causes show at once, some come through staff, and some the manager must work out. Why: The same rule across the game lets the manager learn without being told everything.
Open ideas 1
  • Time is spent as a few weekly focus choices, such as leading one training session, a scouting trip, or a meeting with a player, while staff cover the rest; long trips block days. A budget of hours would feel like bookkeeping.
Time and the week, session 8 4
  • The manager's time is limited by the structure of the week, as in Football Manager: a fixed number of training session slots, one team briefing per match, and similar fixed moments. There is no budget of hours and no burnout of the manager. Why: The person wants the limits of the real week's structure, not bookkeeping. core
  • When the manager does a task personally instead of handing it to staff, the task uses the manager's own attributes from the shared list, better or worse than the staff member's, and bonds with the players and staff involved form faster. core
  • The week's fixed slots are: training sessions per day and week, which match days, travel, and recovery days reduce; one briefing per match and one team talk before, at half-time, and after each match; the press conferences each competition requires; a limited number of one-to-one meetings per week, a weekly staff meeting, and trips that block days. Some inbox messages need a reply by a deadline, and a message left unanswered goes to the staff default or lapses. core
  • One-to-one talks are limited three ways: the manager has a few routine talks per week and the assistant handles the rest by default; talking to the same player again soon loses effect and can annoy him; and talks that an event opens, such as a request, a dip in form, or a contract issue, do not use a weekly slot. core

Action resolution and the probability layer

exploredproblem and solution side

probabilities, shots, passes, duels, errors, variance

Each contact (tackle, first touch, keeper handling, strike, pass, interception) draws from the situation and the attributes; physics carries the ball between contacts. A strike takes two draws; a keeper's reach is physics and the handling a draw; penalties add a guessing game. Duels run in two steps with four outcomes. Errors come from draw tails, lapses, and named events. Checks: xG buckets by a real-data model, shot split, pass curves, duel set, Opta errors, and variance with real and design targets. Core: all of it. Scope: keep all.

Decisions 17
  • The xG-bucket check judges the engine with an xG model fitted on public real shots, reading distance, angle, body part, shot type, and pressure, independent of the engine. Why: The engine must not mark its own homework. Accepted risk: The event contract and the test-only output must carry body part, shot type, and pressure for every shot. core
  • A probability is drawn at each contact, the moment a player touches the ball or the ball meets a player: the tackle, the first touch, the keeper's handling, and the quality of a strike or a pass. Each draw comes from a chance or a spread built from the situation and the attributes. Between contacts, flight, deflections, bodies in the lane, and the keeper's position are physics. Why: Real data measures outcomes at contacts, so they calibrate directly, and physics between them keeps blocks and interceptions emergent; the xG buckets then check the whole chain. Accepted risk: Two systems to tune, and a bad xG bucket can come from either one. core
  • A penalty mixes a guessing game with an ordinary strike: the taker picks a side, the keeper guesses with habits the other side can learn, and the strike's error and the keeper's reach then apply as for any shot. core
  • Three checks guard against shots that are too accurate: the xG-bucket check, a band on the split of wide, blocked, and on-target shots, and conversion by distance. core
  • A keeper's save mixes physics and draws: the keeper's position and dive decide whether the ball can be reached; only then a draw decides hold, parry, or fumble from the shot's speed and closeness and the keeper's handling, with composure under pressure; a small third draw sets a parry's direction, weighted away from goal by a good keeper. One-on-ones set how the keeper narrows the angle; rushing out and command of area act on crosses and through balls. core
  • A strike takes two draws: first a quality (clean, scuff, slice), which pressure or a bad touch can force, then the error in aim, height, and power inside that quality, from finishing, technique, composure, pressure, body part, and the weaker foot. Accepted risk: Two draws per shot to calibrate, and more work on each tick with a shot. core
  • A pass is a contact whose draw adds an error to direction, weight, and height, from passing and technique, the pass type, pressure on the passer, the weaker foot, and body shape; vision and decisions act earlier, in the choice. When a defender in the lane can reach the ball, a draw from anticipation and positioning decides whether the defender cuts it out. The receiver's run sets where the ball is met, and receiving is its own first-touch draw from first touch, ball speed and height, and pressure at receiving. core
  • The pass model is checked by completion by pass kind, by length, and by pressure at receiving, and by how often players choose the safe open receiver compared with real players. core
  • A duel runs in two steps: physics decides the approach, the angle and closing speed from pace, acceleration, and positions; a first draw decides whether the defender commits or holds off, from decisions and aggression; then the contest draw reads the attacker's dribbling, agility, acceleration, balance, and strength against the defender's tackling, positioning, anticipation, and aggression, with the angle, the closing speed, and the local numbers. Accepted risk: Two draws per duel, and duels are the most common contest in a match. core
  • A duel ends in one of four outcomes: the attacker keeps the ball, the defender wins it clean, the ball runs loose, or a foul. Why: When a defender wins, possession changes only about half the time, so loose balls are a real phase. core
  • The duel model is checked by win rate by local numbers, the foul share of duels (about 11 percent), dribble success by third, and the turnover split after each winner. core
  • Errors come from three sources: the far tails of every contact draw, whose spread widens with pressure, fatigue, low composure, and low consistency; rare concentration lapses that grow late in a match; and a small set of named error events (a short back pass, a keeper spilling a cross, a slip), each with its own chance, so commentary and reports can name them. Accepted risk: Three sources to calibrate; each takes a fixed share of the band. core
  • The realism test bands errors on Opta's definition, with a wide band: errors leading to goals at 0.25 to 0.45 a match (8 to 15 percent of goals), keepers about a quarter of them, and a team spread that depends on quality and composure. It adds a check on high turnovers: 11 to 17 a match, 15 to 17 percent ending in a shot. Why: It is the public, per-team measure fans see, and the season-to-season swing is large. Accepted risk: High turnovers mix pressing wins with errors, so a failure there may point at the pressing model rather than at errors. core
  • The season variance bands (the home, draw, and away split, the share of points spread explained by quality, top-quarter against bottom-half results, and Poisson goals) each carry the real target and a design target with a little more upset; the test passes on the design target and reports the distance from real football. Why: The wide world spread of ability was chosen on purpose and gives more upsets than real football. core
  • Team strength is measured two ways for the season check: a strength from the ratings of each club's best eleven must predict points, and an Elo-style rating built from the simulated results must match real Elo spreads between clubs and tiers. core
  • The error sources stay separate and split the error band: the tails of the contact draws, concentration lapses, and the named error events each get a fixed share of the errors leading to goals, each is tuned to its share, and the report counts each source apart. Accepted risk: Three shares to set and tune, and a named event must never also appear as a tail draw of the same kind. core
  • Every player carries every attribute. Keepers use the outfield attributes wherever they fit: the dive reads agility, the stance reads positioning, tackling joins rushing out, and keepers can dribble, score, and show flair. Every outfield player carries hidden goalkeeper attributes, generally poor, set at creation and never developed. Accepted risk: An agile outfield player in goal dives like a keeper, limited only by the hidden poor keeper attributes. core
Open ideas 2
  • Shot outcomes follow an xG model from distance, angle, body part, shot type, defensive pressure, and the shooter's and keeper's abilities: penalties about 75 to 80 percent, most shots from outside the box under 5 percent, and about 10 percent overall.
  • Keepers carry an eccentricity, a hidden tendency that sets how often a keeper dribbles, comes far out, or tries the unusual.
Findings 6
  • A shot today is a physical kick: the shooter aims at a spot whose width grows with finishing, noise shrinks with finishing, and the goal comes from the ball's flight and the keeper's reach. The keeper holds a fast ball with one flat chance of 86 percent, with no keeper attribute in it. crates/engine/src/decision.rs:381; crates/engine/src/sim.rs:972; content/tuning.json:23
  • The engine computes an xG for each shot from distance and angle alone, and uses it only for the match report; it never decides an outcome. crates/engine/src/sim.rs:440; crates/engine/src/sim.rs:877
  • A tackle today is a small chance on each tick for each opponent within reach, 5 percent times tackling divided by tackling plus dribbling. It reads no acceleration, agility, positioning, anticipation, or angle of approach. crates/engine/src/sim.rs:992
  • Opta counts an error as one on-the-ball mistake, or a keeper's spill or failed claim, that directly leads to a shot or a goal. In the Premier League, errors led to 91, 119, and 167 goals in 2022-23 to 2024-25: about 0.24 to 0.44 a match, or 8 to 15 percent of all goals. Teams range from 1 to 18 a season, and keepers make about a quarter of them, about 0.1 a match. https://theanalyst.com/articles/opta-football-stats-definitions; https://www.statmuse.com/fc/ask/premier-league-team-errors-leading-to-goals-2024-25
  • All error events, leading to a shot or a goal, run at about 0.6 to 0.9 a match in both Opta and StatsBomb data, and roughly 35 to 45 percent of them end in a goal. https://github.com/hudl/open-data/blob/master/doc/StatsBomb%20Open%20Data%20Specification%20v1.1.pdf; https://www.statmuse.com/fc/ask/premier-league-team-errors-leading-to-shots-2023-24
  • High turnovers, possessions that start within 40 metres of the opponent's goal, run at 11 to 17 a match in the Premier League, 15 to 17 percent end in a shot, and they give 0.2 to 0.33 goals a match; many are won by pressing rather than given away. https://theanalyst.com/articles/numbers-behind-premier-league-goal-explosion

Machine load and save size

exploredproblem side

the cost of the realism additions on the player's machine

The game promises to run well on an ordinary laptop, with fixed limits the player feels and a relative limit per match in the tests. No realism lever is pulled; when the budget fails, engineering optimises first and comes back only if it still fails. Open: the limit numbers, for the plan.

Decisions 4
  • The game promises to run well on an ordinary laptop, about 4 cores and 8 GB of memory. Why: The widest audience. Accepted risk: Tight limits on matchday time, save creation, save size, and memory, against a world of full people, every pair, and full recent events.
  • The budget has two forms: fixed limits for what the player feels (matchday wait, save creation time, save size, memory) on the reference laptop, and a relative limit per match against the shipped engine as an early warning in the tests.
  • No realism lever is pulled to meet the budget: background matches keep the full engine, the active leagues stay as the player chooses, people and pairs stay full records, and every match keeps its events. Accepted risk: On an ordinary laptop the budget is very likely to fail, since today's engine already takes about 20 seconds per league season on eight cores and this session multiplied the work per tick and the data per save.
  • When the budget fails, engineering gives way first: the team optimises (parallel matches, compact storage, cheaper maths) until the budget holds, and returns to you only if it still fails; no realism is cut before then. Accepted risk: An open-ended amount of optimisation work.

Realism calibration and validation

exploredsolution side

per-match targets, distributions, league tables, divisions, the harness

Targets from five recent seasons in three layers: per league with data, per tier, and banding groups for the rest; a division's style emerges from its players. Tolerance is the real spread plus sampling noise. Every scoreline and whole distributions are checked, and the league table in full. Each change runs a few thousand matches; each release a full run; every report is stored and compared. Leagues without data sit in banding groups fed by the tier formula. Core: all of it. Scope: keep all.

Decisions 7
  • A division's style emerges from its players and their tactics, with nothing set per division by hand; the bands check the result per division. core
  • The season check bands the league table in full, per league, with real and design targets: champion points, the points of the relegated places, the spread of points per game, and the title margin; both the full engine and the fast model must pass. core
  • Each band's tolerance is the real spread of the statistic across leagues and seasons, widened by the sampling noise of the batch size, so a small quick run does not fail by chance while a full run is judged tightly. core
  • The distribution check compares the share of every scoreline (0-0, 1-0, 1-1, up to 4-3 and beyond) with real shares, and compares the goals, shots, and cards totals as whole distributions. core
  • football-data.co.uk and FotMob's Opta pass figures are used for measuring only, under the same accepted risk as StatsBomb and Transfermarkt: the game ships none of the data, and each band records its source so it can be measured again elsewhere. Accepted risk: FotMob serves Opta data for display in its own app, not for reuse, so a complaint means measuring the pass bands again from open sets before release. core
  • Every league is banded, in three layers: each league with public data gets its own targets from five recent seasons, each tier gets a target set, and the other leagues fall into banding groups by tier and strength whose bands come from a formula fitted on the leagues with data. Shots count blocked shots, and pass completion and errors follow Opta's count.
