Accepted risk: The formula extrapolates, so a league unlike any measured one can get a wrong band and send tuning the wrong way. core
  • Each engine change runs a quick set of about 1,000 matches automatically, which judges goals and most per-match rates; rare-event bands, scorelines, and the table wait for the heavier runs. Every report is stored with its version and compared with the last, and a band that moves by more than its tolerance is flagged even when it still passes. Accepted risk: About 7 minutes of 8 cores per change at today's match cost, and the repository has no automated job to run it yet. core
Open ideas 1
  • Per-match targets for both teams together: about 2.6 to 2.9 goals, 24 to 26 shots, 8 to 9 on target, 9 to 11 corners, 20 to 24 fouls, 3.5 to 4.5 yellow cards, a red card in about one match in eight to ten, and pass completion about 78 to 85 percent.
Findings 6
  • The repository already has a calibration run: 1000 matches per sample over 17 bands, among them goals per match 2.4 to 3.2, goalless share 4 to 12 percent, shots on target share, corners, yellow cards, sending-off share, pass accuracy 75 to 88 percent, and goals per xG. The bands are still the wide first guesses, not the measured ones the board chose. content/realism-bands.json:1; crates/engine-cli/src/calibrate/mod.rs:39
  • The calibration report compares two arms of one run (a feature flag off and on); it keeps no history across engine versions, and no automated job reruns it after each change. crates/engine-cli/src/report/compare.rs:1
  • Across 2021-22 to 2025-26 the top five leagues average, per match for both teams: 2.83 goals, 25.2 shots including blocked shots, 8.8 on target, 23.8 fouls, 9.7 corners, 4.08 yellow cards, a red card in 16.8 percent of matches, 0-0 in 6.3 percent, a 43.6, 25.3, and 31.1 percent home, draw, and away split, and 82.0 percent pass completion on Opta's count. Leagues differ: LaLiga shows reds in 22 percent of matches, the Premier League in 11. https://www.football-data.co.uk/data.php; https://www.fotmob.com/leagues/47/stats/season/27110/teams/accurate_pass_team/premier-league-teams
  • Lower divisions pass less and play more directly, clearly in England: pass completion falls from 82.2 to 78.0, 72.2, and 69.3 percent down four tiers, passes per team from about 452 to 370, and the long-ball share rises from 4.7 to 7.4 percent. Continental second tiers pass only 2 to 4 points worse but draw more (26 to 32 percent) and see more 0-0s (up to about 10 percent). https://www.football-data.co.uk/data.php; https://data.fotmob.com/stats/
  • In 2021-22 to 2025-26 top-flight champions took about 2.3 points per game (84 to 95 points in 38 matches in England, Spain, and Italy), 18th place about 0.9, and the spread of points per game across a league was about 0.46; second and third tiers are flatter, at about 0.33. https://www.football-data.co.uk/data.php
  • The gap between 21 to 23 shots and about 25 is a counting rule, not a change in play: the football-data.co.uk count includes blocked shots, and its 2017-18 figure is 24.8 against Wyscout's 21.0 to 23.2 for the same season. Goals rose from 2.71 to 2.83 since 2017-18, and home wins fell by about 1.7 points after COVID. https://www.football-data.co.uk/data.php

Tactical counterplay and team-level AI

exploredproblem and solution side

costs, counters, squad fit, shape, transitions, AI managers

Costs, counters, and squad fit emerge from play through the players, with no table and no fit number. Rest defence follows duties, and line gaps emerge. A round-robin of eight named styles runs on every change and a setting grid before release; every preset needs a clear weakness. AI managers use staff advice and memory, carry a scoutable fingerprint, and are checked for fit and the possession rule. Paired squads check fit. Beyond the brief: the AI learns in the match and an exploit hunt runs before release; players manage games with instructions on top; touchline shouts; conditions change which tactic works. Core: all of it. Scope: keep all.

Decisions 14
  • Costs and counters emerge from play, with no counter table: a high line is exposed because a slow defender loses the race to a ball in behind, and a press tires players because pressing costs energy; the round-robin check proves the counters. Accepted risk: If a dominant tactic emerges, only a change to a mechanism can fix it, which is slower than editing a table, and a cost can come out too small to notice. core
  • Squad fit has no number of its own: each player carries out an instruction with their own attributes and state, so a slow, tired squad presses badly on its own; the fit check proves it. core
  • The round-robin plays about eight named styles (possession, low block, high press, counter-attack, direct, wing play, gegenpress, park the bus) with identical squads, every pairing home and away, on every change, and a grid over the settings themselves (line height, press, tempo, width, directness) before each release. Accepted risk: The setting grid adds many pairings to each release run. core
  • A tactic dominates, and the check fails, when a preset loses clearly to no other preset or takes more than a set share of all points in the round-robin; every preset must have at least one clear weakness. core
  • Rest defence has no setting of its own: the players held back while the team attacks follow from the mentality and each player's duty. core
  • The gaps between the lines and the team's width emerge from where each role stands, moved by the block law; no instruction sets a target gap. Accepted risk: An instruction may change the gaps too little to notice, which only the tactic sensitivity check will catch. core
  • An AI manager judges a tactic two ways: before each match it reads the same noisy staff advice the human gets (fit estimates and the analysts' opposition report), weighed by its philosophy and attributes; over seasons it remembers which tactics worked for it and against whom, and shifts towards them. core
  • Each AI manager has a tactical fingerprint, a recognisable preferred style and habits such as switching to a back five when leading late; analysts reveal it, and a hidden adaptability sets how often the manager breaks habit. core
  • The AI check requires AI managers to pick tactics that suit their squads more often than not, and AI-run clubs to reproduce the measured possession rule over a season. core
  • The fit check builds, for each named style, a suitable squad and an unsuitable one of the same overall level (for example fast, fit pressers against slow ones for the high press), plays both against the same opponents, and requires a clear gap in points and in the style's own statistics. core
  • Two guards stop a human exploit of the AI: an AI manager notices during the match what is hurting it, for example repeated balls in behind, and answers with the counter the round-robin shows, within its fingerprint and adaptability; and before each release an automated exploit hunt plays many tactics against the AI and fails the build if any tactic wins far above its expected rate. Accepted risk: The exploit hunt adds a large search to each release run, and in-match learning adds AI work during the match. core
  • Players manage a game by default from the score, the clock, and their personality (wasting time, tactical fouls, dropping deep), and the manager's instructions push it up or down, each with its cost in cards and pressure. core
  • The manager has a small set of touchline shouts (encourage, demand more, calm down, focus) that move the mind family of effective ability for a while, with a cost when overused. core
  • Conditions change which tactic works, and it emerges: a press in heat drains energy faster, a poor pitch worsens short passes and touches, and wind moves long balls; the round-robin runs under several conditions to prove it. Accepted risk: The round-robin grows by the number of conditions it runs under. core
Findings 2
  • The calibration run already plays every pairing of formations against each other (up to 136 pairings), but it judges each pairing only for realism; it never checks whether one formation beats all the others. crates/engine-cli/src/calibrate/mod.rs:399; crates/engine-cli/src/report/compare.rs:29
  • Each team instruction today is a number that scales one behaviour, for example a high line moves the block up by 5 metres; no instruction carries a designed cost, and nothing reads the squad's fit to it. content/tactics.json:76

Match engine structure

exploredsolution side

two models, phases, pitch, clock, seeded replay

The full engine runs every active-league match; a fast model runs the rest, fitted from the full engine after each change and checked by season bands and per pairing. A written phase machine with a position check at each restart. Grounds differ in size within the laws. Added time prices each event kind, video reviews included. Replay is identical on every supported machine. Beyond the brief: a skip finishes on the fast model from the current state; background matches run first and reveal their events in step; every player thinks every tick; a mid-match save finishes on its old engine. Core: all of it. Scope: keep all.

Decisions 11
  • The fast model must pass the same season bands as the full engine, and on a fixed fixture batch it must match the full engine per pairing of team strengths: win, draw, and loss shares and goals within the same tolerance. Why: A strong team must not do better in one model while the league totals still pass. core
  • The fast model's numbers are fitted from full-engine results after each engine change, so it follows the engine automatically, and the equality check runs with the harness. Accepted risk: Each engine change needs enough full-engine matches to refit the fast model, which adds to the harness run. core
  • The engine has a written state machine for open play, each dead-ball kind, set pieces, stoppages, half-time, and full-time, with entry and exit conditions, and a check that every player stands in a legal, sensible place at each restart, for example the opponents outside the box at a goal kick and the shape restored before play resumes. core
  • Each ground has its own length and width within the laws, real grounds take their real size where known, and the size emerges in play, for example a narrow pitch helps a low block. Accepted risk: The bands must hold across pitch sizes, and a ground far from 105 by 68 can move a club's statistics away from its league's band. core
  • Added time keeps pricing each kind of event at a set number of seconds, with video reviews added as a kind, a small random variance, and the cap. core
  • A match replays identically on every supported machine and operating system: the engine avoids maths that differs between machines, and a test replays a recorded match on each supported system. Accepted risk: Some fast maths is off limits, which works against the laptop budget. core
  • When the player skips to the result mid-match, the fast model finishes the match from the current state: the score, the clock, the cards, and the fatigue. Accepted risk: The fast model must match the full engine from any match state, not only from kick-off, or a result will depend on whether the player watched. core
  • All background matches of a matchday run as fast as the machine allows, before or during the player's match, and their events are revealed to the player at the moment each one happens on the player's match clock. core
  • A mid-match save made before a game update finishes the match on the engine version it started with, and the season then continues on the new version. Accepted risk: Each release must ship the previous engine version beside the new one, for as long as unfinished matches from it may exist. core
  • A skipped match may give slightly different odds than a watched one; the game accepts that gap, and the equality check measures it from sampled mid-match states and reports it. Accepted risk: A result can depend a little on whether the player watched, which your brief ruled out. core
  • Each player and each kind of action draws from its own random stream, so a change to one draw does not reshuffle the rest of the match, and paired seeds work for every sensitivity check. Replay stays identical on every machine. Accepted risk: The engine's random layer changes, and every recorded replay and baseline is regenerated once. core
Open ideas 1
  • A positional tick-based engine runs the matches the player watches, and a lighter statistical model runs background fixtures.
Findings 4
  • The pitch is fixed at 105 by 68 metres as constants; no ground can differ in size. crates/engine/src/pitch.rs:7
  • Added time is already built from events: the rule pack prices each kind of stoppage and each card in seconds, a small random variance is added, and the total is clamped between a floor and a cap. The video referee does not exist yet, so its checks add nothing. crates/engine/src/rules/clock.rs:28; crates/engine/src/rules/clock.rs:44
  • The same seed and build replay byte-identical tick files on the same machine; the test does not cover another machine or another operating system. crates/engine/tests/determinism.rs:1
  • Dead-ball phases exist as stoppage kinds (kick-off, throw-in, corner, goal kick, penalty, free kick, and a drop ball after an injury), each with a delay and a chosen taker; no written state machine names the entry and exit of every phase. crates/engine/src/rules/restart.rs:56

Cohesion across areas

exploredproblem and solution side

the decisions against each other and against the design documents

Raised in session seven when you asked to bring the board into cohesion. The pass read the three design documents against session seven, compared the new areas with the earlier ones, and priced the checks. It merged overlapping decisions, applied the later decision where two disagreed, and brought four real conflicts to you. Three are settled: errors split their budget between sources, tactic familiarity lives with the player and the team value is derived, and the style expectation blends identity with the owner type. The fourth was the check load: about 1.5 million matches for a release run. It is settled: about 1,000 matches on each change, split random streams so paired seeds work, early stopping, only the touched parts of the heavy checks at each milestone, and a full release run overnight on the Mac mini and the VPS. An engine emulator, fitted on spread-out full-engine runs, now tunes settings, screens sensitivity, and triages each milestone, and the full engine still judges every band.

Decisions 4
  • The board studies two ways to shrink the check load: paired seeds, where the normal batch and the changed batch replay the same seeds, and early stopping, where a batch ends as soon as its result is clearly a pass or a fail. core
  • Each milestone runs only the parts of the round-robin and the sensitivity family that its changes could affect, with paired seeds and early stopping. Everything runs in full before each release. Accepted risk: A change can reach a part the milestone skipped, and only the release run then catches it. core
  • A full release run must finish in about 12 hours on the Mac mini and the VPS together. When it does not, the run shrinks through pairing, early stopping, and sampling before any machine is rented. Accepted risk: If the realism work slows each match several times, overnight may need sampling that leaves some checks thinner before a release. core
  • An engine emulator, separate from the fast model, is fitted on a spread-out design of a few hundred input points with about 200 full-engine matches each, and predicts the mean and spread of every banded statistic from attribute levels, tactical settings, and engine constants. It has three roles: a tuning tool that proposes settings the full engine then confirms, a sensitivity screen that ranks which inputs move which statistics so the engine confirms only the ranked results, and milestone triage that predicts which bands a change moves so the milestone runs those parts. Accepted risk: It is reliable only inside its trained ranges, cannot find emergent effects such as a dominant tactic or an AI exploit, and is refitted after each engine change that moves the numbers, about 60,000 to 100,000 matches each time. A band always counts only on the full engine. core
Findings 7
  • The checks chosen in session seven cost far more than the harness plans for. One match costs about 0.43 seconds of processor time on the reference desktop today. The eight-style round-robin needs about 39,000 matches (about 35 minutes on 8 cores), the sensitivity family about 830,000 (about 12 hours), and a full release run about 1.5 million (about 22 hours), before the realism work makes each match slower. .ai/workflows/football-manager-match-engine/05c-benchmark.md:128
  • The player's matchday is light beside the harness: three active leagues of ten matches cost about 13 processor-seconds today, about 3 seconds on a 4-core laptop, so even a several-fold slower engine keeps a matchday wait short. The heavy load of session seven falls on the checks, not on the player's machine. .ai/workflows/football-manager-match-engine/05c-benchmark.md:128
  • The three design documents predate session seven and disagree with it in 18 places. The largest: style from three layers where identity now acts off the pitch; personality traits on 1 to 100 where every rating is now 1 to 20 in tenths; DFL definitions for every band where passes and errors now follow Opta; 2017-18 Wyscout targets where targets are now five recent seasons per league and tier; an unchecked fast model; season bands that pass on real values; 1000 calibration matches; a noisy view of the manager's own squad; consistency as a visible attribute; and ball-local thinking where every player now thinks every tick. The later decision wins in each case, and every piece of work is marked stale. docs/design/realism/01-engine-realism.md; docs/design/realism/02-event-contract-redesign.md; docs/design/realism/03-season-layer.md
  • Session seven depends on four things no document designs: how the ten position ratings map to about 40 roles; how saves and engine versions are numbered so that a mid-match save finishes on its old engine; event kinds for named errors, lapses, and video reviews, and body part, shot type, and pressure on every shot; and the storage of the effective-ability layer, relationships, and every pair. docs/design/realism/01-engine-realism.md:337; docs/design/realism/02-event-contract-redesign.md:420
  • Whether 50 or 100 matches is enough depends on how often the thing happens. With 100 matches, mean goals has a standard error of about 0.17, so only a shift of about a third of a goal shows; the goalless share (6.3 percent) has an error of about 2.4 points and the red-card share about 3.7, so those bands cannot be judged at all. Statistics with many events per match, such as passes and pass completion, are judged well at 100. A one-attribute change on one player moves team goals by a few hundredths, so it needs tens of thousands of unpaired matches. Poisson and binomial standard errors on the session-7 targets (2.83 goals, 6.3 percent goalless, 16.8 percent with a red card)
  • Paired seeds only remove noise if one change does not reshuffle every later random draw. In a tick engine, one different draw changes the whole rest of the match unless each player and each kind of action draws from its own random stream. The engine replays from one seed today, so paired seeds need split streams first. crates/engine/tests/determinism.rs:1
  • The checks can run on a Mac mini and a VPS. Because a match replays identically on every supported machine, one batch can be split across both and the reports still agree. the person's answer in session 7
Settled in the session 8 pass 10
  • The briefing acts on all three: each player's picture of his direct opponent, how faithfully players follow the opposition targets, and mood and focus. This amends the rule that no instruction lands weaker because of the manager, for opposition targets through the briefing. core
  • Work rate and tolerance of long hours apply to AI managers and to staff who become managers, never to the human manager, whose time is limited only by the structure of the week. core
  • Managers carry a hidden value named tactical flexibility that sets how often a manager breaks habit; adaptability keeps its one meaning, settling in a new country. core
  • A delegated hire is judged by the department head, and the director's coach appraisal corrects part of that error when the club has a director. core
  • AI clubs copy what wins two ways: an existing manager drifts a little inside its fingerprint, as far as its tactical flexibility allows and weighed against the club's identity, and new managers arrive carrying the current trend more fully. core
  • The players' combined trait ambition and loyalty splits into two traits for everyone, loyalty to the club and ambition for a bigger stage, so players carry thirteen traits; staff add loyalty to a manager and ambition for the top job as their own. Accepted risk: One more trait for every player, and the design documents' trait list changes again. core
  • Each staff member carries a list of regions and leagues with a level for each, like the languages list: it starts from where the person was born and has worked, and grows by working there. core
  • A player leaks the plan two ways: the controversy trait with low mood or a grudge against the manager sets a player's chance to leak, and an unhappy player also leaks through his agent to the press, set by the agent's own traits. core
  • A squad holds only a few tactics well, a limit set by tactical coaching, and a player's grasp of a tactic the team stops using fades, as routine familiarity already does. core
  • The engine emulator is also fitted on season runs, so it screens the 32 staff attributes, and only the attributes it flags run their full season-long check in the release run. Accepted risk: The emulator needs season-run training data, which adds its own fitting runs before it can screen. core
Questions for the plan 4
  • How a compact summary of each manager's fingerprint is kept for every season, since full match detail is kept only while recent and the AI reads the fingerprint over seasons.
  • Whether rewind and replays re-run the engine or store positions, given that a replay must survive an engine or protocol update and that stored positions cost save size.
  • How bonds and knowledge of players are stored for staff, since the relationship pairs are defined for players and staff change costs injuries through empty bonds.
  • How coaching licence requirements enter each competition's dated rule pack.

Player decision-making and off-ball movement

exploredproblem and solution side

options, perception, time under pressure, runs, defending, traits

Raised in session seven from your sixth brief. The engine today scores five options for the carrier, who sees everything, and moves everyone else to a formation anchor with no runs and no defensive jobs. The board now sets: a picture per player refreshed by timed scans, holding motion and team-mates' calls; decision time before a choice and commit time after it; the whole 53-action catalogue plus game extras, scored as expected value with weights fitted to real choices, and a poor decision-maker going wrong through noisy estimates and a soft pick; off-ball movement as anchor, role patterns, and utility, with runners sharing space by patterns, claims, calls, and space value; defending as a utility inside the team's marking system, with claims and calls; transitions as utility inside a counter-press window; keepers on the same machinery with the measured depth rule; instructions as limits and weights, followed as faithfully as a player's decisions and teamwork allow; traits that bias and style actions and show in reports; players near the ball thinking every tick and others at a human rate; and shape, crowding, behaviour, frozen-player, and crowded-pass bands. Core: all of it. Scope: keep all.

Decisions 19
  • Each player keeps a picture of the pitch that scans refresh. Vision and anticipation set how often a player scans and how wide each scan reaches; a player out of view is remembered where the player was last seen, so a stale picture causes real mistakes such as a pass to a team-mate who has moved. Accepted risk: Twenty-two pictures to keep and update on every tick, and replay must stay identical on every machine. core
  • Pressure acts twice. Before the choice, a player needs a decision time set by decisions and first touch; after it, a set time to carry the action out, for example setting the body for a long pass. A presser who arrives inside either time hurries the player into a wider error, which composure narrows, or blocks the action. Accepted risk: Two timers per player with the ball, and many more held or blocked actions to calibrate against the measured losses under pressure. core
  • Off-ball movement has three layers: an anchor from the shape and the role; role patterns for the named runs, each with a trigger; and an off-ball utility that picks among the runs and the anchor and adds support angles and space seeking. Accepted risk: The largest new piece of work in the area, and a new per-tick load for every player without the ball. core
  • The team's marking system (zonal, man-to-man, or a mix) is a tactical setting that sets each defender's default, and a defensive utility picks each player's job within it on every tick: press, cover, track a runner, mark, or hold the line. Accepted risk: A second utility for every player without the ball, and the system must still reproduce the measured handoffs instead of long man-marking. core
  • A player's picture holds where each seen player stood and where that player was heading, so a passer can lead a runner seen during the run and a defender can read a run early. Team-mates also call: a runner calls for the ball and a team-mate warns 'man on', and a call reaches a player without a scan. Communication sets how often and how well a player calls, which gives that attribute a job in the match. Accepted risk: Calls add a message layer between players, and a call that arrives late or is wrong must be modelled too. core
  • The carrier's options grow beyond shoot, pass to feet, dribble, clear, and hold: passes into space (a lead pass and a through ball onto a run); several carry paths scored apart; crosses (driven, floated, cut-back) and switches of play; and body options (turn, shield, hold up the ball, draw a foul, a one-two, and skill moves gated by technique and agility). More options are wanted beyond these. Accepted risk: Many more options to score for the carrier on every tick, each with its own contact draw to calibrate. core
  • An option's score is expected value: the chance of success times the value gained, minus the chance of losing the ball times the danger of losing it there. Its weights and the risk appetite are fitted so that the engine's choices match the real choice model, which weighs completion, a running receiver, and a forward option far above threat. Instruction, role, and personality move the risk appetite. core
  • A poor decision-maker goes wrong two ways together: the player's estimate of each option's chance and value is noisy, with the noise shrinking as decisions rises, and the pick is a soft choice weighted towards the best score, so a poor decision-maker often takes the second or third option. Composure widens both under pressure. core
  • The option list is the whole catalogue of 53 actions, each family with its own measured outcome curve, plus the actions a manager game needs that no provider labels, such as time-wasting at the corner flag, a deliberate foul to stop a counter, and a quick free kick. Accepted risk: The largest option set in the design, each action with a contact draw and a band, and the unlabelled extras are checked only by watching. core
  • A trait can bias a choice, such as shooting from distance, and set a signature way of doing an action, such as cutting inside onto the stronger foot or chipping the keeper one-on-one. Match reports count how often each player used each trait, so the manager and the analysts can spot it. core
  • A player's scan rate comes from vision and anticipation, falling under close pressure and near the opponent's goal, and the player chooses when each scan happens: good players scan just before receiving, and a scan while the ball is arriving risks the first touch. Accepted risk: A scan-timing choice for every player on every tick, and the scan rate has only one public source to band against. core
  • Defenders share jobs by claims: each scores the jobs alone, and a player who claims a job makes team-mates lower their score for it, so two players rarely press the same attacker. Players also call to each other, such as 'I've got him' and 'push up', and communication and leadership decide how well calls land, so a quiet back line coordinates worse. Accepted risk: Coordination emerges rather than being assigned, so the no-clustering bands and the watched review must catch a back line that fails to organise. core
  • Attackers share space and time their runs three ways together: the role patterns carry the known pairings, such as the winger going wide when the full-back underlaps; for the free choices a runner claims a space or a run and team-mates lower their score for it, and runners call for the ball; and a space a team-mate holds or is running into is worth less. Off-ball movement and anticipation time a run against the offside line. core
  • Two bands guard against passes through crowds beside the interception draw: pass completion by the number of opponents near the lane, and how often players choose a pass whose lane holds opponents, against the real choice model. Accepted risk: The lane-crowding measure must be computed from tracking first, and the watched review does not name it as a separate item. core
  • Four band families join the realism test: shape (width and depth by phase, the three line heights by the ball's distance from goal, the gaps between lines, and players goal-side of the ball); crowding (players within 5, 10, and 15 metres of the ball, even between the teams, and the share of frames with at most one player near the ball); behaviour (the run mix by kind, time on the ball and losses by pressure, defender handoffs, the goal-side share, and PPDA); and a new frozen-player band on how long an outfielder stays still or out of the play, measured from tracking. Accepted risk: Most of these come from 7 DFL and 20 SkillCorner matches, and the frozen-player measure does not exist yet. core
  • Players near the ball or in a duel think on every tick; the others think at a human rate set by decisions and anticipation, about every 0.1 to 0.3 seconds, and any new event (a pass, a touch, a call) wakes a player at once. Movement still updates on every tick. Accepted risk: A player far from the ball reacts a little later than one near it, and the think rate must never change the result of a replay. core
  • When the ball changes hands, the team's counter-press instruction sets a window and how aggressive it is, and inside the window each player's defensive utility, with claims and calls, picks counter-press or recover goal-side from distance to the ball, team-mates nearby, the danger behind, and the player's work rate and energy. core
  • The keeper uses the same picture, utility, and claims as outfield players, with keeper options (come for a cross or stay, sweep or hold, throw, roll, short pass, long kick, punt) and the keeper's attributes. The measured depth rule is the keeper's anchor, organisation of the wall and the line is calls from communication and command of area, and the keeper's choices are checked against the keeper bands. Accepted risk: Keeper organisation has no public data, so only watching checks it. core
  • A positional instruction, such as stay wide or hold the line, is a limit on where the player may go, and a habit can break it only under pressure or low mood. A choice instruction, such as shoot less or play direct, moves the scores of the options it names, and the player's decisions and teamwork set how faithfully the player follows it. The rule that an instruction is carried out as given now covers the limits only. Accepted risk: A player can drift from a choice instruction, which a manager may read as the game ignoring the instruction unless the match report names it. core
Findings 20
  • The ball carrier scores every team-mate between 4 and 45 metres whose passing lane is clear enough; nothing limits what the carrier notices. Vision only adds a bonus to forward passes, and one noise term from decisions and composure blurs every score. crates/engine/src/decision.rs:116; crates/engine/src/decision.rs:166
  • Pressure is one on-off flag, an opponent within 2.5 metres. The carrier re-decides on every tick with no time to decide, and holding the ball only loses a little score each second. crates/engine/src/decision.rs:138; crates/engine/src/decision.rs:216
  • A pass aims at the team-mate's current position with an error from passing skill; there is no lead pass into space and no through ball onto a run. crates/engine/src/decision.rs:272
  • Every player without the ball who is not pressing moves to a formation anchor: the formation slot, moved by block depth and duty, widened by the plan, and shifted with the ball by two fixed gains. The anchor is the same in and out of possession, and nobody makes a run, an overlap, a support angle, or a third-man move. crates/engine/src/team.rs:258; crates/engine/src/decision.rs:42
  • Out of possession, the nearest opponents within the pressing distance, as many as the instruction allows up to four, run straight at the ball and the rest hold their anchors. There is no cover, no tracking of runners, no marking, and the line is only the anchors. A loose ball draws the nearest player of each team. crates/engine/src/decision.rs:31; crates/engine/src/decision.rs:65
  • A dribble targets a point 8 metres toward goal and 4 metres away from the nearest opponent; there is no path choice and no move such as a turn, a feint, or cutting inside. crates/engine/src/decision.rs:299
  • Habits of play are decided on the board but nothing in the engine reads them; the only per-player difference in choices is the role's small offsets to shooting, progress, and dribbling. crates/engine/src/decision.rs:158; crates/engine/src/decision.rs:191
  • Team width is about 45 to 46 metres in possession and 35 to 37 out of it; across 52 team-halves the ranges are 39.0 to 52.8 and 32.4 to 39.7, so the phase gap of about 10 metres is a hard band. Depth is 34 to 36 metres in possession and 30 to 33 out of it, and width barely changes with the ball's side. .scratch/out/FINDINGS.md §12 (SkillCorner 20 matches; DFL 7 matches, 52 team-halves)
  • Each defending line sits at a set distance from its own goal that grows with the ball's distance d from that goal: the last line at 5.9 + 0.486 d metres, the midfield line at 15.6 + 0.611 d, and the front line at 30.1 + 0.591 d. The back-to-midfield gap is 8 to 18 metres and the midfield-to-front gap 12 to 16. .scratch/out/FINDINGS.md §12 (DFL 7 matches)
  • The defending team has 2.6 players goal-side of the ball when the ball is within 20 metres of its goal, rising to 9.3 when the ball is 88 metres or more away; 0.5 to 0.7 attackers stand beyond the last line. .scratch/out/FINDINGS.md §12 (DFL 7 matches)
  • Players rarely crowd the ball: of 20 outfielders, 1.62 are within 5 metres of the ball, 3.71 within 10, and 6.24 within 15; numbers near the ball are even between the teams; in 51.5 percent of live frames at most one player is within 5 metres; and 81 percent of outfielders are more than 10 metres away. .scratch/out/FINDINGS.md §13.3 (DFL, about 154,000 live frames)
  • The 495 off-ball runs a match split into: ahead of the ball 135.8, support 82.6, coming short 63.7, dropping off 54.8, cross receiver 46.8, in behind 37.4, pulling wide 32.2, overlap 15.0, pulling into the half-space 14.7, and underlap 11.6. Forwards make about 98 runs a match and wingers about 189. No public source counts third-man runs. .scratch/out/ACTIONS.md §8.1 (SkillCorner 20 matches)
  • A player keeps the ball for a median of about 1.2 seconds, with a 90th percentile of 3.3 to 3.7. With no pressure at receiving, 1.0 percent of receptions are lost, 5.6 percent are played first time, and 93.4 percent of passes complete; under very high pressure the figures are 32.6, 31.3, and 67.0 percent. Forwards hold the ball about 0.9 seconds per contact and keepers about 2.1. .scratch/out/gap_sc.txt; https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5503225/ (Bundesliga 2012-13, 69,667 ball intervals)
  • Players scan about 0.44 times a second before receiving, central midfielders most and forwards least, elite players up to 0.6 to 0.8. Scanning falls under close pressure (0.38 against 0.48 a second) and to near zero near the opponent's goal. More scanning goes with slightly higher completion and more long forward passes, a small effect next to ability. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7573254/ (Jordet and others 2020, 27 Premier League players, 9,574 possessions)
  • A fitted choice model picks the real pass target 46.9 percent of the time against 36 by chance, weighting the chance of completion, a receiver who is running, and an option ahead; threat barely counts, and players pick the most threatening option only 31.7 percent of the time. .scratch/out/gap_sc.txt (conditional logit on SkillCorner passing options)
  • The RoboCup simulator limits perception with a 90-degree view that a player can halve or double, a wider view refreshing less often, players within 3 metres sensed outside the view, and identities certain only to 20 metres. https://rcsoccersim.readthedocs.io/en/latest/soccerserver.html
  • The same defender is still the nearest to an attacker after 1 second 88.5 percent of the time, after 5 seconds 64.0, and after 10 seconds 53.5, and in the box 80, 51, and 38: defenders hand runners over rather than follow them. Defenders stand goal-side 63 to 73 percent of the time. .scratch/out/gap_idsse_an.txt (DFL 7 matches)
  • Passes allowed per defensive action (PPDA) average 9.9 to 11.8 across the top five leagues (2019-20), and Premier League teams span about 8.8 to 17.7. https://totalfootballanalysis.com/data-analysis/data-analysis-ppda-its-definition-advantages-and-disadvantages; https://www.premierleague.com/en/news/4250153/passes-per-defensive-action-explained
  • The published pitch-control model, which says who reaches each point first, uses a 0.7-second reaction, 5 metres a second top speed, an arrival-time spread of 0.45 seconds, and a ball speed of 15 metres a second. https://github.com/Friends-of-Tracking-Data-FoTD/LaurieOnTracking
  • The research catalogue lists 53 actions seen in real matches, 38 outfield and 15 goalkeeper, 39 measured in full, 10 in part, feints thinly, and three not at all: marking and cover assignments, keeper organisation, and keeper dive kinematics. A match holds about 1,000 possessions, 2,415 passing options offered, 495 runs, 292 pressures, and about 280 carries of 5 metres or more. .scratch/out/ACTIONS.md §1 and §15

Output and presentation

exploredproblem and solution side

commentary, highlights, statistics, ratings, staff explanations, consistency

Raised in session seven from your eighth brief. Today the game shows team totals, 168 template lines of text commentary with no shots or passes, no ratings, no highlight ranking, no staff analysis, and counts some figures on two paths. The board now sets: a true-cause log that staff read through their quality and that scores the playtest; figures and visualisations always true, staff readings fallible by quality, advice fallible, and staff setting depth while the manager's attribute acts on readings only; text commentary with sound from AI-written, reviewed, tagged banks with some grammar, drawing on player history, the season, match threads, and causes, in English first; ratings from action value plus off-ball credit, fair to each role; highlights by win-probability swing, manager moments, and story; a wide, live set of visualisations from a 180-item catalogue, on designed, customisable screens that analysts also fill; a provider-style xG and one definition per metric; one record for everything, a recompute test, and a trace from every claim to its moments; staff analysis live, at half-time, after the match, and across matches; four reading aids; fast-model matches with banded per-player statistics; decision internals kept for all recent full-engine matches; and a playtest on planted and ordinary matches with what-if predictions. Core: all of it. Scope: keep all.

Decisions 19
  • The engine writes a log of true causes as the match runs, for example that a goal came from a three-against-two on the left after the full-back pushed up. The staff read that log through their own quality, and the same log scores the playtest by comparing a playtester's answer with the true causes. Accepted risk: The engine must decide what counts as a cause, which is itself a model that can be wrong. core
  • Explanations hold three layers: every statistic and picture is always true, so a player who doubts the assistant can check the evidence; the staff's reading of what happened can miss or misweigh causes as staff quality falls; and advice about what to do next can be wrong as all staff advice can. The playtest uses an average staff. Accepted risk: A poor assistant can still mislead a player who does not check the evidence. core
  • The game ships written commentary with crowd, whistle, and ball sounds that follow the events, such as the crowd rising on a chance; there is no spoken voice. Accepted risk: A commercial audio set is needed, and the sounds must follow the event record as exactly as the text does. core
  • Commentary comes from large banks of lines, each tagged with the context it fits and chosen with cooldowns. A language model writes and tags most of the banks before release and a person reviews them; a small grammar assembles some sentences from parts for extra variety. No model runs during play, and every fact in a line comes from the event record, never from the line. Accepted risk: Reviewing many thousands of generated lines is a large editorial task. core
  • A player's match rating is built in three steps: each action earns the change it makes to the chance of scoring and of conceding, tackles, blocks, and pressures that force a loss included; off-ball work earns credit from the true-cause log, such as a run that pulled a defender and opened space, a cover position that closed a lane, or a press that forced a long ball; and the total is compared with what the player's role and the match allowed, so a centre-back in a dominant team is not punished for having little to do. The result shows on the familiar 1-to-10 display. Accepted risk: The ratings will differ from the public sites that players know, which reward attackers, so a player may read a fair rating as a strange one. core
  • Each moment gets a highlight score from three kinds: how far it moved, or could have moved, the win probability; whether the manager may act on it (a tiring player, a card, a change of shape, an overload building); and whether it tells a story (a first goal, a milestone, a rival's red card, a named error from the cause log). Each highlight level includes more of each kind, and the manager picks the level. core
  • The game offers a wide set of visualisations: the brief's set (shot maps with xG, an xG timeline, pass networks, heat maps, average positions, and ratings); tactical ones (overload and zone-control maps by phase, chances by lane, pressing and regain maps, shape by phase with line heights); player ones (action maps, sprint and distance figures, trait use); comparisons against the opponent, the season, and the last match; a momentum or threat-flow line and possession chains; set-piece and keeper maps; energy curves and duel maps; and moment replays with overlays of pitch control, the options the carrier saw and missed, and the line heights. Visualisations update as the match runs, and a full catalogue of statistics, model metrics, and visualisations, with a home for each on the match screen, the data hub, the post-match report, and opposition analysis, is being researched. Accepted risk: A very large surface to design and keep true, and pass networks wait for passer and receiver on every pass from the event-contract redesign. core
  • Every figure, visualisation, commentary line, rating, and staff sentence is computed from the one match record the viewer replays; a test recomputes every figure from the replayed record and fails on any mismatch; and every figure and staff claim traces back to its moments, so tapping 'overloaded on the left' shows the clips and the visualisation that prove it. core
  • Staff analysis comes at four times: live alerts when an issue persists, with the moments that show it; a half-time talk of the top one to three issues, each with evidence and a suggested change; a post-match account of the result as ranked causes linked to their moments and visualisations; and trends across matches, such as conceding from crosses in four of five matches. core
  • Commentary draws on four kinds of context: player history (scoring runs, milestones, debuts, former clubs, returns from injury); the season and rivals (the table, a title race or relegation fight, a derby, the stakes); threads inside the match (a running duel, a keeper's great day, a team pinned back, a change that worked); and the true-cause log, so a line can say why. core
  • The playtest passes when testers name the main cause on matches with a planted cause and on ordinary matches, scored by two people against the true-cause log, and when testers' predictions of what a named change would do match a replay of the match with that change. core
  • The fast model produces per-player statistics and ratings drawn from the full engine's distributions for a player of that quality and role, and a check requires them to match those distributions, so scouting, form, and the market work in every league. Scouting and the market read more than five matches. Accepted risk: The fast model's per-player output is a second thing to fit and check beside its results. core
  • Every full-engine match keeps its decision internals (what each carrier saw, the options scored, and the true causes) while it is recent, so opposition analysis can replay an opponent's decisions as well as the manager's own. Accepted risk: The largest addition to save size on the board, on top of every event of recent matches. core
  • Every statistic and visualisation is true for everyone. Depth comes from staff: better analysts unlock more visualisations and faster, deeper analysis. The human manager's tactical attribute acts only on readings and estimates, such as staff reports, opposition reports, and scouting. core
  • Four aids help a player read the figures: a plain name and a one-line definition on every metric; a simple view with a few key figures and an analyst view with the full catalogue; staff sentences that turn figures into words and link back to them; and a good-or-bad mark on each figure against the league, the season, or the opponent. core
  • The game shows an xG fitted on simulated outcomes from the features a real provider sees (location, angle, body part, pressure, assist type). The engine's true scoring chance stays inside the engine for calibration, so xG keeps the puzzle of luck against skill and never reveals finishing or keeping skill. core
  • Each metric has one game definition on every main screen, written in the glossary with the provider it follows; the analyst view in the data hub can switch to a named provider's variant. core
  • Each screen (live match, half-time, post-match report, data hub, opposition analysis, player profile) ships with a designed set from the catalogue's map; the player can add, remove, and arrange panels; and the analysts push the panels that matter in this match, such as the pressing map when the press is failing. core
  • English ships at launch, and the commentary banks and the glossary are built so that other languages can follow. core
Findings 16
  • Commentary today is text from templates only: 54 template sets and 168 lines, at least three plain lines per commented kind. A set can require the minute band, the score state, a scoring streak, a booked player, and a repeat of the same kind; a line used in the last ten minutes is skipped. Shots and passes get no line, and no audio exists. content/commentary/en.json; crates/engine/src/commentary/templates.rs:19; crates/engine/src/commentary/mod.rs:104
  • The commentator remembers only past event kinds, goal times per club, and who is booked, so it cannot say that a player has scored in three matches running. crates/engine/src/commentary/context.rs:15
  • Nothing ranks moments by importance and there is no highlight level: the feed marks goals, the skip button jumps to the next stoppage, and the report lists goals and cards. web/feed.mjs:158; web/main.mjs:871; web/report.mjs:43
  • The live panel shows possession, shots, shots on target, xG, passes, pass accuracy, fouls, corners, and offsides; the reports add cards, restarts, and a list of goals and cards. There is no shot map, xG timeline, pass network, heat map, average positions, or per-player statistic, and no player rating exists. web/stats.mjs:8; web/report.mjs:10; crates/engine/src/sim.rs:384
  • Positions of all 22 players are recorded on every tick, which is enough for heat maps and average positions, but no event names a passer and a receiver, so a pass network cannot be built. crates/engine/src/record.rs:44; crates/protocol/src/event.rs:107
  • The live panel counts from the engine's summary counters, while the reports count in the browser from the event list, so fouls, corners, and offsides come from two paths; no test checks one against the other. crates/engine/src/observe/mod.rs:172; web/report.mjs:29; crates/engine/tests/full_match.rs:47
  • No assistant or staff analysis exists; the only reasons shown are commentary lines on the AI manager's changes. crates/engine/src/commentary/templates.rs:157
  • Public match ratings reward attacking actions most: shots on target carry the largest weight in WhoScored, SofaScore, and FotMob, clearances about half as much or less, and a defender in a dominant team loses rating for having little to do. The three systems do not agree with each other. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40011215/ (Varley and others 2025, over 2,100 players)
  • Action-value models credit defending unevenly: expected threat credits none, VAEP credits tackles and interceptions through the chance of conceding but not pressing without a touch, goals added credits interrupting actions, and on-ball value credits pressures directly. A ranking built this way agreed with scouts up to 21 points more than an older method. https://arxiv.org/pdf/1802.07127; https://www.hudl.com/blog/statsbomb-on-ball-value; https://www.americansocceranalysis.com/what-are-goals-added; https://arxiv.org/pdf/1802.04987
  • An in-game win-probability model for football exists and is proposed for ranking the crucial moments of a match; Football Manager offers four highlight levels, key, extended, comprehensive, and full. https://arxiv.org/abs/1906.05029; https://community.sports-interactive.com/sigames-manual/football-manager-2023/playing-a-match-r4736/
  • FIFA 12 recorded more than 10,000 new commentary lines and players still complained of repetition. Language models summarising play-by-play made 3,312 factual errors in 180 summaries, and structured input cut errors by about two thirds. https://s204.q4cdn.com/701424631/files/doc_news/2011/06/1/588186.pdf; https://arxiv.org/pdf/2510.21034
  • Football Manager added match momentum, pass maps, and analyst requests to help players adjust tactics, and in 2025 players reported its figures as wrong, for example showing a formation they were not using; an explanation that contradicts the match does more harm than none. https://www.footballmanager.com/the-byline/taking-attacking-insights-data-hub; https://steamcommunity.com/app/3551340/discussions/0/690866958234823370/
  • A tactical overload can be found from positions: players per flank or channel per team, pitch control per zone, and where the chances came from. Having more defenders within 10 metres of the ball raised regains within 5 seconds from 30.2 to 36.2 percent. https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10618-021-00763-7; https://www.sfu.ca/~tswartz/papers/pitch_control.pdf
  • A cheap test of whether a person understands a system asks for a prediction or reason plus a confidence, and a what-if probe; open, non-leading questions scored against a planted cause by two coders suit the brief's playtest. https://arxiv.org/abs/1812.04608; https://gamesuserresearch.com/games-user-research-methods/
  • A catalogue of 180 items (144 statistics and model metrics, 36 visualisations) maps each to the live match screen, half-time, the post-match report, the data hub, opposition analysis, or the player profile, and marks which confuse casual players and which help tactical decisions. The most decision-useful are xG difference with xG per shot, field tilt with box entries by channel, PPDA with regain height, turnovers, transition xG with rest defence, line height and compactness, progressive actions, set-piece xG, fatigue drop-off, and runs received with the pass network; the most misleading alone are possession, pass completion, shots without xG, goals minus xG, raw defensive counts, save rate, and one all-in-one value number. docs/research/metrics-and-visualisations.md
  • An xG taken straight from the engine's true scoring chance would reveal the finisher's and the keeper's skill; a shown xG fitted on simulated outcomes from features a real provider has keeps the luck-against-skill question that makes xG useful. docs/research/metrics-and-visualisations.md (researcher's recommendation)

In-game management

exploredproblem and solution side

touchline changes, substitutions, live information, speed and pause, proportionate effects, AI parity

Raised in session seven from your seventh brief. Today a live change is one patch applied in full at the next stoppage, one rule pack serves every competition with no concussion substitutes, the manager sees nine team figures and energy bars, the viewer runs at up to 8 times with its own pause, the AI acts on fixed minutes without changing formation, and nothing measures a change's effect. The board now sets: substitutions and formation changes at a stoppage, and shouts and instructions at once with uptake over time; free pause and chosen automatic pauses; an AI with the same rules, information, and reaction time, using every tool as well as its skill allows; effects that emerge, banded against real game-state data, hunted for exploits, and each with a cost; dated rule packs per competition; head knocks assessed by medics and decided by the manager; live player state, an opponent read, change feedback, and advice with options; team, opposition, and player instructions and game-state plans; a forked-match check over every tool; and plans and triggers applying after a skip. Core: all of it. Scope: keep all.

Decisions 12
  • Substitutions and formation changes wait for a stoppage. Shouts and instructions, such as push higher, slow down, or target the left-back, are called from the touchline and start at once, and players take them up over time: players near the touchline and those with high decisions, teamwork, and tactic familiarity adopt a change first, and the captain's calls spread it. Accepted risk: A change reaches players at different times, so the team can play two ideas at once for a while, which the effect-size check must allow for. core
  • The player can pause at any moment, at several speeds, and queue changes while paused, and can also choose automatic pauses, for example on a goal, an injury, a card, or an assistant alert. core
  • The AI manager shares three things with the human: the same rules (substitution limits and windows, gates, and shouts, with no action open to only one side); the same information (only what its staff can show it, through the same noisy readings, never the engine's true state); and the same reaction time (it notices a problem after its staff do and cannot change on the tick a threat appears). Accepted risk: An AI limited to staff readings and human reaction plays worse, and the exploit hunt must still find no easy win against it. core
  • The size of a change's effect emerges from behaviour and is kept in bounds three ways: the batch check bands each change's effect against real game-state data; the release exploit hunt also tries sequences of changes during a match and fails any sequence that wins far above its expected rate; and every change carries a real cost, for example more attacking means more exposure behind and faster tiring, so no change is free. core
  • Each competition carries its own rule pack from real data: its substitution limit and windows, whether it has concussion substitutes and an extra-time substitute, and which newer laws it applies, such as the keeper's 8-second rule. Each rule records the date it began, so the simulated past seasons use the rules of their time, for example three substitutes before 2020. Accepted risk: A rule history per competition to research and keep, and the realism bands of past seasons must allow for the older rules. core
  • The medical staff assess every head knock by their quality and advise; the manager makes the call. A confirmed concussion uses a concussion substitute where the competition allows one. Leaving a player on against advice risks a worse injury, a fine, and trouble with the board and the press. core
  • During the match the manager also sees: each player's state (energy, sharpness, a knock, card risk, morale, and whether the player has taken up the last instruction); the analysts' read of the opponent (shape, changes, and who is tiring or struggling, at the analysts' accuracy); a short read after each change of what it did, such as winning the ball back higher but conceding balls in behind; and the assistant's one or two proposed changes with their expected cost, which the manager can accept with one action. core
  • The manager has four kinds of in-match tools: team instructions (line height, pressing, width, tempo, directness, and mentality); opposition targets (press a weak player, attack his side, show him onto his weaker foot, and close down or mark a dangerous one); player instructions (stay wide, get forward, hold position, take fewer risks, shoot on sight, man-mark a named opponent); and prepared game-state plans switched with one action, such as protect a lead or chase the game. Accepted risk: Many more controls, each one a candidate for an exploit, which the exploit hunt must cover. core
  • The in-match change check plays matches to a set state, for example 0-1 at minute 60, then forks each one with paired seeds: one copy makes the change and one does not. The differences in shots, xG, and goals for and against are banded against real game-state data, such as trailing teams taking about 3.4 points more of the shots and a formation change lifting attacking output over the next ten minutes. Accepted risk: Forked matches need saved mid-match states and split random streams, and the real effect sizes come from few studies. core
  • The check covers every in-match tool and level, each with an expected direction and a cost, so no control does nothing; a milestone checks the tools its changes could affect, and a release checks all of them. This extends the tactic sensitivity check into the match. core
  • Every in-match tool is open to the AI manager, and its tactical attributes and adaptability set how many it uses and how well: a weak manager makes fewer, cruder changes, while a strong one uses formations, roles, opposition targets, player instructions, plans, substitutions, and shouts. core
  • After a skip, the manager's prepared game-state plans and triggers apply automatically for the rest of the match, and the fast model reflects them. core
Findings 11
  • A live change today is a patch of formation, mentality, six instruction levels, and each player's role and duty, queued and applied in one step at the next stoppage the rule pack allows (every kind except a penalty); a change with no allowing stoppage before full time expires. There are no per-player or opposition instructions and no shouts, and nothing ramps a change in. crates/engine/src/tactics/mod.rs:75; crates/engine/src/tactics/change.rs:96; crates/engine/src/team.rs:186
  • Substitutions follow one rule pack: five substitutes in three windows with half-time free, and one more substitute and window in extra time. There are no concussion substitutes, and the pack path is fixed, so no competition can have its own rules. content/rules/default.json; crates/engine/src/tactics/change.rs:389; crates/engine/src/data/mod.rs:35
  • During a match the manager sees nine team statistics, an energy bar and a word for each player, the substitutions left, and a chip for each queued change; there are no ratings and no assistant feedback. web/stats.mjs:8; web/lineups.mjs:53; web/substitution-picker.mjs:13
  • The viewer plays at 1, 2, 4, or 8 times real speed, pauses its own playback, and pauses itself at half-time; the engine runs a bounded distance ahead of playback. There is no highlights-only mode. web/playback.mjs:13; web/main.mjs:818; crates/engine-cli/src/serve.rs:339
  • The AI manager checks every 30 seconds and at once after an injury or a goal: it replaces injured players, tired players from minute 55, raises mentality and pressing when trailing from minute 70, and lowers mentality with time-wasting when leading from minute 80. It never changes formation or roles, and it uses the same queue, limits, and windows as the human. crates/engine/src/ai.rs:175; crates/engine/src/ai.rs:259
  • Nothing measures whether a change during a match moves shots or goals: the calibration suites compare fixed setups, the flag comparison judges a feature flag, and the queue test checks only that a change applies. crates/engine-cli/src/calibrate/mod.rs:46; crates/engine-cli/src/report/compare.rs:1; crates/engine/tests/tactics_queue.rs:156
  • IFAB Law 3 allows five substitutes in three opportunities, with half-time and the breaks before and within extra time free, and one more substitute and opportunity in extra time where the competition allows it. Permanent concussion substitutes are an optional law since July 2024: the concussion substitute does not count against the limit and the opponent gains one extra substitute; the Premier League, the FA Cup, and the women's top English leagues use them, and UEFA club competitions did not. Since 2025-26 a keeper holding the ball more than 8 seconds concedes a corner. https://www.theifab.com/laws/latest/the-players/; https://www.theifab.com/news/the-ifab-approves-permanent-concussion-substitutes-among-several-changes-to-the-l/; https://www.theifab.com/news/the-ifab-tackles-goalkeeper-time-wasting/
  • Teams trailing by one took 53.4 percent of shots against 46.6 when leading, about 3.4 points above level, while the leading side still scored 52.9 percent of goals because its chances were better. https://blogarchive.statsbomb.com/articles/soccer/score-effects/ (2013-14 Premier League)
  • Substitutes scored a record 13.3 percent of Premier League goals in 2023-24, and 39.4 percent of goals after minute 75; substitutes run more at high intensity than the players they replace; and trailing teams that made their subs by about 58, 73, and 79 minutes improved their result 42.3 percent of the time against 20.5. https://www.premierleague.com/en/news/3788724; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24413912/; https://ideas.repec.org/a/bpj/jqsprt/v8y2012i1n11.html
  • In one Bundesliga team's 98 matches, a formation change raised the team's attacking output in the next ten minutes with an effect size of 0.52 and lowered the opponent's by about 0.3, with regression to the mean a likely part of it. https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/psychology/articles/10.3389/fpsyg.2022.914915/full
  • Football Manager shouts act on morale, last about ten minutes, and depend on personality; FM26 launched without them because they did almost nothing, and players asked for them back. FM players complain both of meta tactics that exploit the engine and of changes that do nothing. In EA FC a switch of game plan takes a few seconds to take shape. https://www.passion4fm.com/how-to-use-touchline-shouts-in-football-manager/; https://www.thesixthaxis.com/2025/12/09/football-manager-26-update-26-1-0-brings-back-shouts/; https://www.videogamer.com/features/these-two-hilariously-broken-football-manager-tactics-have-been-banned-and-heres-why/

Set pieces

exploredproblem and solution side

routines, takers, defensive setups, aerial contests, penalties, throw-ins

Raised in session seven from your ninth brief. Today the nearest player takes almost every restart, a corner is one fixed cross, there are no walls, marking, or aerial model, penalties convert below real rates, and a stored baseline shows almost no corners because nothing blocks shots yet. The board now sets: routines from a library, an editor, and the set-piece coach; named takers per kind and side who choose among options in play; defensive jobs within a marking system, with defenders reacting to what they see; first contact from the delivery's physics, runs, height, jumping, and a heading duel, plus blocks and holding the referee may penalise; long-throw, retention, and quick throw-ins; routine familiarity with a limit on how many a squad holds; quick restarts that defenders can obstruct; per-routine bands, opponents who learn, and execution error against runaway routines; a set-piece share band per league with drift; penalty bands per league with a shoot-out pressure curve; and a coach effect up to the real range, with the team spread banded. Core: all of it. Scope: keep all.

Decisions 11
  • The manager builds routines three ways: a library of named routines for each restart kind with the taker and target players set; an editor that places each player's start spot, run, and timing and the delivery target, as on a board; and the set-piece coach's proposals, which suit the squad and exploit the next opponent using the analysts' read of how that opponent defends. Accepted risk: A free editor is the easiest place to find an exploit, so the per-routine bands and the exploit hunt must cover edited routines too. core
  • The defending manager names the jobs: who marks whom, who guards each post, who stands in the wall, and who stays up for the counter, within a zonal, man-marking, or mixed system. Defenders carry out their jobs with their own attributes and react to the routine they see: a marker follows a decoy run or passes it on, as decisions and communication allow. core
  • At a set piece, the delivery's arc and pace come from the taker's draw; each player's run, timing, height, and jumping set who can reach the ball; a heading duel draw decides between the players who can reach it; and the keeper chooses to come or stay through the same perception and utility as any player. Legal and illegal contact before the ball arrives, such as blocks, screens, and holding, also happens, and the referee may penalise it. Accepted risk: Contact in the box is a new source of fouls and penalties to calibrate, and the referee must see it through the same model as any foul. core
  • Three guards stop a runaway routine beside the exploit hunt: each routine's goal and shot rates are banded against real rates for its kind, and the hunt plays every routine against every defensive setup; AI opponents learn a manager's favourite routines across matches through their analysts and set their defence against them; and every run, block, and delivery carries the players' error, so a routine perfect on paper often fails in play. core
  • The manager names takers for each restart kind and side (left and right corners, near and far free kicks, penalties, long throws), each with a fallback order, and the taker picks among the routine's options in play by what the taker sees, for example going short when the target is double-marked. core
  • Throw-ins come in three kinds: long-throw routines into the box by a player with the long-throw skill, played like a corner with first contact and second balls; retention routines in the own half with runs that make space; and quick throws before the opponent is set, chosen by the thrower's decisions and the instruction, at the risk of a foul throw or a poor choice. core
  • Each routine has a familiarity that drilling raises and time fades, and an unfamiliar routine is carried out with wider timing and position errors. A squad holds only a limited number of routines well at once, set by the coaches and the players' decisions and teamwork, so more routines make each one weaker. core
  • The taker decides whether to take a restart quickly, by the taker's decisions and the team instruction; a quick restart can go wrong or be called back. Defenders can stand in the way or delay it, at the risk of a card. core
  • Each league has a set-piece goal share band from recent seasons, about 20 to 28 percent without penalties and a quarter to a third with them. The world's set-piece style may drift as AI managers copy what works, for example a long-throw boom spreading, while the season check holds the share inside a wider range. Accepted risk: A drifting world can move a league away from its real band, so the season check must tell a drift from a fault. core
  • Penalty rate and conversion are banded per league, with the rate raised where the competition uses video review, and shoot-out conversion is banded apart. A shoot-out pressure curve lowers conversion on kicks that decide elimination, through composure and the pressure trait, checked against the real 70 and 44 percent. core
  • The best set-piece coach and routines together can reach the top of the real spread, about 0.065 xG and about 6 percent goals per corner, and never beyond it; the per-team spread of set-piece xG is itself a band, so the world's teams spread like real ones. core
Findings 13
  • The nearest outfield player takes every restart except goal kicks and kick-offs; a corner is always one lofted cross to the team-mate nearest the penalty spot; a throw-in is a short lofted pass of 3 to 15 metres with no long throws; and a direct free kick is played as open play, with no choice to shoot or cross. crates/engine/src/rules/restart.rs:76; crates/engine/src/decision.rs:521; crates/engine/src/rules/mod.rs:607
  • At a corner or free kick everyone walks to the formation anchor, and opponents are only pushed back to the legal distance: there are no walls, no marking, no players sent into the box, and no players left up. crates/engine/src/rules/restart.rs:215
  • No aerial model exists: heading and jumping are read by nothing in the engine, a ball above reach height cannot be touched, and the keeper has only a larger reach and a flat catch chance, with no claim or punch and command of area used only to pick players. crates/engine/src/sim.rs:953; content/attributes.json:9
  • A foul in the area gives a penalty with no advantage; the nearest outfielder takes it with less aim noise; the keeper does not dive in open play. The shoot-out has a random 2-metre dive and a halved catch chance, tuned by hand to about 70 percent and marked as not calibrated. crates/engine/src/rules/mod.rs:237; crates/engine/src/rules/mod.rs:1020
  • The bands cover corners per team, throw-ins, and goal kicks only; the stored baseline measures 0.004 to 0.007 corners per team against 3.5 to 6.5, about 27 throw-ins against 35 to 55, and about 7 goal kicks against 12 to 22, because nothing blocks or deflects a shot yet. There is no band for set-piece goal share or penalties. The planned slices add real delays, blocks and deflections, and penalty conversion, and leave out headers and routines. .ai/workflows/football-manager-match-engine/implement-evidence/realism-bands-v2/baseline-table.md; content/realism-bands.json:16; .ai/workflows/football-manager-match-engine/03-slice-keeper-and-shots.md:67
  • Non-penalty set-piece goals were about 20 percent of Premier League goals in 2023-24 and 2024-25 and rose to 26.9 to 28.3 percent in 2025-26, a record, with corners alone at 17.5 percent; the previous ten-season high was 23.9 percent. Counting whole possessions after a restart and penalties, the local StatsBomb set gives 36 percent. https://theanalyst.com/articles/premier-league-teams-still-more-direct-2025-26; https://theanalyst.com/articles/arsenal-tottenham-corners-record-premier-league-title-race-relegation-battle; .scratch/out/ACTIONS.md §13
  • About 5 corners per team per match; crossed corners lead to a shot in the possession 38.5 percent of the time and a goal 4.1 percent, short corners 32.6 and 3.3; short corners are 11 to 18 percent. Team xG per corner spans 0.016 to 0.067, and whole-possession goals run about 4.2 per 100 corners. https://theanalyst.com/articles/premier-league-corner-stats-inswingers-vs-outswingers-short-vs-crossed-kai-havertzs-defending; https://www.premierleague.com/en/news/4187343
  • Direct free kicks are scored 3.2 to 3.9 percent of the time now, down from 5 to 7 percent in earlier eras; deliveries into the box from free kicks lead to a shot 27.6 percent of the time and a goal 3.3 percent. Since 2019, attackers must stand at least 1 metre from a wall of three or more. https://theanalyst.com/articles/whats-happened-to-goals-from-premier-league-free-kicks; .scratch/out/ACTIONS.md §13; https://www.scottishfa.co.uk/news/updates-to-laws-of-the-game-2019-20/
  • Penalties per match ran 0.23 to 0.47 across the top leagues before video review, which raised the rate in most leagues by about 13 percent. Conversion averaged about 76 percent in the top leagues before 2019 and about 82 percent in the Premier League since, after keepers' saves fell from 19 to 12 percent under the one-foot-on-the-line rule. Keepers dive to one side on about 95 percent of kicks; kicks go in 63 percent of the time when the keeper guesses right and 90 when wrong. Shoot-out kicks convert about 70 percent, and a kick whose miss eliminates the team about 44. https://www.spreadex.com/sports/blog/features/var-impact-europe-big-5-premier-league/; https://theanalyst.com/articles/premier-league-penalties-like-free-goal; https://pricetheory.uchicago.edu/levitt/Papers/ChiapporiGrosecloseLevitt2002.pdf; https://sites.northwestern.edu/nusportsanalytics/2020/12/04/the-numbers-behind-penalty-shootout-in-world-cup/
  • There are 37 to 44 throw-ins a match in the local data and about half are kept under pressure; a throw-in coach raised Liverpool's retention from 45 to 68 percent. Long throws into the Premier League box rose from 0.9 a match in 2020-21 to about 4 in 2025-26, with one long-throw goal every 12 matches. https://www.thisisanfield.com/2019/09/expert-analysis-shows-the-huge-impact-of-liverpools-throw-in-coach-thomas-gronnemark/; https://theanalyst.com/articles/premier-league-teams-still-more-direct-2025-26; .scratch/out/ACTIONS.md §13
  • At the 2018 World Cup, zonal marking conceded a goal on 6.0 percent of corners and mixed marking on 3.7; in Premier League tracking, a four-zone system conceded more shots but worse ones and fewer goals than a two-zone system; about 80 percent of Premier League teams use mixed systems. Squad height does not predict set-piece goal share. https://vuir.vu.edu.au/40479/1/Kubayi-2020-Cornerkicks.pdf; https://global-uploads.webflow.com/5f1af76ed86d6771ad48324b/606e51c17bf6c8ba83d69a01_LaurieShaw-CornerKicks-RPpaper.pdf; https://www.olbg.com/blogs/premier-leagues-tallest-teams
  • A set-piece coach can double a team's corner goals: Arsenal scored one corner goal per 32 corners before Jover and one per 16.4 after, with non-penalty set-piece goals rising from 6 to 15 to 20 a season; Brentford's rose from 12 to 18 a season while those conceded fell. https://www.premierleague.com/en/news/4187343; http://archive.trainingground.guru/articles/brentford-looking-for-set-piece-coach-after-cuevas-move-to-chelsea
  • Football Manager's set-piece exploits came from AI marking that ignored unusual positions, such as a lurker outside the box or three attackers unmarked after a short long throw, and each fix was a patch to marking assignment. https://community.sports-interactive.com/forums/topic/154901-103-quotcorner-exploitquot-thread/; https://steamcommunity.com/sharedfiles/filedetails/?id=1653318969

Fatigue, injuries, and fixture congestion

exploredproblem and solution side

in-match decline, recovery, sharpness, injury risk and types, rotation

Raised in session seven from your tenth brief, then taken deeper and zoomed out. Today a player has one energy value per match, injuries are flat rolls with no severity, and every match starts every player fresh. The board now sets, in the match: a sprint reserve, a slow reserve, and mental fatigue, kept modest; pacing by reserves; role-specific drain, fresh substitutes, extra time about 10 percent below regulation, and small recoveries in breaks. Between matches: condition over about three days and an accumulated load seen through staff; training at a daily rhythm, a preseason base the off-season loses, and staff who design or warn; sharpness from matches by level and from match-simulation training; travel load, jet lag by time zone, climate, and travel planning; illness at real rates that can spread. Injuries: a hazard per exposure event with real types, layoffs, recurrence, and history by site; training injuries; real frequency; treatment choices where the manager, the player, and the agent have a say; returns through phases; knocks that carry and hide; and a mind that dips, stays cautious, and suffers when injured long. Beyond: congestion costing mostly injury risk; rotation pushed by morale, sharpness, the calendar, and staff; medicals and lower values for injury history; crisis consequences for staff, players, press, and fans; the same state in fast-model leagues; bands judged on full-engine seasons with the emulator between releases; and a save that keeps a window by activity. Core: all of it. Scope: keep all.

Decisions 24
  • In-match fatigue has three parts: a short-term reserve that sprints use and brief rest refills, which sets repeated-sprint ability; a slow reserve that drains through the match; and mental fatigue that raises concentration lapses and slows decisions late, which personality and the fitness staff move. Late tiredness is real but modest, and bands on running by 15-minute period keep the physical drop as small as real data shows. Accepted risk: Three reserves per player to calibrate, and the real late drop is mostly dead time, so true fatigue must stay small. core
  • Between matches a player has a condition that recovers over about three days, faster with natural fitness, youth, and good staff, and an accumulated load over recent weeks from matches, training, and travel.
