High load slows recovery, lowers top output, and raises injury risk, so congestion bites even when condition looks full. The manager sees load only through the sports-science staff's readings, as accurate as their quality. core
  • Each exposure event (a tackle, a sprint, a collision, a landing, a change of direction) carries a small injury chance, scaled by fatigue, load, injury proneness, age, previous injury, the pitch, and the weather. Each kind of event leads to its own injury types, such as sprints to hamstring strains, tackles to ankle and knee injuries, and landings to knee injuries, each with its own recovery time and recurrence risk. Training injuries also happen, moved by the training plan's intensity and the medical and fitness staff. Accepted risk: Many small hazards to fit so that the season totals and the type mix both match real data. core
  • Injury counts, types, and days lost follow real data; only the long-term after-effects of severe injuries are softer, as decided earlier. core
  • A return from injury passes through phases: rehab, training, bench minutes, and full load. Each phase restores sharpness in part, and the manager can skip phases at a recurrence cost, against the medical staff's warning. core
  • A minor knock can carry into the next days, and the player can play at reduced output and a higher risk of a real injury or rest a few days. Some players hide a knock by personality, so the staff may miss it and the manager may pick a player who is not fit. core
  • Four more forces push rotation beside load and injury risk: squad players short of minutes lose morale and hold the manager to promised roles; players without minutes lose sharpness, so an unrotated bench is not ready; real calendars with midweek cups, European nights, and travel strain the first eleven; and the sports-science staff flag players at risk before each match and propose a rotation. core
  • Before a match each player shows an injury-risk word (low, raised, or high) from the staff's reading, with the staff's reasons, such as three matches in seven days and a hamstring history, so the manager learns what drives risk. core
  • Congestion follows real data: it raises injury risk strongly and lowers output only a little, so the reason to rotate is protecting players, as in real football. Accepted risk: Results barely suffer from congestion, so a manager who ignores rotation pays only through injuries, which the injury bands must make felt. core
  • Each injury leaves a raised risk at its body site, high just after return and fading over months with a small raise that stays, plus a small general raise for any injury after a recent one, also fading. core
  • A player low on the slow reserve cuts low-value running, such as jogging back into shape or drifting runs, to protect sprints, by decisions, work rate, and personality; the gaps this opens in the shape are there for the opponent to use. core
  • The season check bands injuries four ways: counts and burden (injuries per squad and per 1000 match and training hours, days lost, and players available for each match); types and layoffs (the share of each type, the median days out per type, and the severity mix); recurrence (the re-injury share and its timing after return); and the congestion effect (the rise in risk with 4 days' rest or fewer and late in the second match of a short cycle, against the real ratios). core
  • Training shapes fitness at three scales: each day's session adds load and moves fitness, and a good plan follows a match-day rhythm of recovery, load, and taper; a preseason builds a fitness base that the off-season loses, so a short preseason means a weaker base and more early-season injuries; and the fitness staff design the plan by their quality when the manager delegates, and warn when the manager's plan overloads the squad. core
  • Illness such as colds, flu, and stomach bugs happens at real rates by season and can spread through a squad, with a few days out and reduced output on return. Accepted risk: A spreading illness can strip a squad before a key match, which a player may find unfair unless the staff's warnings and prevention make it manageable. core
  • The medical staff decide routine care. For serious injuries the manager chooses among the options the staff present, such as surgery or rest, a specialist, or a second opinion, each with its time out, cost, and recurrence risk; the player and the agent also have a say, so a player may seek his own specialist or refuse surgery. Better staff and facilities shorten returns and cut recurrence. core
  • An injury reaches the mind three ways: a return after a long injury lowers confidence for a while, by personality, and a good start restores it; the player is cautious in the actions that caused the injury, such as fewer committed tackles after an ankle injury, fading with minutes; and the long-term injured feel isolated and frustrated, which staff, the manager's contact, and team-mates can ease. core
  • Every match builds sharpness by its level, first-team matches most, then cups, reserve and under-21 matches, then friendlies, and training slows the loss. A match-simulation training session builds some sharpness at a load and injury cost, so a manager can keep a bench sharp without matches. Irregular minutes carry their own risk: a player thrown in after weeks without matches faces a load spike. core
  • Travel adds to accumulated load by distance; crossing time zones adds a jet-lag state of about a day per zone, worse eastward, that dulls decisions and slows recovery; climate moves in-match drain and injury risk, with heat cutting high-intensity running and cold raising muscle risk; and the club plans travel, such as flying one or two days before by charter or scheduled flight, trading cost against recovery. core
  • Fatigue differs by role and time: each role's movement sets its own drain, so full-backs, wide players, and pressing forwards tire faster; a substitute enters with full reserves after a short warm-up curve; extra time continues the reserves about 10 percent below regulation output and adds clearly to the next days' load; and players recover a little during in-match breaks, such as half-time, the breaks before and within extra time, and long stoppages. core
  • Every player everywhere carries condition, load, sharpness, and injuries. The fast model draws injuries and minutes from the full engine's distributions, and AI managers in background leagues rotate by the same rules, so the market, form, and scouting see real availability. core
  • A pre-transfer medical by the buying club's medical staff reads injury proneness and site history at their accuracy, and the club can walk away, renegotiate the fee, or add injury clauses. The shared market valuation also falls with visible injury history, such as days missed in recent seasons, so injury-prone players are cheaper for everyone. core
  • An injury crisis reaches beyond the pitch: the board asks why, and the medical and fitness staff's standing falls when burden runs above what their quality should give; overplayed players lose morale and complain, while a squad that sees the manager protect players trusts him more; and the press runs injury-crisis stories, with fans blaming the manager or the medical staff. core
  • The season injury and fatigue bands are judged on full-engine seasons in the release run, and the fast model is checked against those seasons' distributions; between releases, the engine emulator screens injury and fatigue settings so the release run only confirms. core
  • A save keeps daily load and state for recent weeks, a longer window for players in the active leagues, and a compact injury record (type, site, date, days out) for every player's whole career. Accepted risk: Per-player state for every player everywhere adds to save size beside every pair and full recent events. core
Findings 20
  • Energy today is one value per player from 1.0 that drains with speed squared, scaled only by stamina; pressing costs nothing extra. Below 0.7 one multiplier lowers speed, acceleration, passing, finishing, decisions, and composure together, and only regulation half-time gives energy back. crates/engine/src/fatigue.rs:45; crates/engine/src/fatigue.rs:78; crates/engine/src/fatigue.rs:131; content/tuning.json:158
  • An injury today comes from two flat rolls scaled by injury resistance: the tackled player on a won tackle or foul, and every player once a minute. Fatigue does not change the chance, and an injury has no severity and no time out. crates/engine/src/fatigue.rs:86; crates/engine/src/sim.rs:1009; content/tuning.json:83
  • Every match starts every player fresh; a recovery rate exists in tuning but nothing reads it, and there is no carried fatigue, sharpness, or injury duration. No band checks injury or fatigue rates. crates/engine/src/player.rs:204; crates/engine/src/data/tuning.rs:129; content/realism-bands.json
  • Per minute of live play, high-speed running barely changes late in a match (plus 1.1 percent from minutes 0-30 to 60-90) while ball-in-play time falls 26.8 percent; forwards are the exception at minus 12.3 percent, and substitutes run 21.0 metres of high-speed running per live minute against 15.4 for starters. .scratch/out/FINDINGS.md §16.2a (DFL, 7 matches, 57 full-match outfielders)
  • Elite clubs see about 8 injuries per 1000 hours overall (about 21 to 27.5 in matches and 1.7 to 4.1 in training), about 2 injuries per player and 50 per squad each season, with about 86 percent of players available for a match; lower injury burden goes with more points. Incidence has fallen about 3 percent a season, while hamstring injuries rose from 12 to 24 percent of all injuries. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19553225/; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23645832/; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33547038/; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36588400/
  • Median days out by diagnosis: contusion 4, functional muscle 4 to 5, concussion 5, groin 8, ankle sprain 8, hamstring, quadriceps, and calf strains 13, knee MCL 16, lateral meniscus 36, and ACL 205. Muscle injuries are about a third of all injuries (hamstring 37 percent of them, adductor 23, quadriceps 19, calf 13); severity runs about 17 percent minimal, 23 mild, 41 moderate, and 18 severe. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7146935/; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21335353/
  • Re-injuries are 12 to 16 percent of injuries and keep players out longer; 18 percent of hamstring injuries are recurrences, 69 percent of those within two months of return. A previous injury is the strongest risk factor, about 2.7 times the risk of an injury the next season and 2 to 3 times for the same hamstring, groin, or knee injury. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7146935/; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16855067/
  • About 30 percent of injuries involve contact and 70 percent do not; foul play is involved in 40 percent of match ankle sprains; 61 percent of hamstring injuries happen while running or sprinting; and almost half of match hamstring injuries fall in the last 15 minutes of either half. https://www.uefa.com/multimediafiles/download/uefaorg/medical/02/61/67/86/2616786_download.pdf; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23813486/; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36588400/
  • Congestion raises injury risk far more than it lowers running: one Champions League team saw 25.6 against 4.1 injuries per 1000 hours with two matches a week against one, with running unchanged; across 27 teams, 4 days' rest or fewer raised muscle injuries by 32 percent; and the last 15 minutes of the second match in a two-match cycle carried 3.1 times the risk. Congestion barely changed results, and a meta-analysis found no effect on total distance. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/20400751/; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23851296/; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26682867/; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33068272/
  • Recovery is not complete at 72 hours: hamstring force, physical performance (2 to 4 percent down), muscle damage markers, and soreness are still impaired, while hormonal and technical markers have recovered. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29098658/ (77 studies)
  • The acute-to-chronic workload ratio has no evidence as a way to manage injury risk, and the ratio creates statistical artefacts, so load should not be modelled as that ratio. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32502973/
  • After an ACL injury, elite players return to matches after about 7.4 months, and only 59 to 65 percent play at the same level three years later, with lower minutes and output in the first two seasons back. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27034129/; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29927499/
  • Premier League clubs used 30 to 34 players in 2023-24 and only five players played every minute; a title-winning Lille season had the lowest squad use and the fewest injuries. Football Manager players have complained of too many injuries in every version. https://www.premierleague.com/en/news/4025786; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/25216043/; https://community.sports-interactive.com/forums/topic/594593-football-manager26-injuries/
  • Elite players fall ill about once every second season, with a median of 3 days out (58 percent respiratory, 38 percent stomach), peaking in winter; respiratory infections in the Bundesliga cost about 12 days each. No football data measures spread within a squad, so spread is a design choice. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27034126/ (73 teams, 1.26 million player-days); https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13084848/
  • A full stop in the off-season costs 3 to 21 percent of aerobic capacity, against 1 to 4 percent for players who stay active, and two weeks off takes two to three weeks to rebuild. Teams average 30 preseason sessions (10 to 51); each 10 more go with 22 fewer days lost per 1000 hours in season and one point more match availability, a weak but real link. Training injuries spike in July. https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s40279-020-01407-4; https://doi.org/10.1177/0363546519899359; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/12453838/
  • An irregular match schedule, not total volume, goes with severe muscle injury across 30,097 player-seasons in the top five leagues, and load spikes carry 5 to 7 times the injury rate; compensatory training keeps non-starters' fitness tests level but leaves them exposed to a spike when they play. https://doi.org/10.1080/00913847.2026.2657258; https://www.researchgate.net/publication/331267557; https://doi.org/10.3390/medicina57070645
  • Jet lag lasts about a day per time zone, worse eastward, and stayed raised four days after an 11-zone trip; away disadvantage grows with time zones crossed, but regular travel did not raise injuries. At about 43 degrees high-intensity running fell 26 percent while pass success rose; cooler northern clubs had more injuries and warmer southern clubs more ACL injuries; altitude favours the higher team. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8279034/; https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3378691/; https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22092416/
  • Treatment choices change time out: a partial meniscus removal returns in about 4 months against 6 for a repair, and a high ankle sprain 71 days after surgery against 39 without; surgery on some hamstring tears gives similar returns with fewer re-injuries. Days lost per injury type differ widely between clubs, lower where medical and performance staff communicate well and higher after head-staff turnover. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40815849/; https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9679836/; https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11877254/; https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10660205/
  • Fear of re-injury is common after a hamstring injury and goes with both re-injury and lower performance; after a severe hamstring injury, top speed and high-speed running took up to five weeks of matches to recover, and a mild quadriceps injury about a week. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13223650/; https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12884885/
  • High-intensity running differs by role: wide midfielders about 3,140 metres a match, central midfielders 2,830, full-backs 2,610, attackers 2,340, centre-backs 1,830. Extra time runs about 10 percent below regulation for high-intensity running, speed, and distance, with accelerations and technique falling most. https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/abs/10.1080/02640410802512775; https://scielo.org.za/scielo.php?script=sci_arttext&pid=S1015-51632018000100016; https://journals.plos.org/plosone/article?id=10.1371%2Fjournal.pone.0110995

Long-term integration

exploredproblem and solution side

how each match feeds the season and the career, and how loops are dampened

Raised in session seven from your eleventh brief. The board now sets how each match feeds the wider game: growth in three layers (training base, minutes at the right challenge, a performance extra); form at two speeds with a fading memory; a board that reads points against expected, trend, big matches, and style; reputation from level-weighted ratings, big stages, a visible-stats bias, and a slow fade; value with a slow core and a fast edge; income from merit money, lagged attendance, sponsors by reputation, and European money; and job security from earned credit, rising targets, owner changes, and real AI sackings. Loops are dampened: modest banded mood with complacency and opponents who lift; real league rules, wage pressure, ageing and poaching, and rivals who react; balance that emerges plus a soft pull in AI choices; and four recovery routes. Every match keeps stakes, secondary goals, dead-rubber effects, and stories. A multi-season check of ten fifty-season saves bands title turnover, fortune churn, and up and down rates against a pooled band, and four guards stop long-save drift. Core: all of it. Scope: keep all.

Decisions 16
  • A young player's growth has three layers: training sets the base rate by coaches, facilities, and personality; minutes at the right level of challenge add the most, fastest when the match level sits a little above the player's ability; and good performances add a small extra. Growth moves within the player's narrowing range of futures. core
  • Form runs at two speeds with a fading memory, so no match drops out suddenly: a short form of about three matches feeds mood and selection, and a long form of about a season feeds reputation and value. core
  • After each match the board reads four things, weighted by owner type: points against what the squad's strength and budget predicted; the trend, so a run of bad results counts more than one loss and a recovery softens it; big matches, such as derbies, finals, and rivals; and style and academy minutes against the owner's targets. core
  • A player's reputation moves four ways: ratings and minutes weighted by the level of the league and competition; big stages, such as finals, European nights, and tournaments, which count more; a bias toward visible goals and assists, as real scouts and media show, so creators and defenders are undervalued and good scouting finds an edge; and a slow fade without minutes or at a lower level. core
  • Mood stays without a runaway streak three ways: mood effects stay small and a season check bands streaks against real data, so a win streak is no more likely than chance; after a run of wins some players, by personality, relax and cut effort, while after losses some fight harder; and opponents raise effort and set up more cautiously against a leader or a team on a run. core
  • Four forces limit a rich, winning club: each league's real rules, such as revenue sharing, parachute payments, spending rules, and salary caps where they exist; wage pressure, as success raises contract demands and rival offers; ageing and poaching, as squads age together and bigger clubs buy the stars and the manager; and rivals who react, as AI clubs spend and change tactics to catch the leader and rival owners invest after failures. core
  • Balance comes first from real mechanisms, plus a gentle hidden pull toward the middle inside AI club decisions, such as transfer and wage choices, that the multi-season check tunes to a pooled real band. Accepted risk: A hidden pull can feel like rubber-banding if players discover it, so the pull must act only through AI choices a real club could make and never on match outcomes. core
  • A struggling club recovers four ways: it sells stars, cuts wages, and buys cheap young talent that grows; new owners take over at real rates, sometimes with bad results; luck evens out, and the game shows the manager the underlying numbers, such as expected goals, so an unlucky slump reads as unlucky; and a strong academy now and then produces a golden generation that lifts the club for some seasons. core
  • Every match keeps its weight four ways: the game shows the stakes before it, such as projected places, qualification and relegation chances, board and fan pressure, and prize money per place; mid-table clubs chase secondary goals, such as a cup run, a top-half finish, academy minutes, a record, or a rival's result; matches with nothing at stake change players, as some switch off, some play for a contract or a place, and the manager can try players and tactics; and press, rivalry, and records give each match a story. core
  • Results move income four ways: real prize money per league place and cup round; attendance and season tickets that follow fan mood with a lag, so a bad season costs money the next season; commercial deals that renew at values set by reputation and recent success; and large European sums, which is one of the loops that entrenches the top and one of the loops the multi-season check watches. core
  • A manager's job security builds and falls over seasons: trophies, promotions, and seasons above expected build credit that fades slowly; success raises the board's targets, so an over-performance becomes the next target; a new owner resets credit, targets, and patience; and AI clubs sack at real rates and moments, such as long losing runs, the relegation zone, and season end. core
  • A player's market value mostly follows long form and reputation over seasons, but a big tournament or a breakout half-season moves it quickly, as real hype does. core
  • The multi-season check bands three families: title turnover (distinct champions per decade, the longest title run, and how often a champion retains the title); fortune churn (season-to-season rank correlation, how often a top-four club drops out, and how far clubs rise and fall over ten seasons); and up and down rates (promoted clubs' survival, relegated clubs' return within one and two years, and yo-yo clubs). The champion's points share is not banded. core
  • The multi-season check runs ten saves of fifty seasons each, so slow drift, such as generated-player attributes, reputation, and wages, shows as well as turnover; background leagues use the fast model. Accepted risk: Ten saves give few title-turnover samples per league, and fifty full seasons of the active leagues may not fit the overnight release run on the Mac mini and the VPS, so the size needs a cost check. core
  • The multi-season check bands against one pool of real history across leagues, because a save's history diverges from real history anyway; the soft pull in AI choices is tuned to that pooled band. core
  • Four guards stop long-save drift: each year's generated youth cohort is anchored to a fixed target distribution by country and position; AI clubs give young players minutes by a real share and loan them when they cannot; AI clubs plan squads, replacing ageing stars and filling gaps each window; and club and league reputations drift toward a long-run value set by money and results, so no club stays big or small by inertia. core
Findings 8
  • Titles concentrate: in 2000 to 2010 the Premier League had 3 champions and the Bundesliga 5, while in 2010 to 2020 the Bundesliga had 2 (Bayern won 11 in a row) and Serie A 2 (Juventus won 9 in a row); Lyon won 7 in a row, Rosenborg 13, and Scotland has two clubs. Champions' share of available points in the big five rose from 69.9 percent (1999 to 2004) to 80.5 percent (2014 to 2019), and imbalance rose across 24 leagues. https://football-observatory.com/More-and-more-points-won-by-champions; https://www.cies.ch/cies/news/news-detail/article/new-report-shows-decline-of-competitive-balance-in-european-football; winner lists (tally)
  • Points per game is the team statistic that predicts next season's points worst; goal difference and expected-goal difference predict better. The table after about 12 rounds correlates about 0.8 with the final table, and the leader on 1 January won the title in 9 of 20 seasons. No clean football figure exists for the season-to-season points correlation; the NBA shows 0.64 and the NFL 0.33. https://johnknightstats.substack.com/p/which-statistics-best-predict-future; http://eightyfivepoints.blogspot.com/2016/10/want-to-know-where-your-teams-gonna.html; https://harvardsportsanalysis.org/2018/01/how-much-correlation-between-seasons-is-there-in-each-major-north-american-sports-league/
  • Since 1995, 47 percent of clubs promoted to the Premier League went straight back down and 60 percent within two years; all three promoted clubs fell in 2023-24 and 2024-25. Of 42 relegated clubs from 2009-10 to 2022-23, 40.5 percent returned within a year, 64.3 percent within two, and 31 percent never; parachute clubs were almost twice as likely to go up. https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yo-yo_club; https://theanalyst.com/articles/premier-league-promoted-sides-relegation-championship; https://theesk.org/2025/08/12/the-analysis-series-yo-yos-and-parachutes-premier-league-relegation-subsequent-performance-and-financial-impact-2010-2024/
  • Wage bills explain 80 to 90 percent of league position, and wages predict better than net transfer spend. Studies find competitive balance fell after UEFA's Financial Fair Play, because a cap tied to revenue locks in hierarchies built on past success; parachute payments soften the drop but skew the second tier. https://www.open.edu/openlearn/money-management/management/business-studies/stefan-szymanski-on-the-business-football; https://shura.shu.ac.uk/22038/3/Plumley-UnintendedConsequencesOfFinancialFair(AM).pdf; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Premier_League_parachute_and_solidarity_payments
  • Football results show no hot hand: after controlling for strength, a win streak is followed by slightly worse results, and repeating the last result is slightly less likely than chance. Sacking a manager in a slump mostly rides regression to the mean; Dutch clubs that kept the manager recovered faster. https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/full/10.1080/00036846.2014.969830; https://ideas.repec.org/a/eee/ejores/v148y2003i2p233-246.html
  • Playing in Europe costs little in the league: Europa League clubs did better domestically than matched non-qualifiers over 16 seasons, and a Champions League midweek costs about 0.1 points in the next league match, while European clubs make about 94 starting changes a season against 71. https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC7739226/; https://www.skysports.com/football/news/11096/11502109/champions-league-and-europa-league-impact-on-premier-league-results; https://theanalyst.com/articles/playing-europe-premier-league-form-stats
  • Minutes are a leading predictor of market value for players first seen under 21, but no public study sizes how much minutes at 18 to 21 raise later ability; under 30 percent of loanees are 21 or younger, and players who stay at their training club have better careers. https://arxiv.org/pdf/2212.11041; https://football-observatory.com/Global-analysis-of-player-loans; https://www.efcfootball.com/en/news/efc-and-cies-study-highlights-career-benefits-of-young-players-remaining
  • Football Manager long saves drift: generated players shift in attributes over decades, big AI clubs leave high-potential youngsters without minutes, and AI clubs stop buying the stars they need; league reputation moves about one point a year, which damps drift but makes it sticky. https://www.videogamer.com/features/these-annoying-bugs-are-ruining-long-term-football-m/; https://footballmanager.fandom.com/wiki/Reputation; https://community.sports-interactive.com/forums/topic/581909-league-reputation/

Open now

Open tensions

  • A release run of about 1.5 million matches takes about 33 hours at today's speed of about 12.6 matches a second on 8 cores, before the realism work slows each match.
  • The ten-minute change verdict of at least 12,000 matches needs about 20 matches a second, about 1.6 times today's harness speed (arithmetic from the measured speed).

Settled at the start of session 12

  • The reference machine reports 8 cores and 8 logical processors, so multithreading is off today. Turning it on stays an option, with an estimated gain of 10 to 25 percent.

Top risk, engine architecture

  • Effects that cross module lines, such as fatigue, pressure, and decisions, may hide or double.

Top risks, analytical models

  • Threat may make choices less like real players’.
  • Pitch control and possession value may not fit the budget.
  • Freeze-frame data for the choice test is thin.

Top risks, environmental factors

  • Weather and pitch caps are design values with no real source.
  • The parts of home advantage could add up past its measured band.

Top risks, psychological factors

  • Double counting: players, mentality, momentum, and pressure all push the same way when behind.
  • Thin data: the next-goal, big-match, and three-way shares bands need finely split real data.
  • Stacked mind family: momentum and pressure add two more mind effects under the soft combine.

Top risk, traits, personality, and chemistry

  • Stacked effects: mood, personality, traits, and chemistry together could swamp ability, so the five-match test runs with every family switched on at once. The families now combine softly, and a tail band checks runs of very poor matches.

Accepted risks

  • The blend mixes three rules: an action runs in stages (see, choose, execute, under pressure), each stage has one main attribute with two or three smaller supporting weights, and the weights are fitted from real data where it exists and set by design where it does not. Risk: Several stages per action add calculations on every tick, which presses on the engine's CPU tripwire that the other realism additions already strain.
  • The engine stores attributes in tenths from 1.0 to 20.0, and the screen shows whole numbers from 1 to 20. Risk: Two players shown as 15 can differ slightly in play.
  • A player's overall level follows a normal curve centred on 10 with a spread of about 3.5 on the 1-to-20 scale, wider than the research suggestion of 2.8; single attributes spread wider still, each tier has its own mean, and the largest drop is from the first tier to the second. Risk: The world shows more upsets than real football, which presses on the cup upset band and the per-match realism bands.
  • The strength of the convex curve is set against the five-match target: a top player looks clearly better than an average player in the same role in about two of three runs of five matches. The factor of two for the top gap is only a starting value. Risk: The target sets the whole curve, not the top gap alone, so the top gap can end at any ratio the target allows.
  • Consistency and injury proneness are hidden values, beside the twelve hidden personality traits; injury proneness replaces the visible injury-resistance attribute. Risk: The manager learns them only by watching and through staff, over seasons.
  • For the manager's own players, the visible attributes are exact once the player signs; hidden values stay hidden. Risk: Scouting matters only for the market, and a signing shows its true visible numbers on the first day.
  • Every role ships with all three behaviours together: where the player stands in and out of possession, which actions the player prefers, and how teammates treat the player (a target forward draws long balls). Risk: It is the largest piece of work and the most tuning in the data-model area.
  • A duty (defend, support, attack) scales the role's own behaviour, for example an attacking full-back overlaps more and tracks back less. Risk: Every role needs three tuned variants.
  • The game offers the full genre list of about 40 roles. Risk: With three duties and three behaviours each, about 360 tuned variants, and many roles differ only slightly.
  • Each kind of wider data gets its own data rules, decided as the kind is examined, rather than the player's rules applied unchanged. Risk: Slower, and the kinds may drift apart in scale and in how they are checked.
  • Team numbers act on actions that need several players at once: pressing chains, the offside line, and combinations between pairs. Risk: A second place to tune beside the player layer.
  • On the pitch a club's style comes from two layers, the squad and the tactic. Club identity shapes both from outside by two routes: it shapes who the club hires and buys, and the board and fans expect it, so a manager who plays against it loses standing and the AI manager weighs it in its philosophy. Risk: Two slow loops to tune, and style on the pitch no longer carries a separate identity input for the realism test to check.
  • A club's identity at the start of a save is seeded from the real club's history and reputation, with a fallback where no source exists. Risk: It needs a data source for the clubs of every active league.
  • Identity drifts slowly towards how the club actually plays when it wins, and jumps at big events such as a takeover, a legendary manager, relegation, or a trophy. Risk: The most tuning of the three ways identity can change.
  • When a manager wins against the club's identity, three reactions mix, and each club sets their strength: results buy time, supporter groups split between results and style, and at clubs with a strong identity a winning manager can still lose standing over time. Risk: At some clubs a manager can be sacked while winning, which some players of the genre find unfair.
  • Height sets a player's standing reach in the air, jumping adds to it, and heading decides what happens on contact; tall players also turn a little slower, so height is a trade-off. Risk: Agility and height both touch turning, so the sensitivity test must separate them.
  • Build is a description such as slight, athletic, or powerful, generated from the attributes, with no effect in the engine; weight is not stored. Risk: Build is a field with no engine job, the kind your brief warns against, although it is shown as a word and derived from attributes that do have jobs.
  • Besides the age curves, age acts directly: older players recover more slowly between matches, carry more injury risk under congestion, and fade faster late in a match. Risk: Age overlaps stamina, natural fitness, and injury proneness, so the sensitivity test must separate them.
  • A player new to a country adapts over months: language, climate, and the league's style move the mind family of effective ability, beside the off-pitch effects of nationality. Risk: One more state to track for each player.
  • The game stores every pair at every club in the active leagues, and none at the light clubs. Risk: Memory and save size grow with the number of active leagues, on top of the full staff and agent records already chosen for them.
  • Each player keeps every event of recent matches, for replays and deep reports, and match summaries for older ones. Risk: Large saves, which adds to the load on the player's machine that session six already named as the sharpest risk.
  • Every rating in the game, for staff, managers, referees, and agents as well as players, uses the 1-to-20 scale stored in tenths. Risk: The referee's numbers are sized from published data in the engine's own units, so each must be converted to and from the 1-to-20 scale.
  • Every staff, manager, and agent rating has a named job, and a season-long sensitivity check runs before each release. Risk: Season-long batches are slow, on top of the match batches at every milestone.
  • The xG-bucket check judges the engine with an xG model fitted on public real shots, reading distance, angle, body part, shot type, and pressure, independent of the engine. Risk: The event contract and the test-only output must carry body part, shot type, and pressure for every shot.
  • A probability is drawn at each contact, the moment a player touches the ball or the ball meets a player: the tackle, the first touch, the keeper's handling, and the quality of a strike or a pass. Each draw comes from a chance or a spread built from the situation and the attributes. Between contacts, flight, deflections, bodies in the lane, and the keeper's position are physics. Risk: Two systems to tune, and a bad xG bucket can come from either one.
  • A strike takes two draws: first a quality (clean, scuff, slice), which pressure or a bad touch can force, then the error in aim, height, and power inside that quality, from finishing, technique, composure, pressure, body part, and the weaker foot. Risk: Two draws per shot to calibrate, and more work on each tick with a shot.
  • A duel runs in two steps: physics decides the approach, the angle and closing speed from pace, acceleration, and positions; a first draw decides whether the defender commits or holds off, from decisions and aggression; then the contest draw reads the attacker's dribbling, agility, acceleration, balance, and strength against the defender's tackling, positioning, anticipation, and aggression, with the angle, the closing speed, and the local numbers. Risk: Two draws per duel, and duels are the most common contest in a match.
  • Errors come from three sources: the far tails of every contact draw, whose spread widens with pressure, fatigue, low composure, and low consistency; rare concentration lapses that grow late in a match; and a small set of named error events (a short back pass, a keeper spilling a cross, a slip), each with its own chance, so commentary and reports can name them. Risk: Three sources to calibrate; each takes a fixed share of the band.
  • The realism test bands errors on Opta's definition, with a wide band: errors leading to goals at 0.25 to 0.45 a match (8 to 15 percent of goals), keepers about a quarter of them, and a team spread that depends on quality and composure. It adds a check on high turnovers: 11 to 17 a match, 15 to 17 percent ending in a shot. Risk: High turnovers mix pressing wins with errors, so a failure there may point at the pressing model rather than at errors.
  • The game promises to run well on an ordinary laptop, about 4 cores and 8 GB of memory. Risk: Tight limits on matchday time, save creation, save size, and memory, against a world of full people, every pair, and full recent events.
  • No realism lever is pulled to meet the budget: background matches keep the full engine, the active leagues stay as the player chooses, people and pairs stay full records, and every match keeps its events. Risk: On an ordinary laptop the budget is very likely to fail, since today's engine already takes about 20 seconds per league season on eight cores and this session multiplied the work per tick and the data per save.
  • When the budget fails, engineering gives way first: the team optimises (parallel matches, compact storage, cheaper maths) until the budget holds, and returns to you only if it still fails; no realism is cut before then. Risk: An open-ended amount of optimisation work.
  • football-data.co.uk and FotMob's Opta pass figures are used for measuring only, under the same accepted risk as StatsBomb and Transfermarkt: the game ships none of the data, and each band records its source so it can be measured again elsewhere. Risk: FotMob serves Opta data for display in its own app, not for reuse, so a complaint means measuring the pass bands again from open sets before release.
  • Costs and counters emerge from play, with no counter table: a high line is exposed because a slow defender loses the race to a ball in behind, and a press tires players because pressing costs energy; the round-robin check proves the counters. Risk: If a dominant tactic emerges, only a change to a mechanism can fix it, which is slower than editing a table, and a cost can come out too small to notice.
  • The round-robin plays about eight named styles (possession, low block, high press, counter-attack, direct, wing play, gegenpress, park the bus) with identical squads, every pairing home and away, on every change, and a grid over the settings themselves (line height, press, tempo, width, directness) before each release. Risk: The setting grid adds many pairings to each release run.
  • The gaps between the lines and the team's width emerge from where each role stands, moved by the block law; no instruction sets a target gap. Risk: An instruction may change the gaps too little to notice, which only the tactic sensitivity check will catch.
  • Two guards stop a human exploit of the AI: an AI manager notices during the match what is hurting it, for example repeated balls in behind, and answers with the counter the round-robin shows, within its fingerprint and adaptability; and before each release an automated exploit hunt plays many tactics against the AI and fails the build if any tactic wins far above its expected rate. Risk: The exploit hunt adds a large search to each release run, and in-match learning adds AI work during the match.
  • Conditions change which tactic works, and it emerges: a press in heat drains energy faster, a poor pitch worsens short passes and touches, and wind moves long balls; the round-robin runs under several conditions to prove it. Risk: The round-robin grows by the number of conditions it runs under.
  • The fast model's numbers are fitted from full-engine results after each engine change, so it follows the engine automatically, and the equality check runs with the harness. Risk: Each engine change needs enough full-engine matches to refit the fast model, which adds to the harness run.
  • Each ground has its own length and width within the laws, real grounds take their real size where known, and the size emerges in play, for example a narrow pitch helps a low block. Risk: The bands must hold across pitch sizes, and a ground far from 105 by 68 can move a club's statistics away from its league's band.
  • A match replays identically on every supported machine and operating system: the engine avoids maths that differs between machines, and a test replays a recorded match on each supported system. Risk: Some fast maths is off limits, which works against the laptop budget.
  • When the player skips to the result mid-match, the fast model finishes the match from the current state: the score, the clock, the cards, and the fatigue. Risk: The fast model must match the full engine from any match state, not only from kick-off, or a result will depend on whether the player watched.
  • A mid-match save made before a game update finishes the match on the engine version it started with, and the season then continues on the new version. Risk: Each release must ship the previous engine version beside the new one, for as long as unfinished matches from it may exist.
  • A skipped match may give slightly different odds than a watched one; the game accepts that gap, and the equality check measures it from sampled mid-match states and reports it. Risk: A result can depend a little on whether the player watched, which your brief ruled out.
  • The error sources stay separate and split the error band: the tails of the contact draws, concentration lapses, and the named error events each get a fixed share of the errors leading to goals, each is tuned to its share, and the report counts each source apart. Risk: Three shares to set and tune, and a named event must never also appear as a tail draw of the same kind.
  • What the board and the fans expect of a club's style blends club identity with the owner type, and each owner type sets its own weight, for example a state-backed owner weighs its own targets more. Risk: Two inputs to one expectation, with a weight per owner type to tune.
  • Every overlapping group of attributes has one split. Vision sets which passing options a player sees, decisions how well the player picks among them and when, and anticipation how early the player reads an opponent. Pace is top speed in long runs; acceleration wins short races. Jumping is height and timing, heading is what the player does on contact, and strength holds position in any contact. Aggression is how often and how hard a player commits, with the foul risk; tackling is success once committed. Concentration is rare sudden lapses that grow late in a match; consistency is the hidden everyday spread of each action, within and between matches. Technique shapes how every action is done and which skills a player tries; first touch is control on receipt. Agility is turning and gates hard body skills; balance is staying up and striking off balance. Composure acts on each action under pressure; the pressure trait acts on big occasions. Risk: Lapses are rare, so the sensitivity check needs larger batches to see concentration move.
  • The sensitivity test is a test family of its own beside the realism test. Over a large batch it changes one thing at a time and checks that the related statistics move in proportion and unrelated ones stay still: one attribute on one player, one tactical setting, one role against a neutral default, and one team number such as cohesion or a partnership, which must move only team actions. When and how often each part runs is still open. Risk: Two close roles can both pass while playing almost alike, and the batches are the largest part of the check load.
  • Every player carries every attribute. Keepers use the outfield attributes wherever they fit: the dive reads agility, the stance reads positioning, tackling joins rushing out, and keepers can dribble, score, and show flair. Every outfield player carries hidden goalkeeper attributes, generally poor, set at creation and never developed. Risk: An agile outfield player in goal dives like a keeper, limited only by the hidden poor keeper attributes.
  • Every league is banded, in three layers: each league with public data gets its own targets from five recent seasons, each tier gets a target set, and the other leagues fall into banding groups by tier and strength whose bands come from a formula fitted on the leagues with data. Shots count blocked shots, and pass completion and errors follow Opta's count. Risk: The formula extrapolates, so a league unlike any measured one can get a wrong band and send tuning the wrong way.
  • Every one of the 36 attributes keeps a named job in the engine; none is cut or merged. Consistency and injury resistance keep their jobs as hidden values, and injury resistance becomes injury proneness. Risk: Teamwork, work rate, and communication act on the team, so they need team-level mechanisms that do not exist yet.
  • The convex payoff lives in three places: on the pitch through the curve before the gap, in development where top points cost more to gain, and in the market where value and wages grow about 1.45 times per point of the level a valuer can see, never per point of a true attribute. Risk: Three curves to tune, and their effects compound.
  • Each engine change runs a quick set of about 1,000 matches automatically, which judges goals and most per-match rates; rare-event bands, scorelines, and the table wait for the heavier runs. Every report is stored with its version and compared with the last, and a band that moves by more than its tolerance is flagged even when it still passes. Risk: About 7 minutes of 8 cores per change at today's match cost, and the repository has no automated job to run it yet.
  • Each player and each kind of action draws from its own random stream, so a change to one draw does not reshuffle the rest of the match, and paired seeds work for every sensitivity check. Replay stays identical on every machine. Risk: The engine's random layer changes, and every recorded replay and baseline is regenerated once.
  • Each milestone runs only the parts of the round-robin and the sensitivity family that its changes could affect, with paired seeds and early stopping. Everything runs in full before each release. Risk: A change can reach a part the milestone skipped, and only the release run then catches it.
  • A full release run must finish in about 12 hours on the Mac mini and the VPS together. When it does not, the run shrinks through pairing, early stopping, and sampling before any machine is rented. Risk: If the realism work slows each match several times, overnight may need sampling that leaves some checks thinner before a release.
  • An engine emulator, separate from the fast model, is fitted on a spread-out design of a few hundred input points with about 200 full-engine matches each, and predicts the mean and spread of every banded statistic from attribute levels, tactical settings, and engine constants. It has three roles: a tuning tool that proposes settings the full engine then confirms, a sensitivity screen that ranks which inputs move which statistics so the engine confirms only the ranked results, and milestone triage that predicts which bands a change moves so the milestone runs those parts. Risk: It is reliable only inside its trained ranges, cannot find emergent effects such as a dominant tactic or an AI exploit, and is refitted after each engine change that moves the numbers, about 60,000 to 100,000 matches each time. A band always counts only on the full engine.
  • Each player keeps a picture of the pitch that scans refresh. Vision and anticipation set how often a player scans and how wide each scan reaches; a player out of view is remembered where the player was last seen, so a stale picture causes real mistakes such as a pass to a team-mate who has moved. Risk: Twenty-two pictures to keep and update on every tick, and replay must stay identical on every machine.
  • Pressure acts twice. Before the choice, a player needs a decision time set by decisions and first touch; after it, a set time to carry the action out, for example setting the body for a long pass. A presser who arrives inside either time hurries the player into a wider error, which composure narrows, or blocks the action. Risk: Two timers per player with the ball, and many more held or blocked actions to calibrate against the measured losses under pressure.
  • Off-ball movement has three layers: an anchor from the shape and the role; role patterns for the named runs, each with a trigger; and an off-ball utility that picks among the runs and the anchor and adds support angles and space seeking. Risk: The largest new piece of work in the area, and a new per-tick load for every player without the ball.
  • The team's marking system (zonal, man-to-man, or a mix) is a tactical setting that sets each defender's default, and a defensive utility picks each player's job within it on every tick: press, cover, track a runner, mark, or hold the line. Risk: A second utility for every player without the ball, and the system must still reproduce the measured handoffs instead of long man-marking.
  • A player's picture holds where each seen player stood and where that player was heading, so a passer can lead a runner seen during the run and a defender can read a run early. Team-mates also call: a runner calls for the ball and a team-mate warns 'man on', and a call reaches a player without a scan. Communication sets how often and how well a player calls, which gives that attribute a job in the match. Risk: Calls add a message layer between players, and a call that arrives late or is wrong must be modelled too.
  • The carrier's options grow beyond shoot, pass to feet, dribble, clear, and hold: passes into space (a lead pass and a through ball onto a run); several carry paths scored apart; crosses (driven, floated, cut-back) and switches of play; and body options (turn, shield, hold up the ball, draw a foul, a one-two, and skill moves gated by technique and agility). More options are wanted beyond these. Risk: Many more options to score for the carrier on every tick, each with its own contact draw to calibrate.
  • The option list is the whole catalogue of 53 actions, each family with its own measured outcome curve, plus the actions a manager game needs that no provider labels, such as time-wasting at the corner flag, a deliberate foul to stop a counter, and a quick free kick. Risk: The largest option set in the design, each action with a contact draw and a band, and the unlabelled extras are checked only by watching.
  • A player's scan rate comes from vision and anticipation, falling under close pressure and near the opponent's goal, and the player chooses when each scan happens: good players scan just before receiving, and a scan while the ball is arriving risks the first touch. Risk: A scan-timing choice for every player on every tick, and the scan rate has only one public source to band against.
  • Defenders share jobs by claims: each scores the jobs alone, and a player who claims a job makes team-mates lower their score for it, so two players rarely press the same attacker. Players also call to each other, such as 'I've got him' and 'push up', and communication and leadership decide how well calls land, so a quiet back line coordinates worse. Risk: Coordination emerges rather than being assigned, so the no-clustering bands and the watched review must catch a back line that fails to organise.
  • Two bands guard against passes through crowds beside the interception draw: pass completion by the number of opponents near the lane, and how often players choose a pass whose lane holds opponents, against the real choice model. Risk: The lane-crowding measure must be computed from tracking first, and the watched review does not name it as a separate item.
  • Four band families join the realism test: shape (width and depth by phase, the three line heights by the ball's distance from goal, the gaps between lines, and players goal-side of the ball); crowding (players within 5, 10, and 15 metres of the ball, even between the teams, and the share of frames with at most one player near the ball); behaviour (the run mix by kind, time on the ball and losses by pressure, defender handoffs, the goal-side share, and PPDA); and a new frozen-player band on how long an outfielder stays still or out of the play, measured from tracking. Risk: Most of these come from 7 DFL and 20 SkillCorner matches, and the frozen-player measure does not exist yet.
  • Players near the ball or in a duel think on every tick; the others think at a human rate set by decisions and anticipation, about every 0.1 to 0.3 seconds, and any new event (a pass, a touch, a call) wakes a player at once. Movement still updates on every tick. Risk: A player far from the ball reacts a little later than one near it, and the think rate must never change the result of a replay.
  • The keeper uses the same picture, utility, and claims as outfield players, with keeper options (come for a cross or stay, sweep or hold, throw, roll, short pass, long kick, punt) and the keeper's attributes. The measured depth rule is the keeper's anchor, organisation of the wall and the line is calls from communication and command of area, and the keeper's choices are checked against the keeper bands. Risk: Keeper organisation has no public data, so only watching checks it.
  • A positional instruction, such as stay wide or hold the line, is a limit on where the player may go, and a habit can break it only under pressure or low mood. A choice instruction, such as shoot less or play direct, moves the scores of the options it names, and the player's decisions and teamwork set how faithfully the player follows it. The rule that an instruction is carried out as given now covers the limits only. Risk: A player can drift from a choice instruction, which a manager may read as the game ignoring the instruction unless the match report names it.
  • The engine writes a log of true causes as the match runs, for example that a goal came from a three-against-two on the left after the full-back pushed up. The staff read that log through their own quality, and the same log scores the playtest by comparing a playtester's answer with the true causes. Risk: The engine must decide what counts as a cause, which is itself a model that can be wrong.
  • Explanations hold three layers: every statistic and picture is always true, so a player who doubts the assistant can check the evidence; the staff's reading of what happened can miss or misweigh causes as staff quality falls; and advice about what to do next can be wrong as all staff advice can. The playtest uses an average staff. Risk: A poor assistant can still mislead a player who does not check the evidence.
  • The game ships written commentary with crowd, whistle, and ball sounds that follow the events, such as the crowd rising on a chance; there is no spoken voice. Risk: A commercial audio set is needed, and the sounds must follow the event record as exactly as the text does.
  • Commentary comes from large banks of lines, each tagged with the context it fits and chosen with cooldowns. A language model writes and tags most of the banks before release and a person reviews them; a small grammar assembles some sentences from parts for extra variety. No model runs during play, and every fact in a line comes from the event record, never from the line. Risk: Reviewing many thousands of generated lines is a large editorial task.
  • A player's match rating is built in three steps: each action earns the change it makes to the chance of scoring and of conceding, tackles, blocks, and pressures that force a loss included; off-ball work earns credit from the true-cause log, such as a run that pulled a defender and opened space, a cover position that closed a lane, or a press that forced a long ball; and the total is compared with what the player's role and the match allowed, so a centre-back in a dominant team is not punished for having little to do. The result shows on the familiar 1-to-10 display. Risk: The ratings will differ from the public sites that players know, which reward attackers, so a player may read a fair rating as a strange one.
  • The game offers a wide set of visualisations: the brief's set (shot maps with xG, an xG timeline, pass networks, heat maps, average positions, and ratings); tactical ones (overload and zone-control maps by phase, chances by lane, pressing and regain maps, shape by phase with line heights); player ones (action maps, sprint and distance figures, trait use); comparisons against the opponent, the season, and the last match; a momentum or threat-flow line and possession chains; set-piece and keeper maps; energy curves and duel maps; and moment replays with overlays of pitch control, the options the carrier saw and missed, and the line heights. Visualisations update as the match runs, and a full catalogue of statistics, model metrics, and visualisations, with a home for each on the match screen, the data hub, the post-match report, and opposition analysis, is being researched. Risk: A very large surface to design and keep true, and pass networks wait for passer and receiver on every pass from the event-contract redesign.
  • The fast model produces per-player statistics and ratings drawn from the full engine's distributions for a player of that quality and role, and a check requires them to match those distributions, so scouting, form, and the market work in every league. Scouting and the market read more than five matches. Risk: The fast model's per-player output is a second thing to fit and check beside its results.
  • Every full-engine match keeps its decision internals (what each carrier saw, the options scored, and the true causes) while it is recent, so opposition analysis can replay an opponent's decisions as well as the manager's own. Risk: The largest addition to save size on the board, on top of every event of recent matches.
  • Substitutions and formation changes wait for a stoppage. Shouts and instructions, such as push higher, slow down, or target the left-back, are called from the touchline and start at once, and players take them up over time: players near the touchline and those with high decisions, teamwork, and tactic familiarity adopt a change first, and the captain's calls spread it. Risk: A change reaches players at different times, so the team can play two ideas at once for a while, which the effect-size check must allow for.
  • The AI manager shares three things with the human: the same rules (substitution limits and windows, gates, and shouts, with no action open to only one side); the same information (only what its staff can show it, through the same noisy readings, never the engine's true state); and the same reaction time (it notices a problem after its staff do and cannot change on the tick a threat appears). Risk: An AI limited to staff readings and human reaction plays worse, and the exploit hunt must still find no easy win against it.
  • Each competition carries its own rule pack from real data: its substitution limit and windows, whether it has concussion substitutes and an extra-time substitute, and which newer laws it applies, such as the keeper's 8-second rule. Each rule records the date it began, so the simulated past seasons use the rules of their time, for example three substitutes before 2020. Risk: A rule history per competition to research and keep, and the realism bands of past seasons must allow for the older rules.
  • The manager has four kinds of in-match tools: team instructions (line height, pressing, width, tempo, directness, and mentality); opposition targets (press a weak player, attack his side, show him onto his weaker foot, and close down or mark a dangerous one); player instructions (stay wide, get forward, hold position, take fewer risks, shoot on sight, man-mark a named opponent); and prepared game-state plans switched with one action, such as protect a lead or chase the game. Risk: Many more controls, each one a candidate for an exploit, which the exploit hunt must cover.
  • The in-match change check plays matches to a set state, for example 0-1 at minute 60, then forks each one with paired seeds: one copy makes the change and one does not. The differences in shots, xG, and goals for and against are banded against real game-state data, such as trailing teams taking about 3.4 points more of the shots and a formation change lifting attacking output over the next ten minutes. Risk: Forked matches need saved mid-match states and split random streams, and the real effect sizes come from few studies.
  • The manager builds routines three ways: a library of named routines for each restart kind with the taker and target players set; an editor that places each player's start spot, run, and timing and the delivery target, as on a board; and the set-piece coach's proposals, which suit the squad and exploit the next opponent using the analysts' read of how that opponent defends. Risk: A free editor is the easiest place to find an exploit, so the per-routine bands and the exploit hunt must cover edited routines too.
  • At a set piece, the delivery's arc and pace come from the taker's draw; each player's run, timing, height, and jumping set who can reach the ball; a heading duel draw decides between the players who can reach it; and the keeper chooses to come or stay through the same perception and utility as any player. Legal and illegal contact before the ball arrives, such as blocks, screens, and holding, also happens, and the referee may penalise it. Risk: Contact in the box is a new source of fouls and penalties to calibrate, and the referee must see it through the same model as any foul.
  • Each league has a set-piece goal share band from recent seasons, about 20 to 28 percent without penalties and a quarter to a third with them. The world's set-piece style may drift as AI managers copy what works, for example a long-throw boom spreading, while the season check holds the share inside a wider range. Risk: A drifting world can move a league away from its real band, so the season check must tell a drift from a fault.
  • In-match fatigue has three parts: a short-term reserve that sprints use and brief rest refills, which sets repeated-sprint ability; a slow reserve that drains through the match; and mental fatigue that raises concentration lapses and slows decisions late, which personality and the fitness staff move. Late tiredness is real but modest, and bands on running by 15-minute period keep the physical drop as small as real data shows. Risk: Three reserves per player to calibrate, and the real late drop is mostly dead time, so true fatigue must stay small.
  • Each exposure event (a tackle, a sprint, a collision, a landing, a change of direction) carries a small injury chance, scaled by fatigue, load, injury proneness, age, previous injury, the pitch, and the weather. Each kind of event leads to its own injury types, such as sprints to hamstring strains, tackles to ankle and knee injuries, and landings to knee injuries, each with its own recovery time and recurrence risk. Training injuries also happen, moved by the training plan's intensity and the medical and fitness staff. Risk: Many small hazards to fit so that the season totals and the type mix both match real data.
  • Congestion follows real data: it raises injury risk strongly and lowers output only a little, so the reason to rotate is protecting players, as in real football. Risk: Results barely suffer from congestion, so a manager who ignores rotation pays only through injuries, which the injury bands must make felt.
  • Illness such as colds, flu, and stomach bugs happens at real rates by season and can spread through a squad, with a few days out and reduced output on return. Risk: A spreading illness can strip a squad before a key match, which a player may find unfair unless the staff's warnings and prevention make it manageable.
  • A save keeps daily load and state for recent weeks, a longer window for players in the active leagues, and a compact injury record (type, site, date, days out) for every player's whole career. Risk: Per-player state for every player everywhere adds to save size beside every pair and full recent events.
  • Balance comes first from real mechanisms, plus a gentle hidden pull toward the middle inside AI club decisions, such as transfer and wage choices, that the multi-season check tunes to a pooled real band. Risk: A hidden pull can feel like rubber-banding if players discover it, so the pull must act only through AI choices a real club could make and never on match outcomes.
  • The multi-season check runs ten saves of fifty seasons each, so slow drift, such as generated-player attributes, reputation, and wages, shows as well as turnover; background leagues use the fast model. Risk: Ten saves give few title-turnover samples per league, and fifty full seasons of the active leagues may not fit the overnight release run on the Mac mini and the VPS, so the size needs a cost check.

Open questions

  • None.

Pieces of work

  1. Write the realism test harness design document (step 1 of 12)The test-only output written from one match record, bands with a real target and a design target, the conversion of StatsBomb bands to the DFL schema, and the watched review that replays a real match beside a simulated one. It goes first because every later mechanism is reviewed against it.Routed · needs update
  2. Write the team shape and the block design document (step 2 of 12)The block law (the team slides with the ball at a measured gain), the width in each phase, and the nine phases derived from the engine state. It is the first mechanism, and the first watched review judges it.Routed · needs update
  3. Write the player record reshape design document (step 3 of 12)Competence per position; age, height, and foot; new performance attributes; a personality group with twelve hidden traits; and the scouting views of attributes and personality. It carries the data half of the old player-model card and records the decision to change the record when the realism programme starts.Routed · needs update
  4. Write the action resolution and the choice model design document (step 4 of 12)One resolution model per action family, with the situation first and the player second; the duel, pass, shot, and keeper surfaces; the choice model for when to shoot and to whom to pass; and amplified attribute effects with graded consistency.Routed · needs update
  5. Write the mood, club style, and managers design document (step 5 of 12)Player mood and team mood mediated by personality; club style from identity, squad, and tactic; home ground and reputation; the AI manager's choices from four inputs; and manager attributes that act on information, learning, and trust.Routed · needs update
  6. Write the Tactics Tier 3 design document (step 6 of 12)Team and per-player transitions, per-player and opposition instructions, triggers, live changes at the next stoppage for both managers, set-piece routines, and the phase report after the match.Routed · needs update
  7. Write the conditions, officials, injuries, and the ball in the air design document (step 7 of 12)The full referee with a home leaning, pitch wear and weather that interact, knocks handled by severity and injuries softened by design, and the aerial model on the height the ball already carries.Routed · needs update
  8. Write the event contract redesign design document (step 8 of 12)The event contract rebuilt on the DFL schema with StatsBomb additions, one definition per event kind, written from the same match record as the test-only output. It runs between the other two programmes, because the contract changes when the season layer starts.Routed · needs update
  9. Write the competitions and the world design document (step 9 of 12)The league table, cups, calendar, congestion, and suspensions; the active leagues chosen at save creation; and the result-only model that runs every other league and the simulated past. It is the frame the other three season documents run inside.Routed · needs update
  10. Write the squad and careers design document (step 10 of 12)Team and player form, availability, a training plan, age curves per attribute moved by five factors, loans that vary widely around a zero average, and the career test.Routed · needs update
  11. Write the market design document (step 11 of 12)Valuation from visible data only, a fee as the valuation plus each club's premium, scouting noise and identity weights as the two sources of disagreement, and transfers.Routed · needs update
  12. Write the club business design document (step 12 of 12)The board and the owner type, five income sources with sale money routed by the owner, wages, financial rules with sanctions, sacking and the job market, and the finance test.Routed · needs update
